# **Iamblichus on the Mysteries.**

<note>original: "Jamblichus"</note>

<margin>The Answer of the Preceptor Abammon to the Epistle of Porphyry to Anebo, and a Solution to the Doubts contained in it.</margin>

->**THE EPISTLE OF PORPHYRY TO ANEBO**<-

<note>Porphyry was a student of Plotinus who wrote a letter to the Egyptian priest Anebo questioning the logic of religious rituals; this book is the response to those questions, written by Iamblichus under the pseudonym "Abammon."</note>

### **The Response of the Master Abammon to the Letter of Porphyry to Anebo, and the Solution to the Doubts Expressed Therein.**

<note>original: "The Answere of the Preceptor Abammon to the Epistle of Porphyry to Anebo, and a Solution to the Doubts contained in it."</note>

<summary>This title page introduces Iamblichus's defense of theurgy and divine mysteries, framed as a response from the Egyptian master Abammon to the skeptical inquiries of the philosopher Porphyry.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, Abammon, Anebo, Neoplatonism, Egyptian Mysteries, Theurgy, Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
# INTRODUCTION.

---

It appears to me that there are two descriptions of persons by whom the present work must be considered to be of inestimable worth: the lovers of antiquity and the lovers of ancient philosophy and religion. To the former of these it must be invaluable, because it is replete with information derived from the wise men of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient inhabitants of Mesopotamia known for their astronomy and astrology</note>, the prophets of the Egyptians, the dogmas of the Assyrians, and the ancient pillars of Hermes <note>Refers to the legendary "Emerald Tablet" or pillars upon which Hermes Trismegistus was said to have inscribed all knowledge</note>. To the latter, it is invaluable because of the doctrines contained in it. Some of these originated from the Hermaic pillars and were known by Pythagoras and Plato, serving as the sources of their philosophy. Others are profoundly theological and unfold the mysteries of ancient religion with an admirable conciseness of diction, and an inimitable vigor and elegance of conception. To

<summary>The author introduces the work as a vital resource for historians and philosophers, claiming it contains ancient wisdom from Chaldean, Egyptian, and Assyrian sources that influenced Plato and Pythagoras.</summary>
<keywords>Antiquity, Ancient Philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Chaldeans, Egyptians, Pythagoras, Plato, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory praise of the work, transitioning from the excellence of the text to a defense of ancient theology and its origins.</meta>

To this may also be added, as the finishing touch <note>original: "colophon of excellence"</note> of its quality, that it is the most comprehensive, the clearest, and the most satisfactory defense in existence of genuine ancient theology.

This theology—the sacred operations of which, called *theurgy* <term>theurgy: a ritual practice intended to invite the presence or action of divine beings</term>, are developed here—has for the most part been viewed only in its corrupted forms since its destruction. It has been seen among uncivilized nations or during the decline and fall of the Roman Empire, with which it gradually fell while overwhelmed with pollution. At length, it totally vanished from what is called the civilized part of the world. This will be evident to the intelligent reader from the following remarks. These remarks are a summary of what I have discussed more extensively elsewhere on this subject; they also demonstrate that the religion of the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and Greeks is no less scientific than it is sublime.

In the first place, this theology celebrates the immense principle of all things as something superior even to existence <note>original: "being"</note> itself. It is regarded as exempt from the whole of creation, of which it is nevertheless the inexpressible <note>original: "ineffably"</note> source; and

<summary>The author argues that ancient theology and theurgy have been unfairly judged by their later corruptions and asserts that the religions of the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and Greeks are scientifically rigorous and sublime.</summary>
<keywords>ancient theology, theurgy, Roman Empire, Chaldeans, Egyptians, Greeks, metaphysics, divine principle</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the supreme principle in ancient theology, explaining why it remains unclassified within the traditional divine hierarchies while introducing the concept of the triad and the monad.</meta>

Therefore, it does not think it appropriate to list it with any triad* or order of beings. Indeed, it even apologizes for giving the name of the simplest of our concepts to that which is beyond all knowledge and all conception. However, it calls this principle *the one* and *the good*; by the first of these names, it indicates its transcendent simplicity, and by the second

---

<note>* According to this theology, as I have shown elsewhere, in every order of things, a triad is the immediate offspring of a monad. Hence, the intelligible triad proceeds directly from the inexpressible principle of things. Phanes, or intelligible intellect, who is the last of the intelligible order, is the monad, leader, and producing cause of a triad which is called νοητὸς καὶ νοερός, that is, *intelligible* and at the same time *intellectual*. In the same way, the end of this order produces directly from itself the intellectual triad: Saturn, Rhea, and Jupiter. Again, Jupiter, who is also the Demiurgus, is the monad of the super-mundane triad. Apollo, who exists at the end of the super-mundane order, produces a triad of liberated Gods (Θεοὶ ἀπόλυτοι). And the end of the liberated order becomes the monad of a triad of mundane Gods. This theory, which is the product of the most complete science, is also in perfect agreement with Chaldean theology. And thus it is said in one of the Chaldean oracles, "*In every world a triad shines forth, of which a monad is the ruling principle*" (Παντὶ γὰρ ἐν κόσμῳ λάμπει τριὰς ἧς μονὰς ἄρχει). I refer the reader who wishes to be fully convinced of all this to my translation of Proclus's *Theology of Plato*.</note>

<summary>The author explains that the supreme principle (the One) is beyond all human conception and categorization, and details the Neoplatonic hierarchy where monads produce triads across various divine orders.</summary>
<keywords>the One, the Good, Neoplatonism, monad, triad, Phanes, Demiurgus, Chaldean theology, Proclus, divine hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the supreme principle, defining it as both "the one" and "the good." It explains that while these terms are inadequate for describing the ineffable, they represent the soul's natural movement toward the divine source, which acts as the ultimate principle of all principles.</meta>

its existence as the object of desire for all beings. For all things desire the good. At the same time, however, it asserts that these names are in reality nothing more than the parturitions of the soul, which—standing, as it were, in the vestibules of the adytum of the deity—announce nothing concerning the ineffable, but only indicate its spontaneous tendencies toward it; these names belong more to the immediate offspring of the first God than to the first itself. Hence, as the result of this most venerable conception of the supreme, when it ventures not only to name it—though it is ineffable—but also to assert something of its relation to other things, it considers this as its preeminent characteristic: that it is the *principle of principles*. It is necessary that the characteristic property of a principle, in the same way as other things, should not begin from a multitude, but should be gathered into one monad as a summit, which is the principle of all principles.

The scientific reasoning from which this doctrine is deduced is the following. Since the principle of all things is *the one*, it is

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction regarding "the one" as the principle of all things, arguing that the transition from the divine source to the physical world must be a continuous progression through similar orders of being.</meta>

necessary that the progression of beings should be continued, and that no vacuum should intervene in either incorporeal or corporeal natures. It is also necessary that everything which has a natural progression should proceed through similarity. As a result of this, it is likewise necessary that every producing principle should generate a number of the same order as itself: namely, *nature* generates a natural number; *soul*, one that is psychical (that is, belonging to the soul); and *intellect*, an intellectual number. For if whatever possesses a power of generating produces similar things before dissimilar ones, every cause must deliver its own form and characteristic peculiarity to its offspring; and before it generates that which gives subsistence to progressions far distant and separate from its nature, it must constitute things proximate to itself according to essence, and conjoined with it through similarity. It is, therefore, necessary from these premises—since there is one unity, the principle of the universe—that this unity should produce from itself, before everything else, a multitude of natures characterized

<summary>The author argues that existence must be a continuous progression without gaps, where every creative principle first produces a multitude similar to itself before generating more distant forms of life. This ensures that the "one unity" at the source of the universe produces an immediate order of similar natures before expanding into the rest of creation.</summary>
<keywords>progression of beings, vacuum, incorporeal, corporeal, similarity, producing principle, nature, soul, intellect, subsistence, essence, unity, principle of the universe</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction regarding "the one" as the principle of all things, explaining how the primary unity produces a multitude of divine natures (the Gods) which serve as the foundation for all subsequent levels of existence.</meta>

by unity, and a number the most allied of all things to its cause; and these natures are no other than the Gods.

According to this theology, therefore, from the immense principle of principles—in which all things causally exist, absorbed in superessential light and involved in unfathomable depths—a beautiful progeny of principles proceeds, all sharing largely in the ineffable, all stamped with the hidden characters of deity, and all possessing an overflowing fullness of good. From these dazzling summits, these ineffable blossoms, these divine propagations, *being, life, intellect, soul, nature, and body* depend; *monads* suspended from *unities*, deified natures proceeding from deities. Each of these monads, too, is the leader of a series which extends from itself to the last of things, and which, while it proceeds from its leader, at the same time remains in and returns to it. And all these principles, and all their progeny, are finally centered and rooted by their summits in the first great all-comprehending one. Thus all beings proceed from, and are comprehended in, the first

<summary>This page describes the descent of reality from a singular, "superessential" source through the Gods (the primary principles) down to the physical world. It explains that all levels of existence—including life, intellect, and body—are organized into series led by "monads" that ultimately remain rooted in the original "One."</summary>
<keywords>theology, superessential light, deity, monads, unities, being, life, intellect, soul, nature, body, the One, divine hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of how all levels of existence originate from a singular source, explaining that every level of reality—intellect, soul, nature, and body—is an unfolding of a primary version of itself, all ultimately contained within the "First One."</meta>

being: all intellects emanate from one first intellect; all souls from one first soul; all natures blossom from one first nature; and all bodies proceed from the vital and luminous body of the world. And, lastly, all these great monads are contained within the first one, from which both they and all their dependent series are unfolded into light. Hence this first one is truly the unity of unities, the monad of monads, the principle of principles, the God of Gods, one and all things, and yet the one prior to all.

No objections of any weight, no arguments except those that are sophistical, can be urged against this most sublime theory, which is so compatible with the unperverted conceptions of the human mind that it can only be treated with ridicule and contempt in degraded, barren, and barbarous ages. Ignorance and impious fraud, however, have until now conspired to defame those inestimable works * in which this and many other grand and important dogmas can

<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, which stated that ignorance and fraud have defamed the primary philosophical works where ancient dogmas can be found.</meta>

alone be found; and the theology of the ancients has been attacked with all the insane fury of ecclesiastical zeal, and all the feeble flashes of mistaken wit, by men whose conceptions on the subject, like those of a man between sleeping and waking, have been muddled and wild, fantastic and confused, preposterous and vain.

Indeed, it has always been, and still is, admitted by all nations and all religions that—after the great incomprehensible cause of all—a divine multitude exists, cooperating with this cause in the production and government of the universe. This is true however much they may differ in their opinions respecting the nature of the subordinate deities and the veneration which man is to pay to them, and however primitive the conceptions of some nations on this subject may be when compared with those of others. Hence, says the elegant *Maximus Tyrius*, "You will see one law and assertion in agreement throughout all the earth: that there is one God, the king and father of all things, and many Gods, sons of God, ruling together with him. This the Greek says, and"

The deification, however, of dead men, and the worshiping of men as Gods, formed no part of this theology when it is considered according to its genuine purity. Numerous instances of the truth of this might be cited, but I shall mention for this purpose, as indisputable witnesses, the writings of Plato, the *Golden Pythagorean Verses*<note>† “Diogenes Laertius says of Pythagoras that he instructed his disciples *not to give equal degrees of honor to the Gods and heroes.* Herodotus (in Euterpe) says of the Greeks, *that they worshiped Hercules in two ways: one as an immortal deity, and so they sacrificed to him; and another as a hero, and so they celebrated his memory.* Isocrates (Encomium of He-</note>, and the Treatise of Plutarch

<summary>The author argues that true ancient theology did not involve the worship of mortal men as gods, distinguishing between immortal deities and celebrated heroes. <meta>This page completes the reference to Plutarch’s *On Isis and Osiris* and provides extensive evidence from ancient inscriptions and authors to support the distinction between the worship of immortal gods and the honors granted to deceased heroes.</meta>

on *Isis and Osiris*. All the works of Plato, indeed, show the truth of this position,

<note>Helen) distinguishes between the honors of heroes and Gods when he speaks of Menelaus and Helen. But the distinction is nowhere more fully expressed than in the Greek inscription upon the statue of Regilla, wife of Herodes Atticus (as Salmasius thinks), which was set up in his temple at Triopium and copied from the statue itself by Sirmondus; where it is said *that she had neither the honor of a mortal nor yet that which was proper to the Gods.* Ουδε ιερα θνητοις, αταρ ουδε θεοισιν ομοια. It seems by the inscription of Herodes and by the testament of Epicteta, surviving in Greek in the *Collection of Inscriptions*, that it was in the power of particular families to keep festival days in honor of some of their own family and to give heroic honors to them. In that noble inscription at Venice, we find three days appointed every year to be observed, and an association established for that purpose with its own laws. The first day is to be observed in honor of the Muses, and sacrifices are to be offered to them as *deities*. The second and third days are in honor of the *heroes* of the family; they showed the difference between this honor and that of deities by the interval of time between them and the preference given to the latter. But whatever the *difference* was, the fact that a distinction was acknowledged among them appears by this passage from Valerius, in his excellent oration found in Dionysius of Halicarnassus, *Roman Antiquities*, book ii. p. 696.

> “*I call*,” says he, “*the Gods to witness, whose temples and altars our family has worshiped with common sacrifices; and next after them, I call the Genii of our ancestors, to whom we give* δευτερας τιμας, *the second honors next to the Gods (as Celsus calls those,* τας προσηκουσας τιμας, *the due honors that belong to the lower demons).*”</note>

From this we notice that the heathens did not confuse all degrees of divine worship, giving to the lowest object the same which they supposed to

He argues that the heathens recognized different levels of divine honors and did not treat mortals and deities as equals in their religious practices.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the distinction between divine and human honors by referencing the Pythagorean Laws and the *Golden Verses*, following the previous discussion of historical inscriptions and Platonic philosophy.</meta>

but this is particularly evident from his Laws. The *Golden Verses* command that the immortal Gods be honored first, as they are established by law; afterwards the illustrious Heroes, under which name the author of the verses also includes angels and demons, properly so called; and in the last place, the terrestrial demons, that is, such good men who excel in virtue above the rest of mankind. But to honor the Gods as they are established by law is, as Hierocles observes, to reverence them as they are arranged by their <term>demiurge</term> and father; and this is to honor them as beings not only superior to man, but also to demons and angels. Therefore, to honor men, however excellent they may be, as Gods, is not to honor the Gods according to the rank in which they are placed by their Creator; for it is confusing the divine with the human nature, and is thus acting directly contrary to the Pythagorean

divine worship, which had been preserved inviolable among them. The worship of the Gods had been maintained in temples, with altars, images, sacrifices, hymns, prostrations, and the like; but it is by no means fitting, he says, for us to confuse these things, either by raising men to the honors of the Gods, or lowering the Gods to the honors of men. For if Alexander would not permit any man to usurp his royal dignity by the votes of men, how much more justly may the Gods disdain for any man to take their honors for himself. And it appears from Plutarch<note>Life of Artaxerxes; Aelian, Various History, book 1, chapter 21.</note>, that the Greeks thought it a mean and base thing for any of them, when sent on an embassy to the kings of Persia, to prostrate themselves before them, because this was only permitted among them in divine adoration. Therefore, he says, when Pelopidas and Ismenias were sent to Artaxerxes, Pelopidas did nothing unworthy, but Ismenias let his ring fall to the ground, and stooping for it, was thought to be performing his adoration; which

was completely as good an expedient as the Jesuits advising that the crucifix be held in the mandarin's hands while they performed their worship in the heathen temples in China.

Conon\* *also refused to offer worship, viewing it as a disgrace to his city;* and Isocrates† *accuses the Persians for doing so, because in this they showed that they despised the Gods rather than men, by prostituting their honors to their princes.* Herodotus mentions Sperchies and Bulis, who could not by the greatest violence be brought to offer worship to Xerxes, because it *was against the law of their country to give divine honor to men* ‡. And Valerius Maximus § says, " *the Athenians put Timagoras to death for doing it;* so strong a conviction had possessed them that the manner of worship which they used for their Gods should be preserved sacred and inviolable." The philosopher Sallust also, in his *Treatise on the Gods and the World*, says, "It is not unreasonable to suppose that impiety is a species of punishment, and that those who have had a know-

xix

knowledge of the Gods, and yet despised them, will in another life be deprived of this knowledge. And it is necessary that the punishment for those who have honored their kings as Gods should consist of being expelled from the Gods*.

When the ineffable transcendence of the first God—which was considered the grand principle in the Heathen religion by the best theologians of all nations, and particularly by its most illustrious promulgators, Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato—was forgotten, this forgetfulness was undoubtedly the primary cause of dead men being deified by the Pagans. If they had properly directed their attention to this transcendence, they would have perceived it to be so immense as to surpass eternity, infinity, self-subsistence, and even essence itself; and they would have seen that these qualities in reality belong to those venerable natures which are, as it were, first unfolded into light from the unfathomable

<note>* And he who ascends to the principles must seek whether it is possible for there to be anything better than the assumed principle; and if something more excellent is found, to seek again concerning that, until we come to the highest conceptions, beyond which we have none more venerable; and we must not stop the ascent. This single extension of the soul toward God is the best and, as much as possible, unerring. And of the goods we conceive—the most venerable, most holy, and most primary, both in names and things—by ascribing these to him, we know for certain that we have ascribed nothing worthy. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the incomprehensible nature of the first principle, concluding the previous quote about the Egyptian view of God as a "thrice unknown darkness."</meta>

which is even beyond these? If the Heathens, therefore, had considered as they ought this transcendence of the supreme God, they would never have presumed to equate the human with the divine nature, and consequently would never have worshiped men as Gods. Their theology, however, is not to be blamed as the cause of this impiety, but rather their forgetfulness of the most sublime of its dogmas, and the confusion that necessarily accompanied this neglect.

But to return to the present work. To some who are familiar with the writings of Porphyry, who know how high he ranks among the best of the Platonists, and that he was called by them *the philosopher* on account of his excellence, it may seem strange that he should have been so unskilled in theological mysteries, and so ignorant of the characteristics of the beings superior to man, as he may appear to have been by his letter to Anebo. That he was not, however, actually so unskilled and

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Porphyry's apparent ignorance in his letter to Anebo was a rhetorical device used to elicit a more thorough explanation from his correspondent.</meta>

ignorant, is evident from his admirable Treatise on Abstinence from Animal Food, and his Αφορμαι προς τα νοητα, or Auxiliaries to Intelligibles. His apparent ignorance, therefore, must have been assumed for the purpose of obtaining a more perfect and extensive solution to the doubts proposed in his Epistle than he would otherwise have received. But at the same time that this is admitted, it must also be observed that he was inferior to Iamblichus in theological science, who so greatly excelled in knowledge of this kind that he was not surpassed by anyone and was equaled by few. Hence he was called by all succeeding Platonists *the divine*, in the same manner as Plato, "to whom," as the sharp-witted Emperor Julian remarks, "he was later in time only, but not in genius*."

The difficulties attending the translation of this work into English are necessarily great, not only from its sublimity and no-

### THE
# EPISTLE OF PORPHYRY
### TO THE
# EGYPTIAN ANEBO.

---

->*Porphyry to the Prophet Anebo, greeting.*<-

I begin my friendship with you by invoking the Gods and good demons, and through those philosophical inquiries which have an affinity with these powers. Much has been said about these matters by the Greek philosophers; however, for the most part, the principles of their belief are derived from conjecture.

In the first place, therefore, it is granted that there are Gods. But I inquire what the specific characteristics of each of the more excellent <term>genera</term> are, by which they are distinguished from each other; and whether we must say that the cause of the distinction between them arises from their energies, or their passive motions, or from things

<vocab>Porphyry, Anebo, Gods, demons, Greek philosophers, genera, energies</vocab>

<summary>Porphyry opens his letter to the Egyptian prophet Anebo by proposing a philosophical discussion on the nature of divine beings. He seeks to understand the specific characteristics that distinguish different classes of gods and demons, questioning if these differences arise from their actions or their inherent natures.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Anebo, Neoplatonism, theology, demons, divine genera, Greek philosophy, divine classification</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's inquiry into the nature and classification of divine beings, following the introduction of his letter to Anebo.</meta>

...that are subsequent, or from their different arrangement with respect to bodies; as, for instance, from the arrangement of the Gods with reference to ethereal bodies, but of demons to aerial, and of souls to terrestrial bodies?

I also ask why, since all the Gods dwell in the heavens, theurgists only invoke the terrestrial and subterranean Gods? Likewise, how are some of the Gods said to be aquatic and aerial? And how are different Gods allotted different places and the parts of bodies according to their specific boundaries, even though they possess an infinite, indivisible, and incomprehensible power? How can there be a union among them if they are separated by the divisible boundaries of parts, and by the difference of places and the bodies they inhabit?

How do theologians, or those who are wise in divine matters, represent the Gods as passive? For it is said that, for this reason, erect phalli are displayed and obscene language is used. But if they are impassive, the invocations of the Gods will be in vain—those which claim they can appease the anger of the divinities and bring about a reconciliation with them. Furthermore, what are called the "necessities" of the Gods will be pointless; for that which is impassive cannot be allured, nor compelled, nor forced by necessity. How, therefore, are many

<summary>Porphyry questions the logic of classifying gods by their physical locations and challenges the idea that divine beings can be affected by human rituals or emotions. He seeks to understand how gods can be both infinite and yet restricted to specific elements like air, water, or the earth.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, divine hierarchy, impassivity, ritual, ethereal, terrestrial gods, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, theology</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s critical examination of the nature of the Gods and demons, specifically questioning whether they possess passions or are purely intellectual and incorporeal beings.</meta>

things in sacred rituals performed for them as if they were passive? Likewise, invocations are made to the Gods as if they were passive; so that not only are demons passive, but the Gods are as well, in accordance with what Homer says:

> “And even the Gods themselves are flexible.*”

But if we claim, as some do, that the Gods are pure intellects, while demons, possessing souls, merely share in that intellect, then pure intellects will be even less capable of being enticed and will remain completely unmixed with the physical world of the senses. Supplications, however, are foreign to the purity of the intellect and therefore should not be made to it. Instead, the things offered in sacred rites are offered as if to beings that have senses and souls.

Are the Gods, then, distinguished from demons by the fact that the former are incorporeal (without bodies) while the latter are corporeal (possessing bodies)? Yet, if the Gods are purely incorporeal, how can the sun, the moon, and the visible celestial bodies be Gods?

Similarly, how are some Gods beneficent while others are harmful?

What is it that links the Gods in the heavens who possess bodies with those who are incorporeal?

->* Iliad, book 10, verse 493.<-

<summary>Porphyry questions how the Gods can be influenced by rituals if they are impassive intellects and asks for clarification on the distinction between incorporeal Gods and visible celestial bodies.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the nature and classification of divine beings, moving from their passivity to the specific distinctions between different classes of spirits.</meta>

What is it that distinguishes demons from the visible and invisible Gods, since the visible are connected with the invisible Gods?

In what way do a demon, hero, and soul differ from each other? Is it in essence, or in power, or in energy?

What is the sign of a <term>God</term>, or <term>angel</term>, or <term>archangel</term>, or <term>demon</term>, or a certain <term>archon</term>, or <term>soul</term> being present? For to speak boastfully and to display a phantom of a certain quality is common to both Gods and demons, and to all the superior kinds. Consequently, the kind of the Gods would in no respect be better than that of the demons.

Since ignorance of and deception regarding divine natures is impiety and impurity, but scientific knowledge of the Gods is holy and beneficial, ignorance of honorable and beautiful things will be darkness, while knowledge of them will be light. And the former, indeed, will fill men with all evils through a lack of learning and through audacity; but the latter will be the cause of every good for them. [I wish you, therefore, to reveal the truth to me regarding these specific matters*.]

[And, in the first place, I wish you to explain

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry, specifically focusing on the mechanics and varieties of divination and divine inspiration.</meta>

[...to me distinctly\*] what it is that is achieved in divination? For we frequently obtain knowledge of future events through dreams while we are asleep; at that time, we are not in a tumultuous ecstasy, for the body is then at rest; yet we do not understand what takes place then in the same manner as when we are awake.

But many, through enthusiasm and divine inspiration, predict future events and are then in such a wakeful state that they even act according to their senses, and yet they are not conscious of the state they are in—or at least, not as much as they were before.

Some of those who suffer a mental alienation also act enthusiastically upon hearing cymbals or drums, or a certain modulated sound—such as those who are Corybantically inspired, those who are possessed by Sabazius, and those who are inspired by the Mother of the Gods. But some act enthusiastically by drinking water, as the priest of Clarius in Colophon; others by being seated at the mouth of a cavern, as those who prophesy at Delphi; and others by inhaling the vapor from water, as the prophetesses in Branchidæ. Some also become enthusiastic by standing on

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the various methods and sources of divination, questioning the nature of divine involvement and the mechanisms of inspiration.</meta>

characters, such as those who are filled by the entry of spirits. Others, who are conscious of what they are doing in other respects, are divinely inspired in their imaginative faculty; some, indeed, use darkness as a collaborator, others use certain potions, and others use incantations and compositions. Some act, according to the imagination, through water; others on a wall, others in the open air, and others in the sun, or in some other of the celestial bodies. Some also establish the art of investigating the future through the viscera, through birds, and through the stars.

I also ask concerning the method of divination: what is it, and what is the quality by which it is distinguished? All diviners, indeed, assert that they obtain a foreknowledge of future events through Gods or daemons, and that it is not possible for anyone else to know the future except those who are the lords of the future. I doubt, therefore, whether divinity is so subservient to men that it is not opposed to some becoming diviners from meal <note>This refers to aleuromancy, mentioned in the vocabulary list below.</note>.

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the sources of divination, shifting the focus from external rituals to whether the phenomenon originates within the soul's own passions or through external material influences.</meta>

energies, as is the case with those powers that are drawn down through you <note>priests</note> by the necessities that accompany invocation.

Or does the soul assert and imagine these things, and are they, as some think, the passions of the soul, excited by small incentives?

Or is a certain mixed form of existence produced from our soul and divine inspiration derived from outside?

Hence it must be said that the soul generates the power which has an imaginative perception of the future through motions of this kind, or that the things which are drawn from matter constitute daemons through the powers inherent in them, and especially things drawn from the matter taken from animals.

For in sleep, when we are not occupied with anything, we sometimes obtain a knowledge of the future.

But that a passion of the soul is the cause of divination is indicated by this: that the senses are occupied, that fumigations are introduced, and that invocations are employed; and likewise, that not all men, but those who are more simple and young, are better suited for prediction.

Nature, likewise, art, and the sympathy of things in the universe—as if they were the parts of one animal—contain <term>premanifestations</term> of certain things with reference to each other. And bodies are so prepared that there is a <term>presignification</term> of some by others, which is clearly indicated by the works performed in predicting the future. For those who invoke the divinities for this purpose have stones and herbs about them, tie certain sacred bonds which they also untie, open places that are shut, and change the deliberate intentions of the recipients, so as to make them worthy even though they were previously depraved. Nor are the makers of effective images to be despised. --- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the nature of spirits involved in theurgic rites, questioning whether the effects are produced by genuine divinities or by a deceptive class of lower spirits.</meta>

...and effective, though no divinity or <term>daemon</term> is drawn down by these images.

But there are some who suppose that there is a certain obedient genus of <term>daemons</term>, which is naturally fraudulent, omniform, and various, and which assumes the appearance of Gods and daemons, and the souls of the deceased; and that through these, everything which appears to be either good or evil is effected; for they are not able to contribute anything to true goods, such as those of the soul, nor to have any knowledge of them, but they abuse, deride, and frequently impede those who are striving to be virtuous. They are likewise full of pride, and rejoice in vapors and sacrifices. 

<term>Jugglers</term> <note>Meaning charlatans or sorcerers who use trickery or low magic.</note> likewise fraudulently attack us in many ways, through the intensity of the expectations which they raise.

It very much indeed perplexes me to understand how superior beings, when invoked, are commanded by those who invoke them, as if they were their inferiors; and they think it necessary that he who worships them should be just, but when they are called upon to act unjustly, they do not refuse so to act. Though the Gods, likewise, do not hear him who invokes them if he is impure from sexual connections, yet, at the same time, they do not refuse to lead anyone to illegal sexual activity.

<summary>Porphyry questions whether theurgy is the work of deceptive spirits who mimic gods to trick humans, and he highlights the contradiction of gods who demand moral purity from worshippers while assisting in unjust or immoral acts.</summary>
<keywords>daemons, deception, theurgy, virtue, contradictions, sexual purity, jugglers, Porphyry, Anebo</keywords>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;[I am also doubtful regarding sacrifices, specifically what usefulness or power they possess in the universe and with the Gods, and for what reason they are performed—appropriately, indeed, to the powers who are honored by them, but also usefully to those by whom the gifts are offered*.]
Why do the interpreters of prophecies and oracles also think it necessary to abstain from animals, lest the Gods should be polluted by the vapors arising from them; and yet the Gods are especially attracted by the vapors of animals?
Why is it necessary that the inspector <note>who presides over <term>sacred rites</term></note> ought not to touch a dead body, though most sacred operations are performed through dead bodies? --- Page 39 ---
<meta>Continuing Porphyry’s skepticism from the previous page, this section addresses the absurdity of humans using empty threats against the gods and questions the symbolic imagery used in Egyptian theology.</meta>

secrets of Isis, or point out the <term>arcanum</term> in the <term>adytum</term>, or stop the <term>Baris</term>, or scatter the members of Osiris to Typhon <note>or that he will do something else of a similar kind \*</note>, does not he who says this—by thus threatening what he neither knows nor is able to accomplish—prove himself to be extremely stupid? And what wretchedness does it not produce in those who, like very silly children, are possessed by such vain fear and are terrified by such fictions? And yet Chæremon, who was a sacred scribe, writes these things as if they were spread by the Egyptians. It is also said that these and similar things are of a most compelling nature.

What also is the meaning of those mystic narrations which say that a certain divinity is revealed into light from mire, that he is seated above the lotus, that he sails in a ship, and that he changes his forms every hour according to the signs of the zodiac? For they say he presents himself to the view in this way, and they thus ignorantly adapt the particular impressions of their own imagination to the God himself. But if these things are asserted symbolically, as symbols of the powers of this divinity, I request an interpretation of these symbols. For

<summary>Porphyry criticizes the practice of threatening gods with impossible deeds and questions the literal or symbolic meaning of Egyptian divine imagery, such as gods emerging from mire or traveling in ships.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Osiris, Typhon, Chæremon, Egyptian mythology, religious symbols, zodiac, theurgy, divine imagery</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's inquiry into Egyptian theology, shifting from the symbolism of divine imagery to the use of meaningless names in invocations and the Egyptian conception of the First Cause.</meta>

It is evident that if these are similar to the experiences of the sun when it is eclipsed, they would be seen by all people who intently observe the God.

What also is the purpose of names that have no meaning? And why, among such names, are those that are foreign preferred over our own? For if the listener looks for their meaning, it is enough that the concept remains the same, regardless of which words are used. For the one who is invoked is not of the Egyptian race; and even if he were Egyptian, he would not use the Egyptian language, or, in short, any human language. For either all these are the artificial inventions of sorcerers, and masks arising from our own emotions, which rumor attributes to a divine nature; or we ignorantly form concepts of divinity that are contrary to its actual state of being.

I also wish for you to explain to me what the Egyptians believe the first cause to be: whether it is intellect, or something above intellect? Whether it exists alone, or along with something else? Whether it is incorporeal or corporeal? And whether it is the same as the <term>Demiurgus</term>, or existed before the <term>Demiurgus</term>? Similarly, whether all things come from one principle or from many principles? Whether the Egyptians have a knowledge of matter or of primary corporeal qualities? And whether they allow matter to be

<summary>Porphyry questions the use of meaningless foreign names in religious invocations and asks for a clarification of the Egyptian understanding of the "First Cause" and its relationship to the Demiurge and matter.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian theology, divine names, First Cause, intellect, Demiurgus, incorporeal, corporeal, matter, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the Egyptian conception of the first principle, specifically examining the views of Chæremon regarding the physical nature of the gods and their relationship to the stars.</meta>

unbegotten, or to be generated? For Chæremon and others certainly do not believe there is anything prior to the visible worlds. At the beginning of their writings on this subject, they admit the existence of the Gods of the Egyptians, but no others except those called the planets, the Gods who complete the zodiac, and those that rise along with them; as well as the divisions into decans and the horoscopes. They also admit the existence of those called the powerful leaders, whose names are found in the calendars, along with their serving duties, their risings and settings, and their signs of future events. For Chæremon saw that the claims of those who say the sun is the <term>Demiurgus</term>, as well as what is asserted concerning Osiris and Isis and all the sacred myths, may be explained by the stars and their phases, their disappearances and appearances, or by the waxing or waning of the moon, or by the course of the sun, or the nocturnal and diurnal hemispheres, or the river <note>the Nile</note>. And, in short, the Egyptians explain everything through physical causes and nothing through incorporeal and living essences. Most of them likewise make our free will dependent upon the motion of the stars, and bind everything—though I do not know how—with indissoluble bonds

<summary>Porphyry discusses Chæremon’s view that Egyptian theology is entirely rooted in physical phenomena, such as astrology and the movements of the stars, rather than incorporeal realities. This perspective reduces the Egyptian gods and myths to astronomical events and suggests that human destiny is strictly bound by celestial motions.</summary>

<keywords>Chæremon, Egypt, Gods, planets, zodiac, decans, horoscopes, Osiris, Isis, Demiurgus, Nile, incorporeal, astrology, determinism</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s investigation into the Egyptian belief that all things are bound by the necessity of fate and the movement of the stars, questioning how the gods and personal spirits relate to this determinism.</meta>

of necessity, which they call fate. They also connect fate with the Gods; whom, nevertheless, they worship in temples and with statues, and other things, as the only ones who can dissolve fate.

Concerning the personal daemon, it must be inquired how he is assigned by the lord of the geniture, and according to what kind of efflux, or life, or power, he descends from him to us? And also, whether he exists, or does not exist? And whether the discovery of the lord of the geniture is impossible, or possible? For if it is possible, he is happy who, having learned the scheme of his nativity and knowing his proper daemon, becomes liberated from fate.

The canons, also, of <term>genethliology</term> [or prediction from the natal day] are innumerable and incomprehensible. And the knowledge of this mathematical science cannot be obtained; for there is much disagreement concerning it, and Chaeremon and many others have written against it. But the discovery of the lord, or lords, of the geniture—if there are more than one in a nativity—is almost admitted by astrologers themselves to be unattainable, and yet they say that on this the knowledge of the proper daemon depends.

Furthermore, I wish to know whether the personal daemon rules over some specific part of us? For it appears to certain persons,

<summary>Porphyry questions the possibility of identifying one's personal daemon through astrology to escape fate, noting that even astrologers admit the difficulty of identifying the "lord of the geniture" upon which this knowledge depends.</summary>

<keywords>fate, Gods, daemon, lord of the geniture, efflux, genethliology, nativity, Chaeremon, astrologers</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s inquiry into the nature of the personal daemon, moving from the difficulty of identifying it through astrology to exploring its specific functions and its relationship to the soul and the Gods.</meta>

that daemons preside over the parts of our body, so that one is the guardian of health, another of the form of the body, and another of physical habits, and that there is one daemon who presides in common over all these. And again, that one daemon presides over the body, another over the soul, and another over the intellect; and that some of them are good, but others bad.

I am also doubtful whether this daemon is not a certain part of the soul <note>such, for instance, as the intellectual part</note>; and if so, he will be happy who possesses a wise intellect.

I see likewise that there is a twofold worship of the personal daemon; the one being the worship as of two, but the other as of three. By all men, however, the daemon is called upon by a common invocation.

I further ask whether there is a certain other hidden way to happiness, separate from the Gods? And I am doubtful whether it is necessary to look to human opinions in divine <term>divination</term> and <term>theurgy</term>? And whether the soul does not devise great things from casual circumstances? Moreover, there are certain other methods which deal with the prediction of future events. And, perhaps, those who possess divine divination indeed foresee what will happen, yet are not on this account

<summary>Porphyry questions whether the personal daemon is a specific spiritual entity governing body and soul or simply the intellectual part of the human soul. He also asks if there are hidden paths to happiness or divination that operate independently of the Gods or human opinion.</summary>

<keywords>daemons, soul, intellect, personal daemon, worship, happiness, Gods, divination, theurgy, future events</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's inquiry into the limitations of divination and the search for the true source of human happiness, questioning whether those who communicate with divine beings are actually providing useful guidance for the soul.</meta>

happy; for they foresee future events, but do not know how to use this knowledge properly.

I wish, therefore, that you would point out to me the path to happiness, and show me in what the essence of it consists. For among us <note>Greeks</note> there is much verbal contention about it, because we form a conjecture of good from human reasoning. But by those who have devised the means of associating with beings more excellent than man, if the investigation of this subject is omitted, wisdom will be professed by them in vain; as they will only disturb a divine intellect about the discovery of a fugitive slave, or the purchase of land, or, if it should so happen, about marriage, or merchandise. And if they do not omit this subject, but assert what is most true about other things, yet say nothing that is stable and worthy of belief about happiness, in consequence of employing themselves about things that are difficult, but useless to mankind; in this case, they will not be conversant either with Gods or good daemons, but with that daemon who is called fraudulent; or, if this is not admitted, the whole will be the invention of men, and the fiction of a mortal nature.

<summary>Porphyry asks for the true path to happiness, arguing that if spiritual seekers only use divine communication for trivial worldly matters, they are either wasting their wisdom or dealing with deceptive spirits.</summary>
<keywords>happiness, Greeks, divine intellect, daemons, fraudulent spirits, human reasoning, divination, Porphyry</keywords>

## SECTION I.

### CHAPTER I.

HERMES, the God who presides over language, was formerly very rightly considered common to all priests; and the power who presides over the true science concerning the Gods

is one and the same throughout the whole of things. Therefore, our ancestors dedicated the discoveries of their wisdom to this deity, inscribing all their own writings with the name of Hermes. I also, quite rightly understanding that the letter sent to my disciple Anebo was intended for me, shall give you a true answer to your inquiries. the ancient Greeks should have obtained appropriate instruction from the sacred scribes of their time, but that you, who are our contemporary and think in harmony with those ancients, should be frustrated in your wish by those who are now living and who are called common teachers. I, therefore, now apply myself to the present discussion; and please, consider that the same person to whom you sent the letter returns you an answer. Or, if it should seem right to you, accept that it is I who speaks with you in writing, or some other Egyptian prophet, for this is of no consequence. Or, what I think is even better, disregard the consideration of whether the speaker is a lower or a higher character, but direct your attention to what is said, so as to readily encourage your mind to examine whether what is asserted is true or false.

In the first place, therefore, we shall categorize the types of the proposed problems so that we may know their quantity and quality. And, in the next place, we shall show from what theological systems the doubts are taken, and according to what sciences they are investigated. For some things that are badly confused require a certain distinction; others are concerned with the cause through which

Since such is the nature of the subjects under discussion, they are drawn from many sources and from different sciences. For some topics introduce observations from what the wise men of the <term>Chaldeans</term> have delivered; others raise objections from what the prophets of the <term>Egyptians</term> teach; and there are some that, adhering to the theory of philosophers, make inquiries in accordance with them. There are now likewise some that, from other opinions which do not deserve to be mentioned, elicit a certain doubt; and others originate from the common concepts of mankind. These matters, therefore, are themselves variously arranged and are connected with each other in many forms. Therefore, because of all these reasons, a specific discussion is required for handling them in an appropriate manner.

## CHAP. II.

WE shall, therefore, deliver to you the specific dogmas of the Assyrians and also clearly explain our own opinions to you. We will collect some things from the countless writings of the ancients, while drawing others from those details which were gathered by the ancients into a single treatise and pertain to the complete knowledge of divine natures. If you should also propose any philosophical inquiry, we shall discuss it for you according to the ancient pillars of Hermes, which Plato and Pythagoras knew previously, and from which they formed their philosophy. But for such things as involve foreign inquiries, or which are contradictory and argumentative, we shall address them mildly and appropriately, or we shall demonstrate their absurdity. Likewise, for those which proceed in accordance* with common conceptions, we shall endeavor to discuss them in a way that is perfectly familiar and clear. And indeed, those things which require the experience of divine operations for an accurate knowledge of them, we shall explain as far as this is possible to be achieved by words alone; but those that are

---

* <note>In the original κατα τας κοινας εννοιας (kata tas koinas ennoias), which Gale erroneously translates as *against common opinions*.</note>

<summary>The author outlines his intent to explain Assyrian dogmas and personal opinions using ancient writings and the "pillars of Hermes." He commits to addressing philosophical doubts appropriately while explaining divine operations through words as much as possible.</summary>

<keywords>Assyrians, dogmas, Hermes, Plato, Pythagoras, common conceptions, divine operations, theurgy, Thomas Gale</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from Chapter II, specifying how different types of inquiries—theological, theurgic, and philosophical—will be addressed according to their respective principles.</meta>

full of intellectual theory, we shall develop with a view to the purification of the soul. But indications of this theory worthy of notice may be mentioned, by which it is possible for you, and those who resemble you, to be led by the intellect to the essence of real beings. And with respect to such things as become known by a reasoning process, we shall leave none of these without a perfect demonstration. But in all things we shall give to each that which is appropriate. And such questions, indeed, as are theological, we shall answer theologically; such as are theurgic, theurgically; but such as are philosophical, we shall, in conjunction with you, philosophically explore. Of these, also, such as extend to first causes, we shall bring to light by following them according to first principles. But such as pertain to morals, or to ends, we shall fitly discuss according to the ethical mode. And, in a similar manner, we shall examine other things methodically and appropriately. Let us, therefore, now turn to your inquiries.

<summary>The author explains that different subjects—theological, theurgic, and philosophical—will be addressed using their own specific methods to lead the reader toward soul purification and an understanding of reality. He concludes the introduction by preparing to answer the recipient's specific questions.</summary>

## CHAP. III.

<margin>Damascius, in *On Principles*, says, “where difference does not exist, there will be no knowledge.” And, “the contact of one with the one is above knowledge.” Likewise, “the intellectual perception of the first intelligible is without any difference or distinction.” <note>Greek: ετεροτητος μη ουσης, μηδε γνωσις εσαι. Et συναφη ως ενος προς εν, υπερ γνωσιν. Alibi, αδιακριτος η του πρωτου νοητου νοησις.</note></margin>

In the first place, therefore, you say, “*it must be granted that there are Gods.*” To speak this way, however, is not correct regarding this subject. For an innate knowledge of the Gods coexists with our very essence; and this knowledge is superior to all judgment and deliberate choice, and exists prior to reason and demonstration. It is also united from the beginning with its proper cause, and it exists together with the soul's essential tendency toward the good. If, indeed, it is necessary to speak the truth, contact with divinity is not knowledge. For knowledge is, in a certain respect, separated <note>from its object</note> by *otherness*. But prior to the knowledge which knows one thing as another, there is a uniform connection with divinity; this connection is dependent on the Gods and is spontaneous and inseparable from them. Hence, it is not proper to grant this, as

<summary>The author argues that knowledge of the Gods is not a premise to be proven, but an innate part of the human essence that precedes reason and logic. He distinguishes between ordinary knowledge, which requires a separation between the knower and the known, and a higher, inseparable connection with divinity.</summary>
<keywords>innate knowledge, divinity, Damascius, otherness, soul, essence, Neoplatonism, intelligible, first principles</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the existence of the Gods is not a matter of intellectual debate or "granting" a premise, but an innate reality. It then extends this discussion to the hierarchy of superior beings, including daemons and heroes.</meta>

...as if it might not be granted, nor should we admit it as something ambiguous (for it is always uniformly established in its activity). Nor are we worthy to explore it in this way, as if we had sufficient authority to approve or reject it. For we are contained within it—or rather, we are filled by it—and we possess the very thing that we are <note>or that by which our essence is characterized</note> in knowing the Gods.

I shall say the same thing to you concerning the superior classes that follow the Gods—I mean daemons, heroes, and undefiled souls. For it is necessary to understand regarding these that there is always in them one definite principle of essence, and to remove from them the indefiniteness and instability of the human condition. It is also necessary to separate from them that in-

---

<note>Between souls that always remain on high in purity, such as the souls of *essential heroes*, and those that descend into the regions of mortality and are defiled with vice (such as the souls of the majority of mankind), the class of *undefiled souls* exists. These descend into the realms of generation partly from that necessity by which all human souls are, at times, drawn down to the earth, and partly for the benevolent purpose of benefiting those of an inferior class. But they descend without being defiled by vice. They are also called heroes <term>κατα σχεσιν</term> <note>kata schesin</note>, that is, according to *disposition*, in order to distinguish them from *essential* heroes. And in the *Pythagorean Golden Verses*, they are named the *terrestrial heroes*.</note>

<summary>The author explains that humans are contained and filled by the divine, making the knowledge of the Gods an essential part of our being rather than a subject for external judgment. He further introduces a hierarchy of superior beings—daemons, heroes, and undefiled souls—noting that they possess a stable essence distinct from human instability.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the innate knowledge of the Gods, completing the word "inclination" from the previous page's discussion on avoiding human instability in divine matters.</meta>

inclination to one side of an argument rather than another, arising from the balance of a reasoning process. For a thing of this kind is foreign to the principles of reason and life, and instead tends toward secondary natures and to such things as pertain to the power and conflict of generation. But it is necessary that the more excellent classes of being should be understood uniformly.

The innate perception, therefore, of the perpetual presence of the Gods, will be made like them. Hence, as they have an existence which is always invariably the same, so also the human soul is joined to them by knowledge, according to a sameness of being; by no means pursuing an essence which is above all these through conjecture, or opinion, or a logical process—all of which originate in time—but becoming united to them through the pure and faultless acts of intellect which the soul received from the Gods from eternity. You, however, seem to think that there is the same kind of knowledge of divine natures as of anything else, and that one thing, rather than another, may be granted from opposites, in the same manner as is common in logical discussions. There is, however, no similarity whatever between the two kinds of knowledge. For the knowledge of divine natures is different from that of other

things, and is separated from all opposition. Likewise, it neither exists by being granted now, nor by coming into being, but was from eternity, uniformly co-existent with the soul. And this is all I have to say to you concerning the first principle within us, from which it is necessary for those to begin who speak or hear anything about the natures that are superior to us.

## CHAP. IV.

With respect to your inquiry, “ *what are the peculiarities in each of the more excellent <term>genera</term>, by which they are separated from each other?* ” if you understand by peculiarities the specific differences under the same genus, which are distinguished by opposite qualities—as the rational and irrational are under the genus of animal—we by no means admit peculiarities of this kind in things which have neither one common essence, nor an equal distinction, nor receive a composition from something common, which is indefinite and defines the peculiarity. But if you understand the peculiarity to be—as in primary and secondary natures, differing in their whole essence and whole genus—a certain simple condition of being, definite in itself; in this case,

<note>Between souls that always remain on high in purity, such as the souls of *essential heroes*, and those that descend into the regions of mortality and are defiled with vice (such as the souls of the majority of humanity), the class of *undefiled souls* exists. These descend into the realms of generation partly from that necessity by which all human souls are, at times, drawn down to the earth, and partly for the benevolent purpose of benefiting those of an inferior class. Bu...</note>

<summary>The text concludes that the soul's knowledge of the divine is an eternal, innate principle rather than a logical conclusion, and begins to explore the essential differences that distinguish various classes of superior beings.</summary>
<keywords>innate knowledge, divine natures, peculiarities, genera, essence, co-existent, essential heroes, undefiled souls</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the inquirer's method regarding the distinct characteristics (peculiarities) of superior beings, following the discussion of the soul's innate knowledge of the divine.</meta>

your conception of peculiarities will be reasonable. For these peculiarities of things, which have an eternal existence, are simple and entirely exempt. The inquiry, however, proceeds imperfectly. For it was necessary, in the first place, to inquire what the peculiarities of the superior <term>genera</term> are according to essence; next, what they are according to power; and then, what they are according to energy. But, as your question currently stands, regarding the peculiarities by which these <term>genera</term> are separated, you speak only of the peculiarities of energies. Hence you inquire about the differences in the final aspects pertaining to them; but you leave uninvestigated those things that are primary and most honorable in them, which are the elements of their difference. In the same place, something is also added concerning "*efficacious and passive motions*," which is a division that is by no means suited to the differences between the superior <term>genera</term>. For the opposition of action and passion is not inherent in any of them; rather, their energies are unrestrained, immutable, and without relation to their opposites. Therefore, we must not admit in them motions of the kind that arise from action and passion. For we do not even admit a self-motion in the soul which consists

<summary>The author critiques the inquirer for focusing only on the final "energies" or actions of superior beings rather than their primary essence and power. It is argued that these higher entities are beyond the worldly dualism of action and passion.</summary>

of the mover and that which is moved; but we conceive that it is a certain simple, essential motion, existing from itself*, and not possessing a relationship to another thing, and exempt from acting on, or being acted upon by, itself. Who, therefore, can tolerate that the characteristics of the <term>genera</term> superior to the soul should be distinguished according to active or passive motions?

What you added—"*or of accidents*"—is also foreign to these <term>genera</term>. For in composite things, and things which exist together with or within others, or are contained by others, some things are conceived as preceding, and others as following; some as essences, and others as later additions to those essences. For there is a certain arrangement among them, and disharmony and distance come between them. But, in the more excellent <term>genera</term>, all things must be conceived *in τῳ ειναι*, that is, *in merely existing*; and wholes have a preceding existence, are separate by themselves, and do not derive their <term>hypostasis</term> from or within others; so that there is nothing in them which is accidental. Consequently, their characteristics are not defined by accidents.

Likewise, at the end of your inquiry, you

For your question asks, “*How are <term>essences</term> known by <term>energies</term>, by <term>physical motions</term>, and by <term>accidents</term>?*” However, the very contrary to all this takes place. For if <term>energies</term> and motions were constitutive of <term>essences</term>, they would be the masters of the difference which exists between them. But if <term>essences</term> generate <term>energies</term>—the former being separate and prior to the latter—they will impart to motions, <term>energies</term>, and <term>accidents</term> that by which they differ from each other. This, therefore, exists in a way contrary to what you suppose for the purpose of discovering the unique characteristic which you now investigate.

In short, whether you think that there is one <term>genus</term> of the <term>Gods</term>, one of <term>demons</term>, and similarly of <term>heroes</term>, and <term>souls</term> that are essentially <term>incorporeal</term>; or whether you admit that these are individually many, you inquire what the difference between them is according to their unique characteristics. For if you believe that each of these is one <note>and the same genus</note>, the whole arrangement of <term>scientific theology</term> is thrown into confusion. But if, as truth requires, you admit that they are distinguished by <term>genus</term>, and that there is not one common essential definition among them—but rather that those of them which are prior are exempt from those that are inferior—it is not possible to discover their common boundaries. And even if

### CHAP. VII.

Of the extremes, therefore, one is supreme, transcendent, and perfect, while the other is last in rank, deficient, and more imperfect. The former is capable of accomplishing all things at once, uniformly and in an instant; but the latter is neither able to effect all things, nor act at once, nor suddenly, nor indivisibly. The former also creates and governs all things without being drawn toward them; but the latter is naturally disposed to incline toward and be turned toward the things which it creates and governs. And the former, as the primordial source and cause, precedes all things in power; but the latter, being dependent on the will of the Gods as its cause, has existed alongside it from eternity. The former likewise, according to one vigorous peak, encompasses the ends of all activities and essences; but the latter passes from some things to others,

<vocab>intermediates, extremes, transcendent, primordial, consubsistent, peak, activities, essences, indivisibly</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the contrast between the highest divine beings and the lowest spiritual entities, describing the former as perfect, independent causes that act instantly, and the latter as dependent, imperfect beings that act sequentially.</summary>

Furthermore, the highest and the incomprehensible pertain to the former, as does that which is superior to all measure and is formless in such a way as not to be restricted by any form. But the latter is overcome by inclination, habit, and propensity; it is held back by desires directed toward what is less excellent and by familiarity with secondary natures. Thus, finally, it is shaped by the various measures derived from them.

Intellect, therefore—which is the leader and king of all beings and the demiurgic art of the universe—is always present with the Gods with unchanging sameness, perfectly and without lack, being purely established in itself according to a single energy. But the soul participates in a divisible and multiform intellect, directing its attention to the governance of the whole. It also providentially cares for inanimate natures, being born into different forms at different times.

From the same causes, therefore, order and beauty itself are inherently united with the more excellent genera; or, if one would prefer to put it this way, the cause of these is inherently united with them. But with the soul, the participation in intellectual order and divine beauty is always present. And with the former, indeed, the

But the soul has certain distinct boundaries, marking the limits of its dominion. 

Since these are the different characteristics of the extremes, it will not be difficult to understand what we have just said, and to perceive the intermediate characteristics of daemons and heroes. These are allied to each of the extremes, possessing a similarity to each while moving away from both toward the middle. They embrace a harmonious communion mingled from both extremes and are connected to it in appropriate measures. Therefore, these must be understood as the characteristics of the first divine genera.

### CHAP. VIII.

**BUT** neither must we admit that cause for the distinction of these genera which you add—namely, "*that it is an arrangement with reference to different bodies; as, for instance,*"

<summary>The text establishes that while the divine has sovereignty over all, the soul has limited dominion; it also defines daemons and heroes as intermediate beings that bridge these two extremes. --- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the refutation of the idea that divine genera are distinguished solely by their relationship to different types of bodies.</meta>

of gods to ethereal bodies, but of daemons to aerial bodies, and of souls to such as are terrestrial.” For such an arrangement as this, which resembles that of Socrates to a tribe when he is a senator, is unworthy of the divine genera, because all of them are essentially unrestrained and free. To this it may be added that it is dreadfully absurd to ascribe to bodies a principal power of giving a specific distinction to the first causes of themselves. For bodies are in servile subjection to these causes and are subservient to generation. And further still, the genera of the more excellent natures are not in bodies, but the former externally rule over the latter. Hence, they are not changed in conjunction with bodies. Again, they impart from themselves to bodies every such good as the bodies are able to receive, but they themselves receive nothing from bodies; so that they will not derive from them certain peculiarities. For if they were like the habits of bodies, or like material forms, or were in some other way corporeal-formed, it would perhaps be possible for them to be changed along with the differences of bodies. But if they are separate from bodies and essentially pre-exist unmingled with them, what reasonable distinction produced from bodies can be transferred to them? To which also may be

added, that this assertion of yours makes bodies more excellent than the <term>divine genera</term>, since the former provide a seat for superior causes and essentially instill their own characteristics into them. He, therefore, who arranges the <term>allotments</term>, distributions, and <term>consociations</term> of governors with the governed, will evidently assign the primary authority to the more excellent natures. For, because the <term>presiding powers</term> are such <note>as we have shown them to be</note>, for this reason they possess such an allotment and give it an essential specific distinction; they are not, however, made to resemble the nature of their receptacles.

It is necessary, therefore, to admit a principle of this kind regarding <term>partial souls</term>. For just as the life which the soul received, prior to its insertion into a human body, and just as the form which it readily expressed; so also is the <term>organical body</term> which it has suspended from itself, and such is the resulting corresponding nature, which receives the more perfect life of the soul. But with respect to more excellent natures—which, as wholes, contain the principle <note>of parts</note> within themselves—inferior things are produced within superior natures: bodies within <term>incorporeal essences</term>, and things fabricated within their <term>fabricators</term>; and, being circularly contained within them, they are directed and governed by them. Intellect, also—both that which is particular and that which is universal—is in a similar manner contained within the classes of being that are more excellent than intellect. Since, therefore, secondary natures are always directed toward primary ones, and superior beings are the leaders of inferior essences, acting as their models, essence and form pass from superior to subordinate natures, and things that come after are primarily produced within those that are more excellent; so that order and measure are derived from primary to secondary beings, and the latter derive their very nature from the former. But the opposite must not be accepted—namely, that specific characteristics flow from less excellent things to the natures which precede them.

false deserves to be discussed. For such a discussion does not require a vast number of arguments; rather, he tires himself in vain who, proposing things that are false, later endeavors to disprove them as things that are not true. For how is it possible that an essence—which is naturally incorporeal and has nothing in common with the bodies that participate in it—should be distinguished from other things by physical qualities? How can that which is not physically present within bodies be separated by physical locations? And how can that which is not enclosed by the divisible boundaries of subjects be divisibly detained by the various parts of the world? 

Moreover, what is there that can prevent the Gods from being everywhere? And what can restrain their power from extending as far as the celestial arch? For to achieve this would require a more powerful cause, one capable of enclosing and limiting them to specific parts. But truly existing being, which is essentially incorporeal, is everywhere, wherever it may wish to be. And that which is divine and transcends all things would <note>if what you say were admitted</note> be transcended by the perfection of the whole world and, as a certain part, would be contained by it. In short, this opinion completely subverts sacred institutions and the theurgic communion of the Gods with men, since it removes the presence of the more excellent genera from the earth. For it says nothing other than that the divine dwell far from earthly natures, and that this, our place of residence, is deserted by them. According to this assertion, therefore, neither can we who are priests learn anything from the Gods, nor do you rightly inquire of us as though we knew more than others, since we would differ in no respect from other men.

None of these assertions, however, is sound. For the Gods are not detained in certain parts of the world, nor are earthly natures deprived of their providential attention. But the divinities are characterized by this: that they are not encompassed by anything, and that they encompass all things within themselves. But terrestrial natures possess their existence in the *pleromas** of the Gods; and when they become adapted for divine participation, then

Through these arguments, therefore, we have shown that this entire division is false; that your method of investigating specific characteristics is irrational; and that to suppose the governance of the Gods is fixed in a certain place is by no means to understand the full essence and power within them. It would have been appropriate, therefore, to have omitted your opposing inquiry concerning this distribution of more excellent beings, as it does not contradict true conceptions in any way. However, because it is necessary to direct one's attention toward true science rather than disputing with men, we shall also adapt this inquiry to a rational and theological understanding.
