<meta>This page continues the response to the inquiry regarding the distribution of the Gods across various realms, specifically addressing the misconception that they are confined only to the heavens.</meta>

For what you assert in the beginning is not true—that the Gods revolve in the heavens alone—since all things are full of them. You also inquire, “*How are some of the Gods said to be aerial, and why are different Gods allotted different places and limited portions of bodies, even though they possess infinite, indivisible, and incomprehensible power? And how, likewise, will there be a union among them, as they are separated by divisible boundaries of parts, and by differences of places and the bodies associated with them?*” For all these, and an infinite number of other similar questions, one and the same best solution will be obtained by examining the nature of divine allotment.

A divine nature, therefore, whether it is assigned certain parts of the universe—such as heaven or earth, or sacred cities and regions, or certain groves, or sacred statues—externally <note>That is, without attachment, physical proximity, or a dependent relationship to the things it illuminates.</note> illuminates all these, in the same way the sun externally irradiates all things with its rays. Hence, as light encompasses the things which are illuminated by it, so also the power of the Gods externally encompasses its participants. Likewise, as the solar light is present in the air in an unmixed manner; this is manifest from the fact that no light is left behind

<summary>The author clarifies that the Gods are omnipresent rather than confined to the heavens and explains that their presence in specific locations is like sunlight illuminating objects without being bound by or mixed with them.</summary>
<keywords>divine allotment, omnipresence, solar light analogy, aerial Gods, divine power, participation, Iamblichus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of how divine presence works using the analogy of solar light, transitioning from the observation that light does not remain in the air once its source is removed.</meta>

in the air, once that which illuminated it has departed, even though heat is still present in it after that which heated it is entirely withdrawn. In the same way, the <term>light of the Gods</term> illuminates separately, and while being firmly established in itself, it proceeds entirely through all beings. Moreover, the light which is the object of <term>sensible perception</term> is one, continuous, and everywhere the same whole. Thus, it is not possible for any part of it to be separate or cut off from the whole, nor to be enclosed in a circle, nor at any time to depart from its illuminating source. 

In the same manner, therefore, while the whole world is <term>partible</term>, it is divided around the one and <term>impartible</term> <term>light of the Gods</term>. But this light is everywhere one and the same whole, and is impartibly present with all things that are able to participate in it. Through an all-perfect power, it fills all things, and by a certain <term>causal comprehension</term>, it encloses and terminates the whole of things within itself, is everywhere united to itself, and joins ends to beginnings. All of heaven and the world, imitating this, revolve with a <term>circular motion</term>, are united to themselves, and lead the elements which are carried around in a circle. Hence, the world causes all things to be in each other and to tend toward each other; it makes the end of one thing coalesce with the beginning of another,

### CHAP. X.

After these things, you again add another division for yourself, “ *in which you separate the essences of the more excellent <term>genera</term> by the difference between the passive and the impassive.* ” But I do not admit this division either. For none of the more excellent <term>genera</term> are passive, nor are they impassive in such a way as to be distinguished in opposition to that which is passive; nor are they naturally suited to receive <term>passions</term>, but rather they are liberated from them through virtue or some other noble condition of being. Instead, it is because they are entirely exempt from the opposition of action and <term>passion</term>; and because they are not at all suited to suffer, and possess essentially an

For consider, if you are willing, the last of divine natures, *namely* a <term>soul</term> purely liberated from bodies. What does such a <term>soul</term> have to do with the <term>generation</term> that exists in pleasure, or the restoration to a natural condition, since such a <term>soul</term> is above nature and lives an unbegotten life? Why, also, should it participate in the pain that leads to corruption and dissolves the harmony of the body, since it is beyond all body and the nature which is distributed around the body, and is entirely separate from the harmony which descends from the <term>soul</term> into the body? Nor does it require the <term>passions</term> which precede <term>sensation</term>; for it is neither detained in a body, nor enclosed by it, so as to require <term>corporeal organs</term> in order to perceive certain other bodies which are external to these organs. And, in short, being indivisible and remaining in one and the same form, and also being essentially <term>incorporeal</term> and having no communication with a generated and passive body, it cannot suffer anything either through division or through a change in quality, nor can it possess anything allied to any kind of <term>mutation</term> or <term>passion</term>.

Therefore, that which remains <note>or the participant of the rational soul</note> becomes the cause of suffering for the <term>composite</term>. The cause, however, is not the same as its effect. Hence, just as the <term>soul</term> is the primary source of composite animals that are subject to birth and decay, but is itself unbegotten and indestructible; so also, although the participants of the <term>soul</term> suffer and do not fully <note>that is, truly</note> possess life and existence, but are entangled with the indefiniteness and diversity of matter, yet the <term>soul</term> is in itself immutable. This is because it is essentially superior to that which suffers; its impassivity does not come from a deliberate choice that leans toward either being affected or unaffected, nor does it receive an added immutability through the participation of some habit or power.

Therefore, again, they pursue the causes of forms and of what is beautiful and good, recognizing baseness from base language. And thus, indeed, the thing itself—namely, turpitude—is averted, but the knowledge of it is made manifest through words, and those who use them transfer their desire to that which is contrary to baseness.

Another reason for these things may also be assigned. The powers of the human passions within us, when they are entirely restrained, become more intense; but when they are called into activity, gradually and proportionally, they rejoice in being moderately <note>What Iamblichus asserts here is perfectly true and confirmed by experience—namely, that the passions, when moderately gratified, are overcome without violence. But Gale, failing to understand this, says, “This is as true as if he had said, you will extinguish fire by adding oil to the furnace.” For a moderate gratification of the passions is not like pouring oil on fire; since this comparison only applies to them when they are indulged excessively.</note> gratified and are satisfied; and from this, becoming purified, they are rendered manageable and are overcome without violence. For this reason, in comedy and tragedy, by observing the passions of others, we check our own passions, cause them to be more moderate, and are purified of them. Similarly, in sacred ceremonies, through certain sights and sounds of base things, we are liberated from the harm that results from the actions performed by them <note>See my Dissertation on the Eleusinian and Bacchic Mysteries.</note>. Such things are therefore introduced for the sake of our soul and for the reduction of the evils that cling to it.

## CHAP. XII.

You also say, “*that invocations are directed to the Gods as if they were passive beings, so that not only are demons passive, but likewise the Gods.*” This, however, is not the case. For the illumination that takes place through invocations is spontaneously visible and self-perfect; it is very far from all downward attraction; it proceeds into visibility through divine energy and perfection, and it surpasses our voluntary movement as much as the divine will of the good transcends a life chosen by deliberation. Through this will, therefore, the Gods—being benevolent and favorable—impart their light to <term>theurgists</term> in ungrudging abundance, calling their souls upward to themselves, granting them a union with themselves, and accustoming them, while they are still in the body,

<summary>The author describes ritual acts as remedies that heal the soul and free it from the bonds of generation. He argues that divine invocations do not make the Gods passive, but rather allow them to voluntarily grant illumination and union to the theurgist.</summary>
<keywords>Heraclitus, generation, soul, liberation, remedies, Chapter XII, invocations, Gods, demons, divine energy, theurgists, illumination</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 12 regarding the nature of divine invocations and how they affect the soul rather than the Gods.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

to be separated from bodies, and to be led back to their eternal and intelligible principle.

But it is evident, from the effects themselves, that what we now describe is the salvation of the soul. For the soul, in contemplating blessed spectacles, acquires another life, acts according to another energy, and is then rightly considered as no longer ranking in the order of man. Frequently, likewise, abandoning her own life, she exchanges it for the most blessed energy of the Gods. If, therefore, the ascent through invocations grants the priests purification from passions, a liberation from the cycle of generation, and a union with a divine principle, how is it possible to associate it with anything related to passion? For an invocation of this kind does not draw down the impassive and pure Gods to that which is passive and impure; but, on the contrary, it renders us—who have become passive through generation—pure and immutable.

Neither do the invocations which implore the Gods to *incline* toward us join the priests to them through passion; but they procure for them the communion of an indissoluble connection, through the friendship which binds all things together. Hence, it does not, as the name seems to imply, incline the intellect of the

<keywords>salvation of the soul, divine energy, purification, generation, impassive Gods, invocations, theurgy, divine union, immutable, cosmic friendship</keywords>

On this account also, such names of the Gods as are adapted to sacred concerns, and other divine symbols, are able—as they are of an anagogic or elevating nature—to connect invocations with the Gods themselves.

## CHAPTER XIV.

Furthermore, with respect to "what are called the necessities of the Gods," the whole truth of this is that necessities are unique to, and exist in such a way as accords with, the nature of the Gods <note>It is well observed by Proclus that "divine necessity concurs with the divine will." *Theia anankē suntrechei tē theia boulēsei*. Proclus, in *Timaeus*, book 1.</note>. Hence, they do not exist as if they were externally derived or were the effect of violence, but in such a manner as the good ought to be from necessity, so the Gods entirely exist and are by no means otherwise disposed. This necessity, therefore, is mingled with a beneficent will and is the friend of love; through an order adapted to the Gods, it possesses identity and immutability; and because it is contained within one boundary, it remains in this and never departs from it. Hence,

<summary>This page defines "divine necessity" not as an external force or violence, but as an internal, immutable necessity that is perfectly aligned with the benevolent will and nature of the Gods.</summary>

## CHAPTER XV.

After this, you move on to another division into opposites, namely the division of Gods in relation to dæmons. For you say, "*that the Gods are pure intellects;*" but you propose this opinion as a hypothesis, or you recount it as a dogma adopted by certain people. And you infer, "*that dæmons are psychical essences participating in intellect.*" Neither, therefore, am I ignorant that this is the opinion of many philosophers; but I do not think it is proper to hide from you what appears to me to be the truth. For all such opinions are full of confusion; since they wander from dæmons to souls, which also participate in intellect; and from the Gods to an immaterial intellect in activity, which the Gods entirely excel by a priority of nature. Why, therefore, is it re-

<summary>The author asserts that divine nature is unaffected and unforced by theurgical practices and begins to challenge the philosophical definition of Gods as pure intellects and dæmons as psychical essences, arguing it confuses them with souls and lower intellects.</summary>

But it is necessary that your doubts regarding this distinction should be properly considered, as the discussion of them belongs to the priestly province.

Furthermore, having said that “*pure intellects are inflexible <note>that is, not to be changed or altered</note> and unmingled with sensible things,*” you doubt “*whether it is necessary to pray to them.*” But I think it is necessary to pray to no others than these. For that within us which is divine, intellectual*, and one—or intelligible, if you are willing to call it that—is most clearly awakened in prayer; and, when awakened, it strongly seeks that which is similar to itself and becomes united to perfection itself. But if it should seem incredible to you that an incorporeal nature can be capable of hearing sounds, and if you should argue that the sense of hearing is necessary for this purpose so that it may perceive what is said by us in prayer, you deliberately forget the excellence of primary causes, which consists in both knowing and

<keywords>prayer, pure intellects, divine nature, incorporeal, primary causes, sensible things, Neoplatonism, intellectual union, sacerdotal</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that divine beings do not perceive through physical senses but contain the essence of prayers within themselves, and it justifies the use of intense supplication as a means for imperfect humans to attain divine likeness.</meta>

comprehending in themselves, all at once, the whole of things. The Gods, therefore, do not receive prayers within themselves through any physical powers or organs, but rather contain within themselves the energies of pious invocations—and especially those which, through sacred ceremonies, are established in and united to the Gods. For then, in reality, a divine nature is present with itself and does not connect with the intellectual thoughts in prayer as something separate from its own nature.

“Supplications, however,” you say, “are too foreign to the purity of intellect to be offered to the Gods.” But this is by no means the case. For on this very account—because we fall short of the Gods in power, purity, and everything else—we act in the most appropriate manner by invoking them with the most earnest supplications. For the consciousness of our own nothingness, when we compare ourselves with the Gods, causes us to turn spontaneously to humble prayer. But through supplication, we are in a short time led to the object of our prayer, acquire its likeness through intimate conversation, and gradually obtain divine perfection instead of our own weakness and imperfection.

If, indeed, it is considered that sacred prayers

Or what passion can approach a thing of this kind, the purity of which even the most virtuous human conduct cannot easily equal?

You say, however, “*that the things which are offered in supplications are offered as if to sensory natures and natures pertaining to the soul.*” And, indeed, if the offerings consisted of bodily and composite powers alone, or of such things as are merely subservient to bodily organs, your assertion would be true. But as the offerings participate in incorporeal forms, in certain rational principles, and more simple measures, their suitability is to be examined according to this alone. And if a certain alliance, or likeness, is present, whether it is near or remote, it is sufficient to effect the contact of which we are now speaking. *For there is not anything which, even in the smallest degree, is adapted to the Gods, to which the Gods are not immediately present, and with which they are not joined.* The connection, therefore, of supplications with the Gods is not as it would be with sensory or soul-based

### CHAPTER XVI.

The difference which separates "*Gods from demons by the corporeal and incorporeal*" is the next topic in what you have written. This distinction is much more common than the previous one, yet it is so far from expressing the peculiarities of their essence that it does not even allow for a conjectural knowledge of them, nor of any accidents which pertain to them. For it is not possible from these things to understand whether they are animals or not, and whether they are deprived of life or have no need for it at all. Furthermore, it is not easy to guess how these names are predicated—whether they are used in a common sense or applied to many different things. For if they are used in common, it is absurd that a line and time, God and demons, fire and water, should all be classified under the same incorporeal <term>genus</term>. But if they are applied to many different things, what reason is there when you speak of the incor—

<summary>The author critiques the common distinction between Gods and demons based on being "corporeal" or "incorporeal," arguing that such terms are too vague to define their true essence. He points out that using "incorporeal" as a general category is illogical because it would group divine beings with unrelated concepts like time, lines, or elements.</summary>

<keywords>Gods, demons, corporeal, incorporeal, essence, genus, superessential hyparxes, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of dividing Gods from demons based on the categories of "corporeal" and "incorporeal." It completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the ambiguity of the term "incorporeal."</meta>

poreal, that you should rather demonstrate by it Gods than points; or when you speak of the corporeal, that you should not be thought to speak of the earth rather than of demons?

For neither is this very thing defined: whether Gods and demons have bodies, or are carried in bodies, as in a vehicle, or use them, or contain them, or are uniquely the same* as body.

But, perhaps, it is not proper to examine this distinction very minutely. For you do not propose it as your own decision, but you present it as the opinion of others.

### CHAPTER XVII.

We will exchange, therefore, this division for the doubt which may be raised by you against the present opinion. “*For*,” it may be said by you, “*how, in accordance with what we assert, can the sun and moon, and the visible beings in the heavens, be Gods, if the Gods are only incorporeal?*” To this we reply, that the celestial divinities are not contained by bodies, but contain bodies in their divine lives and

<summary>The author argues that the distinction between corporeal and incorporeal is too vague to define divine beings and questions whether Gods are the same as their bodies or merely use them. <meta>This page continues the explanation of how celestial bodies (such as the sun and moon) relate to the incorporeal nature of the Gods, arguing that these bodies are uniquely suited to divine life rather than being a burden to it.</meta>

energies; that they are not themselves converted into body, but they have a body which is converted to its divine cause; and that body does not impede their intellectual and incorporeal perfection, nor cause them any trouble by its intervention. Hence, it does not require a great deal of attention, but follows the divinities spontaneously, and in a certain manner, by its own motion, not needing manual direction; but, through an anagogic <note>elevating</note> tendency, being itself uniformly raised up by itself, to the *one* of the Gods.

It may also, if necessary, be said that a celestial body is most closely allied to the incorporeal essence of the Gods. For as the latter is one, so the former is simple; as the latter is indivisible, so the former is indivisible*; and as that is immutable, so this is unchanged in quality. If, likewise, it is admitted that the energies of the Gods are uniform, a celestial body also has one circular motion. To which may be added, that it imitates the sameness of the Gods by a perpetual motion, which is invariably the same, and which exists according to one principle.

Hence, this celestial body does not consist of things contrary and different, as is the case with our body; nor does the soul of the celestial Gods merge with the body into one animal from two distinct things; but the celestial animals of the Gods are entirely similar and united together, and are throughout wholes, uniform, and incomposite <note>not made of parts</note>. For things of a more excellent nature are always transcendent in them, in the same manner; and things of an inferior nature are suspended from the dominion of those that are prior, yet in such a way as never to draw this dominion down to themselves. But all these are gathered into one coordinated arrangement and perfection; and, in a certain manner, all things in the celestial Gods are incorporeal, and entirely Gods; because the divine form which is in them predominates, and introduces one total essence everywhere throughout. Thus, therefore, the visible celestials are all of them Gods, and in a certain manner incorporeal.

### CHAP. XVIII.

Your next inquiry questions, “*how some of the Gods are beneficent <note>doing good</note>, but others malefic <note>doing harm</note>.*” This opinion, therefore, is taken from the predictors of nativities <note>astrologers who cast birth charts</note>. It is, however, entirely far from the truth. For all the Gods are good, and are invariably the causes of good; and all of them are uniformly directed toward one good, according to the beautiful and good alone. The bodies, likewise, which are subject to them possess immense powers; some of which are firmly established in the divine bodies themselves, but others proceed from them into the nature of the world, and into the world itself, descending in an orderly manner through the whole of generation <note>the material realm of birth and change</note>, and extending without hindrance as far as to things which have a partial existence.

With respect to the powers, therefore, which remain in the heavens in the divine bodies themselves, there can be no doubt that all of them are similar. Hence, it remains that we should discuss those powers which are transmitted from there to us, and are mixed with generation. These, therefore, descend with unchanging sameness for the salvation of the universe,

<summary>The author refutes the astrological claim that some Gods are harmful, asserting that all Gods are inherently good and directed toward the same goal. He explains that divine powers descend from the heavens into the physical world in an orderly way to preserve the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Gods, beneficent, malefic, nativities, divine bodies, generation, partial existence, salvation of the universe, astrology, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how divine, unchanging powers interact with the realm of generation (the material world of change and birth).</meta>

and consistently encompass the whole of generation in the same way. They are also unaffected and unchanging, even though they extend into that which is subject to change and influence. For because generation is diverse and made up of different things, it receives *the oneness* of the Gods—and that part of them which is without variation—with conflict and divisibility, in accordance with its own oppositions and divisions. It also receives that which is unaffected in a passive way; and, in short, it participates in them according to its own specific nature, rather than according to their divine power. Just as, therefore, that which is generated <note>or has its existence in the process of becoming</note> participates in being in a generative way, and body participates in the incorporeal <note>the non-physical</note> in a physical way; so, too, the physical and material substances within generation participate in the immaterial and ethereal bodies—which are above nature and generation—in a confused and disorderly manner. Therefore, those who attribute color, shape, and touch to intelligible forms are foolish, simply because the things that participate in those forms possess such qualities; likewise, those who attribute wickedness to the celestial bodies are mistaken because the things that receive their influence sometimes produce evil outcomes. For the influence coming from the source could not be of that nature unless the receiver underwent some transformation.

<summary>The author explains that while the Gods are unchanging and pure, the material world receives their influence in a fragmented and disorderly way based on its own nature. He argues that it is a mistake to blame the celestial bodies for evil or physical traits, as these are results of the material recipient rather than the divine source.</summary>

<keywords>generation, impassive, immutable, divine oneness, incorporeal, intelligible forms, celestial bodies, participation, Neoplatonism, material substance</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how divine, unchanging powers are received by the material world of generation, explaining that the "evil" or "disorder" observed below is a result of the recipient's nature rather than the divine source.</meta>

But if that which is shared is received as if in another and different thing, this other thing in earthly natures is evil and disorderly. Therefore, this participation becomes the cause of the wide differences in secondary natures, as well as the mixing of material substances with immaterial outflows; and besides these, another cause is this: that what is imparted in one way is received in another by terrestrial substances. Thus, for instance, the outflow of Saturn is restrictive, but that of Mars is motivating; but the passive <term>genesiurgic</term> receptacle in material substances receives the former through freezing and cooling, but the latter through an inflammation that exceeds moderation. Do not, therefore, the corruption and lack of symmetry arise from a deviation that causes difference, and which is material and passive? Hence the weakness of material and earthly places—being unable to receive the genuine power and most pure life of the ethereal natures—transfers its own passive suffering to the primary causes. It is just as if someone with a diseased body, unable to bear the life-giving heat of the sun, should falsely dare to say, as a consequence of looking to his own illnesses, that the sun is not useful to health or life.

A certain thing of this kind also may take

<summary>The author explains that the perceived "evil" or disorder in the material world arises because earthly substances lack the strength to receive divine influences purely, distorting them according to their own limitations. <meta>This page continues the argument that perceived "evil" results from the partial and fragmented nature of material recipients rather than from divine sources. It then begins Chapter 19, addressing the relationship between embodied celestial Gods and incorporeal Gods.</meta>

place in the harmony and <term>crasis</term> of the universe: for the same things may be the salvation of the whole, through the perfection of the inherent qualities and the recipients; but they may be harmful to the parts, through their <term>partible</term> lack of symmetry. In the motion, therefore, of the universe, all the circulations preserve the whole world as invariably the same; but some one of the parts is frequently injured by another part, which we see is sometimes the case in a dance.

Again, therefore, corruptibility and mutability are passions <term>connascent</term> with partial natures. But it is not proper to ascribe these to wholes and first causes, either as if they existed in them, or as if they proceeded to terrestrial substances from them. Hence, through these points it is demonstrated that neither the celestial Gods nor their gifts are the cause of evil.

In a similar manner, this is also the illustrious prerogative of the cause and orderly distribution of the Gods, because of which the same union of all the divinities extends from on high as far as to the end of the divine order. But if this deserves to be doubted, the contrary would be wonderful—namely, that there should not be this union of the visible and intelligible Gods. And this is sufficient concerning the contact with, and establishment of, the sensible in the intelligible Gods.

## CHAP. XX.

After this, you again resume the same inquiries, for which what has already been said may be considered a sufficient solution. Since, however, it is necessary—according to the proverb—to frequently speak of and consider things that are beautiful, we shall not pass over these details as if they had already been sufficiently answered; instead, by repeatedly discussing them, we may perhaps obtain from all of them a certain perfect and great scientific benefit. For you doubt “*what it is that distinguishes daemons from the visible and invisible Gods, since the visible are joined with the invisible divinities.*” But I, beginning with this as the first point, shall demonstrate how they differ. Because the visible Gods are united to the intelligible Gods and share the same form with them, while daemons are far removed from them in essence and scarcely represent them through similarity, for this reason they are separated from the visible Gods. They also differ from the invisible Gods according to the very nature of what is invisible*.

---

<note>That is, according to the difference between the invisibility of Gods and the invisibility of daemons.</note>

For daemons, indeed, are in-

<summary>This page addresses the distinction between daemons and the visible and invisible Gods, explaining that while visible Gods are essentially united with the intelligible, daemons are distant in nature and only faintly resemble the divine.</summary>
<keywords>daemons, visible Gods, invisible Gods, intelligible Gods, divine essence, Neoplatonism, divine distinction</keywords>

visible, and in no way to be perceived by the senses; but the Gods transcend rational knowledge and material intelligence. And because they are unknown and unapparent to these, they are named in this way; but they are said to be invisible in a way very different from how this is asserted of daemons. What, therefore, do the invisible Gods, insofar as they are invisible, have that is more excellent than the visible Gods? Nothing. For that which is divine, wherever it may be and whatever role it may possess, has the same power and dominion over all the natures that are arranged under it. Moreover, even if the invisible Gods should become visible, they still rule over invisible daemons. For neither the place that receives divinity, nor any particular part of the world, produces any change in the dominion of the Gods. Instead, the entire essence of the Gods remains everywhere the same, indivisible and unchanging, which all subordinate beings similarly revere, in the order assigned to them by nature.

### CHAPTER XXI.

The division, however, between the passive and the impassive <note>Impassive: incapable of suffering or being affected by external agency.</note> which you adopt might be rejected by some as not being applicable to either of the more excellent genera <note>Referring to the categories of divine and daemoniacal beings.</note>, for the reasons we have already listed. It also deserves to be overturned because it implies that these genera are passive, based on what occurs during religious ceremonies. For what sacred institution, or what religious practice following priestly laws, is carried out through passion, or results in the fulfillment of certain passions? Is not each of these ordained by law from the beginning, in accordance with the sacred laws of the Gods, and in an intellectual manner? Each also imitates both the intelligible and celestial order of the Gods, and contains the eternal measures of beings, as well as those admirable signatures sent here by the Demiurge <note>The artisan-like figure responsible for fashioning and maintaining the physical universe.</note> and father of all things, through which matters of an ineffable nature

<summary>This page concludes the comparison between Gods and daemons and begins challenging the idea that divine beings are subject to "passions" during religious rites. The author argues that sacred ceremonies are intellectually ordained and mirror the divine order of the Demiurge.</summary>
<keywords>Gods, daemons, passive, impassive, religious ceremonies, priestly laws, Demiurge, intelligible order, sacred laws</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that sacred rites and symbols are not expressions of human passion but are manifestations of divine order and causes that transcend human reason.</meta>

are revealed into light through arcane symbols; formless things are conquered by forms; things more excellent than any image are expressed through images; and all things are accomplished through a divine cause alone, which is separated from passions to such a great degree that reason is not able to come into contact with it.

This, therefore, is essentially the cause of our straying into a multitude of misconceptions. For men, being in reality unable to understand the reasons for sacred institutions, yet believing that they are able, are completely swept away by their own human passions and form conjectures about divine matters based on things pertaining to themselves. In doing so, however, they err in two ways: because they fall away from divine natures; and because, being deprived of these, they drag them down to human passions. But it is necessary not to understand things performed for both Gods and men—such as genuflections <note>The act of kneeling or bending the knee in worship.</note>, adorations, gifts, and first fruits—in the same way, but to distinguish one from the other, in accordance with the difference between things more and things less honorable; and to revere the former as divine, but to disregard the latter as human and as performed for men. It is also proper to consider,

# SECTION II.

---

## CHAP. I.

It is also necessary to demonstrate to you how daemons, heroes, and souls differ from each other, and whether this difference is based on essence, power, or energy. I say, therefore, that daemons are produced according to the generative and demiurgic powers of the Gods, at the most remote limit of progression and the final division into parts. But heroes are produced according to the reasons <note>or effective principles</note> of life in divine natures; and from these, the first and perfect measures of souls receive their completion and division into parts.

Since, however, the nature of daemons and heroes is thus generated from different causes, it is also necessary that the essence of the one should be different from that of the other. Hence, the essence of daemons is effective and perfects mundane natures, and gives completion to the oversight of generated in-

<meta>catchword: in-</meta>

<summary>This chapter defines the ontological distinctions between daemons, heroes, and souls, explaining that daemons arise from the Gods' generative powers while heroes stem from the principles of divine life. These differing origins dictate their unique essences and their respective roles in the governance of the natural world.</summary>

<keywords>daemons, heroes, souls, essence, power, energy, demiurgic powers, divine life, mundane natures, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the distinction between daemons, heroes, and souls, specifically focusing on their essences, powers, and activities (energies) within the divine hierarchy.</meta>

individuals. But the essence of heroes is vital and rational, and is the leader of souls. And, regarding the powers of each, those of daemons must be defined as prolific, overseeing nature and the bond by which souls are united to bodies. But it is necessary to attribute life-giving powers to heroes, which are the leaders of humanity and are liberated from the realm of generation.

---

## CHAPTER II.

It follows, therefore, that next we should define their activities. Those of daemons, indeed, must be viewed as occupied with the world and more widely extended in their effects; but those of heroes as less extended and as directed toward the order of souls. Since these have been distinguished in this way, the soul follows, which extends to the very end of the divine orders. Being assigned certain portions of powers from these two classes, the soul is abundant with divisible additions and other prerogatives derived from itself. It also produces at different times different forms, rational principles, and behaviors, which originate from different sources; and, according to each part of the world, it employs various lives and ideas;

<sig>G 2</sig>
<vocab>heroes, daemons, soul, life-giving powers, generation, divine orders, divisible additions</vocab>

<summary>This chapter distinguishes the powers and activities of daemons and heroes, explaining that daemons oversee natural bonds while heroes lead human souls. It also describes the soul as the final stage in the divine hierarchy, characterized by its varied forms and diverse manifestations of life.</summary>

<keywords>daemons, heroes, souls, divine hierarchy, life-giving powers, generation, rational principles, Neoplatonism, ontology</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul, contrasting its flexible essence and activities with those of daemons and heroes, and describing its potential elevation to the angelic order.</meta>

...becoming naturally united with, and likewise withdrawing from, whatever natures it pleases; being assimilated to all things, and at the same time, through difference, being separated from them; drawing forth rational principles allied to real beings and created natures; and connecting itself with the Gods, according to other harmonies of essences and powers than those by which daemons and heroes are united to the divinities. It likewise possesses the eternity of a similar life and activity to a lesser degree than daemons and heroes; yet, through the benevolent will of the Gods and the illumination imparted by them, it frequently proceeds higher and is elevated to a greater, *i. e.* to the angelic order; when it no longer remains within the boundaries of the soul, but the whole of it is perfected into an angelic soul and an undefiled life. Hence, also, the soul appears to contain within itself all kinds of essences and rational principles, and forms or species of every variety. If, however, it is necessary to speak the truth, the soul is always defined according to one certain thing, but by adapting itself to preceding causes, it is joined to different causes at different times.

So great, therefore, being the difference between the activities of daemons, heroes, and souls throughout, it is no longer proper to doubt what it is which separates them from

each other; but they are to be distinguished by the unique nature of each. And so far as they are able to form one union, the communion between them must be examined. For in this way, it will be possible to truly comprehend and define separately the concept that ought to be formed of them.

---

### CHAPTER III.

However, let us now proceed to the appearances of the Gods and their perpetual attendants, and show what the difference is in their appearance. For you inquire, “*by what indication the presence of a God, or an angel, or an archangel, or a daemon, or a certain archon* <note>i.e., ruler</note>, *or a soul, may be known.*” In a word, therefore, I conclude that their appearances correspond with their essences, powers, and energies. For just as they are, so also do they appear to those who invoke them, and they exhibit energies and ideas consistent with themselves, and proper indications of their own nature. But so that we may descend to particulars, the <term>phasmata</term>, or luminous appearances, of the Gods are uniform; those of daemons are various; those of angels are simpler than those of daemons, but are subordinate to those

<summary>This page begins a discussion on how to distinguish between the divine and spiritual orders—Gods, angels, daemons, and souls—by their outward appearances. The author asserts that these manifestations are always consistent with the essential nature and power of the being appearing.</summary>
<keywords>Gods, angels, archangels, daemons, archon, soul, phasmata, divine appearances, essences, energies</keywords>

See the fourth book of my translation of Proclus on the *Timaeus* of Plato.</note>, who govern the sublunary elements—will be more varied, but organized in an orderly way. However, if they are the powers that preside over matter, they will indeed be more various and more imperfect than those of the archons <note>properly so called</note>; and those of souls will appear to be varied in every way. And the <term>phasmata</term>, indeed, of the Gods will be seen shining with a beneficial light; those of archangels will be terrifying, and at the same time mild; those of angels will be milder; those of daemons will be dreadful; those of heroes (which you have omitted in your inquiry, but to which we shall give an answer for the sake of truth) are milder than those of daemons; but those of archons, if their dominion pertains to the world, produce astonishment, but if they are material, they are harmful and painful to the spectators; and those of souls are similar to the heroic <term>phasmata</term>, except that they are inferior to them.

Again, therefore, the <term>phasmata</term> of the Gods are entirely unchanging in their magnitude,

In addition to these peculiarities, divine beauty indeed shines with an immense splendor, as it were; it fixes the spectators in astonishment, imparts a divine joy, presents itself to the view with ineffable symmetry, and is exempt from all other species of beauty. But the blessed spectacles of archangels possess the greatest beauty themselves, yet they are not as ineffable and admirable as those of the Gods. Those of angels divide, in a divisible manner, the beauty which they receive from archangels. But the demoniacal and heroical self-visible spirits both have beauty in definite forms, yet the former is adorned with rational principles that define their essence, and the latter exhibits fortitude. The <term>phasmata</term> of <term>archons</term> may be divided in two respects. For some of them exhibit a beauty which is spontaneous and of a ruling character; but others exhibit an elegance of form which is artificial and renewed. And the <term>phasmata</term> of souls are indeed adorned with definite rational principles, but these principles are more divided than those in heroes, are divisibly circumscribed, and are dominated by a single form. If, however, it is necessary to define all of them in common, I say that each participates in beauty according to its arrangement, the peculiar nature which it possesses, and its allotment.

### CHAP. IV.

Proceeding, therefore, to their other peculiarities, we say that with the Gods, indeed, there is an acuteness and rapidity in their energies, which shine forth with greater speed than those of intellect itself, though in themselves they are immovable and stable. With archangels, their speeds are, in a certain respect, mingled with effective energies. Those of angels partake of a certain motion, and do not, like archangels, possess a power that is effective through speech. The operations of <term>daemons</term> appear to be more rapid than they are in reality. In the motions of the <term>heroic phasmata</term>, a certain magnificence presents itself to the view; but in accomplishing what they wish to achieve, their energies are not as rapid as those of <term>daemons</term>. In the <term>phasmata</term> of <term>archons</term>, the first energies appear to be most excellent and authoritative; but the second have a more abundant representation, yet in action fall short of their goal. And the <term>phasmata</term> of souls are seen to be more moveable, yet are weaker than those of heroes.

In addition to these things also, the magnitude of the <term>epiphanies</term> <note>or manifestations</note> in the Gods, indeed, is so great as sometimes to

When archangels appear, certain parts of the world are moved, and a divided, preceding light goes before them. But they exhibit a magnitude of light proportional to the magnitude of their dominion. The angelic light is less than the archangelic and more divided; but in <term>daemons</term> it is even more divided, and the magnitude of the manifestation is not always equal among them. The manifestation of <term>heroes</term> is even less than that of <term>daemons</term>, but it exhibits more of an elevated condition. Again, the manifestation of such <term>archons</term> as preside over <term>mundane forms</term> presents itself to the view as immeasurably great; but those among them who are distributed throughout matter exhibit an abundance of pride and arrogance in their manifestations. Those of souls are not all seen to be equal, but appear to be less than those of <term>heroes</term>. And, in short, the magnitude of the manifestation is appropriately present in each of these according to the magnitude of their powers and the breadth of the empire through which they extend themselves and in which they exercise their authority.

Similarly, according to what has been said, the fire of the <term>Gods</term> indeed shines forth with an indivisible and ineffable light and fills all the depths of the world in an empyrean*, but not in a mundane, manner. But the fire of <term>archangels</term> is indeed indivisible, yet it is seen to have around it an abundant multitude, either preceding or following it. The fire of <term>angels</term> is divided, except that it shows itself in the most perfect ideas. That of <term>daemons</term> is even more narrowly limited by a distribution into parts; it is expressible and does not overwhelm the sight of those who have seen more excellent beings. The fire of <term>heroes</term> has, in a certain way, the same qualities as that of daemons, but at the same time falls short of an exact resemblance to it. Moreover, regarding the <term>archons</term>, the fire of those belonging to a higher order is more transparent; but the fire of those who are material is darker. And the fire of <term>souls</term> is seen to be much divided and varied in form, and is mixed from many of the natures that are in the world. Again, the fire of the Gods appears to be entirely stable.

-> ### CHAP. V. <-

MOREOVER, that which purifies souls is perfect in the Gods; but in archangels, it is <term>anagogic</term> <note>leading the soul upward</note>. Angels alone dissolve the bonds of <term>generation</term>. Demons draw souls down into nature, but heroes lead them to a providential attention to perceptible works. Archons either hand over to them the government of worldly concerns or the inspection of material natures. And souls, when they become apparent, tend in a certain respect toward <term>generation</term>.

Furthermore, consider this also: you should attribute everything that is pure and stable in the visible image to the more excellent classes of beings. Hence, you should ascribe to the Gods that which in the image is transcendently splendid and is firmly established in itself. That which is splendid, but is estab-

To all of these, therefore, you should contrast that which is rashly carried along, is unstable, and is filled with foreign natures—all of which is suited to the inferior orders.

These may also now be divided according to the difference of their mixture. For <term>mundane</term> <note>worldly</note> vapors are mingled with demons and are carried along unstably, contrary to the motion of the world. <term>Genesiurgic</term> <note>relating to birth or generation</note> compositions of <term>pneumatic</term> <note>spiritual or airy</note> substances are mingled with heroes, around which substances they also move. The <term>archons</term> of the world remain invariably the same, exhibiting the <term>mundane</term> nature they possess. But the <term>archons</term> of matter are full of material substances. And souls are filled with an abundance of stains and foreign spirits; when they become visible, each of these ranks presents itself to view along with these attachments.

The following will also be significant indicators to you [of the difference between these powers]. With the Gods, matter is immediately consumed. With archangels, it is consumed in a short time. With angels, there is a dissolving of and elevation from matter. By demons, matter is elegantly adorned. With heroes, there is a harmonious adaptation to it in appropriate measures,

Concerning souls, also, those who are pure present themselves to the view apart from matter, but those of a contrary description are seen surrounded by it.

## CHAP. VI.

MOREOVER, the gifts arising from the manifestations are not all equal, nor do they produce the same results. But the presence <note>For *periousia* [abundance] here, it is necessary to read *parousia* [presence].</note> of the Gods, indeed, imparts to us health of body, virtue of soul, purity of intellect, and in one word elevates everything in us to its proper principle. And that within us which is cold and destructive, it annihilates; that which is hot, it increases and renders more powerful and predominant; and it causes all things to accord with soul and intellect. It also emits a light, accompanied by intelligible harmony, and

<summary>This text explains how celestial governors and pure souls transcend matter while material ones are immersed in it, and details the healing and elevating effects of divine presence on the human condition.</summary>
<keywords>archons, matter, souls, divine manifestations, health, virtue, intellect, intelligible harmony</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison of the manifestations of various spiritual orders, detailing how each level—from archangels to souls—affects the human recipient and their environment.</meta>

exhibits what is not a body as a body to the eyes of the soul, through the eyes of the body. The presence of archangels likewise imparts the same things, except that it does not impart them always, nor in all things, nor does it bestow goods which are sufficient, perfect, and incapable of being taken away; nor is their appearance accompanied by a light equal to that of the Gods. The presence of angels imparts in a divisible manner even more partible goods, and the energy through which it becomes visible falls very short of containing within itself a perfect light. That of demons indeed makes the body heavy, afflicts it with diseases, draws the soul down toward nature, does not depart from bodies and the senses allied to bodies, and detains within this terrestrial place those who are hastening toward divine fire, and it does not liberate them from the bonds of Fate. The presence of heroes is in other respects similar to that of demons, but it is accompanied by this peculiarity: it inspires certain noble and great undertakings. The appearance of the mundane <term>archons</term>, which is visible in itself, imparts worldly goods and everything pertaining to human life; but that of the material archons extends material benefits and such works as are terrestrial. Moreover, the vision of souls that are undefiled and established in the order

It is accompanied by sacred hope and imparts those benefits which sacred hope claims for itself. ### CHAP. VII.

**MOREOVER**, in these manifestations, there is an indication of the rank held by the powers that appear. For the Gods are surrounded by either other Gods or angels; but archangels have angels either preceding them, arranged alongside them, or following behind them. They may also be accompanied by a certain other multitude of angels who serve them as guards. Angels exhibit, along with themselves, the specific works of the order to which they belong. Good demons <note>The original spelling is "dæmons," referring to intermediary spirits between gods and men.</note> allow us to observe, along with themselves, their own works and the benefits they provide. However, avenging demons display the types of punishments they inflict; and such other demons as are corrupt are surrounded by certain harmful—

Archons [of the first rank] exhibit, together
with themselves, certain portions of the world;
but other archons attract to themselves the 
disorder and confusion of matter. With re-
spect to the soul, if it ranks as a whole, and does
not belong to any particular species, it presents
to the view a formless fire, extended through
the whole world, which is indicative of the
total, one, indivisible, and formless soul of the
universe; but a purified soul exhibits a fiery
form, and a pure and unmingled fire. Then,

an undefiled and stable form, and it most

propriate order. But the soul which inclines
downward draws along with it the signs of
bonds and punishments, is heavy with material

## CHAPTER IX.

Finally, the dispositions of the soul of those who invoke the Gods to appear receive, when they become visible, a liberation from the passions, a transcendent perfection, and an entirely more excellent energy; they also participate in divine love and an immense joy. But when archangels appear, these dispositions receive a pure state of being, intellectual contemplation, and an unchanging power. When angels appear, they participate in intellectual wisdom and truth, pure virtue, stable knowledge, and a proportionate order. But when demons are seen, the souls receive the desire for generation and a natural longing, together with a wish to accomplish the works of Fate and a power effective in such matters. If heroes are seen, they derive from the vision other similar characteristics and many impulses which contribute to the fellowship of souls. But when

Thus, therefore, we have appropriately defined the gifts of these powers, in accordance with the proper order of each; and the details regarding the manifestations about which you inquired have received a suitable reply. And that is enough for these questions.

For a God, an angel, and a good demon instruct men on what their proper essence consists of; and they never use an addition in their language that exceeds their power or their appropriate goodness. For truth coexists with the Gods in the same way that light does with the sun. And, at the same time, we say that divinity is not in need of any beauty or virtue that could possibly be added to it through language. Moreover, angels and demons always receive truth from the Gods, so they never assert anything contrary to this, as each of them is essentially perfect; nor can they add anything to it for the sake of praise.

When, then, does the deception you mentioned—of “speaking boastfully”—take place? For when a certain error occurs in the <term>theurgic art</term>, and instead of the <term>autoptic</term> (or self-visible) images that ought to appear, others are seen instead, then inferior powers assume the form of the more venerable orders and pretend to be those whose forms they take; and because of this, they utter arrogant words that exceed the authority they possess. For, as it appears to me,

<summary>The author argues that true divine beings never boast or lie, as truth is inherent to their nature; deception only occurs when errors in theurgic practice allow inferior spirits to impersonate higher ones.</summary>
<keywords>theurgic art, autoptic, phantasm, divinity, demons, deception, spiritual hierarchy, truth</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to distinguish between genuine divine manifestations and the deceptive imitations of inferior spirits, emphasizing that errors in theurgic practice do not invalidate the true nature of the gods.</meta>

If any fraud arises from the first principle, much falsehood is derived from that perversion, which it is necessary for the priest to learn from the whole order of the <term>phasmata</term>; by the proper observation of which, they are able to refute and reject the fictitious* pretext of these inferior powers, as not at all belonging to true and good spirits. Nor is it right to introduce errors into the true judgment of things; for in other sciences or arts, we do not judge their works by the aberrations which may happen to take place within them. You should not, therefore, characterize things which are rarely performed correctly even through ten thousand labors by the errors which may, through ignorance, happen to them; but rather assert something else about them. For if the works which result from the appearance of these powers are such as you say—*namely*, if they are arrogant and false—yet the operations involving the fire of true spirits are genuine and true. For, as in all other things, those which are primary begin first with themselves and bestow upon themselves that which they give to others (as, for instance, in essence, in life, and in motion), so also the natures which provide truth to all beings primarily proclaim the truth of

<meta>This page continues the argument that divine beings only reveal their true nature and do not use deceptive appearances. It refutes the idea that higher beings would use phantasms or falsehoods to communicate with humans.</meta>

Gods reveal true images of themselves, but by no means project phantasms of themselves, such as those that exist in water or in mirrors. For what reason should they exhibit these? Should we say it is to bring with them an indication of their own essence and power? This, however, is certainly not the case. For these phantasms become a cause of deception to those who believe in them, and draw the spectators away from the true knowledge of the Gods. Should we say, then, that it is because they provide a certain usefulness to those who behold them? But what advantage can be derived from falsehood? If, therefore, this is not the case, could it be natural for divinity to project a phantasm from itself? But how can that which is firmly established in itself, and which is the cause of essence and truth, produce a certain deceitful imitation of itself in a foreign place? 

By no means, therefore, does divinity either transform himself into phantasms, nor project these from himself to other things; rather, he emits, through illumination, true representations of himself in the true character of souls. Corresponding to this, the attendants of the Gods also strive to emulate the self-visible truth of the Gods. But as for what you now say—" *that it is common to Gods and <term>dæmons</term>, and the rest of the more excellent <term>genera</term>, to produce fictitious images and to speak boast-*

These <term>genera</term>, however, have nothing in common, nor is the communion between them illusory; nor is it appropriate to judge first <term>essences</term> and the true impressions of forms within them based on such natures as are lowest, or from the errors that occur in them. For by thinking of these <term>essences</term> in this way, we shall think correctly and in a way pleasing to the Gods.

For, even if we grant that “*ignorance and deception are error and impiety,*” it still does not follow that, on this account, the things offered to the Gods and divine works are false. For a conception of the mind does not unite <term>theurgists</term> with the Gods; since, if this were the case, what would prevent those who philosophize theoretically from having a <term>theurgic union</term> with the Gods? In reality, however, this is not the case. For the perfect efficacy of <term>ineffable</term> works—which are divinely performed in a way surpassing all intelligence—and the power of inexplicable symbols, which are known only to the Gods, impart <term>theurgic union</term>. Therefore, we do not perform these things through intellectual perception; since, if this were the case, their intellectual energy would be provided by us; neither of which is true. <meta>This page continues the argument that divine union is achieved through sacred symbols rather than human intelligence, emphasizing that divine causes are self-moved and not dependent on inferior human activities.</meta>

*<term>symthemata</term>** themselves perform their own proper work by themselves, and the ineffable power of the Gods itself knows its own images by itself. It does not, however, know them as if it were stimulated by our intelligence; for it is not natural that things which encompass should be stimulated by those that are encompassed, nor perfect natures by imperfect ones, nor wholes by parts. Therefore, divine causes are not previously called into activity by our intellectual perceptions; but it is necessary to consider these, as well as all the best dispositions of the soul and the purity belonging to us, as certain contributing causes; since the things which properly stimulate the divine will are the divine <term>symthemata</term> themselves. And thus, things pertaining to the Gods are moved by themselves, and do not receive any principle of their own proper activity from any inferior nature within themselves.

I have, however, been this lengthy so that you may not think all the authority of the activity in <term>theurgic operations</term> is in our power, and so that you may not suppose their true work consists in our conceptions, or that their failure consists in our deception. Therefore, neither this, nor anything else of a similar human nature within us, contributes at all to the outcome of divine actions.

Accept, therefore, these points, which are made in passing but serve as a sufficient reply to your entire conception of the theurgic art. Your assertions also relate to the same matter, where you say, "that the science of the Gods is sacred and useful," and call the ignorance of honorable and beautiful things darkness, but the knowledge of them light; and also add, "that the ignorance of these things fills men with all evils, through lack of learning and audacity, but the knowledge of them is the cause of all good." For all these assertions point toward the same conclusion as the previous ones and are addressed together with them in this discussion. It is necessary, therefore, to leave them aside, move on to the inquiries concerning divination, and concisely resolve them.

# SECTION III.

## CHAP. I.

In the first place, therefore, you ask me to explain to you clearly, “*what exactly is accomplished in the foreknowledge of future events?*” However, what you are trying to learn is immediately impossible. For, according to the meaning of your question, you think that foreknowledge is something created, or something that exists in the process of coming to be, and pertains to things that have a natural existence. It is not, however, one of those things that exist by coming into being, nor is it brought about through physical change, nor is it invented and devised as something useful for the purposes of life; nor, in short, is it a human work. Instead, it is divine and supernatural, and is sent down to us from the heavens above. It is also unbegotten and eternal, and naturally possesses a prior existence.

The greatest remedy, therefore, for all such doubts is this: to know the principle of divina-

<vocab>foreknowledge, existence, divine, supernatural, eternal, divination</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that foreknowledge of the future is not a human invention or a physical process, but a divine and eternal gift from the heavens. He begins to establish that understanding the true principle of divination is necessary to resolve doubts about its nature.</summary>
<keywords>foreknowledge, divination, divine origin, eternity, supernatural, human work vs. divine work, Porphyry</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the principle of divination, which was introduced at the end of the previous chapter.</meta>

tion, that it neither originates from bodies, nor from the passions related to bodies, nor from a certain nature and the powers surrounding nature, nor from any human means or the habits pertaining to them. Neither does it originate from a certain art, externally acquired, concerning those things that serve the needs of life. For its entire authority belongs to the Gods and is granted by them; it is also brought about by divine works or signs, and it possesses divine visions and scientific <term>theorems</term>. All other things, however, are subjected as instruments to the gift of foreknowledge transmitted from the Gods; *namely*, those things pertaining to our soul and body, and those that exist in the nature of the universe or are inherent in particular natures. But some things are already underlying, as in the order of matter, such as places or certain other things of a similar kind.

If someone, however, ignoring primary causes, should attribute divination to secondary functions—such as the motions of bodies, the changes of passions, or certain other motions, the activities of human life, or animal or physical rationales—and should think that in doing so he states something obvious; or if, considering the symmetries of these things in relation to each other as causes, he should believe

## CHAP. II.

Regarding the divination, therefore, that occurs in sleep, you say the following: “*We frequently obtain through dreams, when we are asleep, a knowledge of future events, not being in a state of ecstasy by which we are greatly agitated, for the body is quiet; but we do not perceive what we see as clearly as when we are awake.*” However, what you describe usually happens in human dreams—those prompted by the soul, or by some of our thoughts, or by reason, or by imaginations, or certain daily concerns. These, indeed, are sometimes true and sometimes false; in some matters they grasp reality, but in many they deviate from it.

But the dreams called *theopemptoi*, or those sent from God, do not exist in the manner you mention. Instead, they take place either when sleep is leaving us and we are beginning to wake—at which point we hear a certain voice that briefly tells us what must be done—or voices are heard by us between sleeping and waking, or when we are fully awake. And sometimes, indeed, an invisible and incorporeal spirit surrounds the reclining—

<sig>I 2</sig>

<vocab>divination, dreams, ecstasy, theopemptoi, God, soul, spirit</vocab>

<summary>The author distinguishes between ordinary human dreams, which are often unreliable reflections of daily life, and divinely sent dreams called theopemptoi. These divine dreams occur during the transition to wakefulness or while fully awake, offering clear guidance through voices or spiritual presences.</summary>

<keywords>divination, dreams, theopemptoi, divine revelation, human soul, spiritual voices, wakefulness, ecstasy</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of theophanies and divine dreams, specifically detailing how the divine spirit interacts with the person receiving the vision and the sensations involved.</meta>

recumbents, so as not to be perceived by sight, but by a certain other shared sensation and intelligence. The entrance of this spirit is also accompanied by a noise, and he spreads himself on all sides without any contact, and performs wonderful works that lead to the liberation of the passions of the soul and body. But sometimes a bright and tranquil light shines forth, by which the vision of the eyes is held, and which causes them to close, even though they were open before. The other senses, however, are in an alert state, and in a certain respect have a shared sensation of the light revealed by the Gods; and those reclining hear what the Gods say, and know, through a continuous perception, what they are then doing. This, however, is seen in an even more perfect way when the vision perceives, when intellect, being strengthened, follows what is performed, and this is accompanied by the movement of the observers. Since such and so many are the differences of these dreams, none of them is similar to human dreams. But *wakefulness**, a holding of the eyes, a

Take away, therefore, from divine dreams, among which also divination is included, " *the being asleep*," and also the assertion " *that we do not understand what we see in sleep as clearly as when we are awake*." And, if it is necessary to speak the truth, the presence of the Gods in the former case is necessarily clearer and more precise, and produces a more perfect perception than in the latter. Therefore, some who do not know these signs of prophetic dreams, and who imagine that they have something in common with human dreams, only rarely and by chance obtain a foreknowledge of the future; and as a result of this, they reasonably doubt how dreams contain any truth. ### CHAP. III.

The wise*, therefore, speak as follows: The soul has a twofold life—one in conjunction with the body, and the other separate from all body. When we are awake, we mostly employ the life held in common with the body, except when we separate ourselves entirely from it through pure intellectual and <term>dianoetic</term> <note>Dianoetic refers to the discursive reasoning or the cognitive faculty of the mind.</note> energies. But when we are asleep, we are perfectly liberated, as if from certain surrounding bonds, and use a life separated from <term>generation</term> <note>Generation refers to the realm of physical birth, change, and material existence.</note>. Therefore, this form of life—whether it is intellectual or divine, and whether these two are the same thing or each is uniquely its own thing—is then awakened in us and acts in a way

<note>In the original, there is nothing more than "λεγουσι δε ταδε" (but they speak thus) in this place; but the sense requires that we should read "λεγουσι δε οι σοφοι ταδε" (but the wise speak thus). This emendation is confirmed by the versions of *Scutellius* and *Gale*.</note>

<note>The necessity of this correction is also evident from what Iamblichus adds shortly after, namely *that we must remove "being asleep" from divine dreams*; that is, being in a deep sleep.</note>

<summary>The author describes the soul as having two lives: one tied to the body during waking hours and another, freer life that emerges during sleep. This liberated state allows the soul to engage in intellectual or divine activities while distanced from the physical world of generation.</summary>

Since, therefore, intellect observes real beings, but the soul contains within itself the reasons of all created natures, it follows quite properly that, according to a cause which encompasses future events, it should have a foreknowledge of them, as they are arranged in their <term>precedaneous</term> <note>Prior or preceding.</note> reasons. And it possesses a <term>divination</term> even more perfect than this when it joins its individual life and intellectual energy to the wholes from which it was separated. For then it is filled from those wholes with all certain knowledge, so as to mostly attain, through its conceptions, an understanding of everything that is brought about in the world. Indeed, when it is united to the Gods by a liberated energy of this kind, it then receives the most true fullness of <term>intellections</term> <note>Intellectual perceptions or acts of understanding.</note>, from which it sends forth the true divination of divine dreams and derives the most genuine principles of knowledge. But if the soul connects its intellectual and divine part with more excellent natures, then its <term>phantasms</term> <note>Mental images or visions.</note> will be more pure—whether they are phantasms of the Gods, or of beings that are essentially incorporeal, or, in short, of things contributing to the truth of <term>intelligibles</term> <note>Realities grasped by the intellect rather than the senses.</note>. It understands what has been and what will be; it also surveys the whole of time and the deeds accomplished within it, and it is assigned the task of providentially attending to and correcting them in an appropriate manner. Indeed, it heals diseased bodies and properly manages those things among men that exist in an erroneous or disorderly way. It also frequently brings about the discovery of the arts, the distribution of justice, and the establishment of legal institutions. Thus, in the temple of Esculapius, diseases are healed through divine dreams; and through the sequence of nocturnal visions, the art of medicine is obtained from sacred dreams. In this way, too, Alexander's entire army was preserved—which would otherwise have been completely destroyed in the night—because Bacchus appeared in a sleep and pointed out a solution to the most grievous calamities. The city of Aphutis was likewise saved when it was besieged by King Lysander, thanks to a dream sent to him by Jupiter Ammon. For afterward, he very quickly withdrew his army from there and immediately ended the siege.

What need is there, however, to be long-winded in mentioning every detail of things that happen daily and which exhibit a power beyond all language? What, there-

### CHAP. IV.

AFTERWARDS, also, you say, “*that many, through enthusiasm and divine inspiration, predict future events, and that they are then in so wakeful a state, as even to <term>energize</term> according to sense, and yet they are not conscious of the state they are in, or at least, not so much as they were before.*” I wish, therefore, here to point out to you the signs by which those who are rightly possessed by the Gods may be known. For they either subject the whole of their life, as a vehicle or instrument to the inspiring Gods; or they exchange the human for the divine life; or they <term>energize</term> with their own proper life about divinity. But they neither <term>energize</term> according to sense, nor are in such a vigilant state as those who have their senses excited from sleep (for neither do they apprehend future events in that way); nor are they moved as those are who <term>energize</term> according to impulse. Nor, again, are they conscious of the state they are in, neither as they were before, nor in any

<summary>The author concludes the discussion on divine dreams and begins examining divine possession or "enthusiasm" in a wakeful state. He explains that those truly possessed by the Gods act as divine instruments, moving beyond human consciousness and sensory impulses.</summary>

<keywords>divine divination, enthusiasm, divine inspiration, possession, Gods, divinity, energize, wakefulness, sensory perception</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the characteristics of divine possession, specifically focusing on the physical insensibility and supernatural abilities of those who are inspired.</meta>

other way; nor, in short, do they turn their own intelligence toward themselves, or exercise any knowledge which is uniquely their own.

The greatest indication, however, of the truth of this is the following. Many, through divine inspiration, are not burned when fire is applied to them, as the inspiring influence prevents the fire from touching them. Many, also, even if they are burned, do not realize that they are so, because they do not then live an animal life.

And some, indeed, though pierced with spits, do not perceive it; while others who are struck on the shoulders with axes, and others whose arms are cut with knives, are by no means conscious of what is being done to them.

Their activities, likewise, are not at all human. For inaccessible places become accessible to those who are divinely inspired; they are thrown into fire, and pass through fire, and over rivers—like the priest in Castabalis—without being injured. But from these things it is demonstrated that those who <term>energize</term> enthusiastically are not conscious of the state they are in, and that they live neither a human nor an animal life according to sense or impulse, but instead exchange this for a certain more divine life, by which they are inspired and perfectly possessed.

### CHAP. V.

There are, therefore, many species of divine possession, and divine inspiration is excited in many ways; for this reason, also, the signs of it are many and different. For either the Gods who inspire us are different, and thus produce a different inspiration; or, the mode of enthusiasm being various, produces a different <term>afflatus</term> <note>a divine breath or impulse</note>. For either divinity possesses us, or we give ourselves up wholly to divinity, or we share a common energy with him. And sometimes, indeed, we participate in the lowest power of divinity, sometimes in his middle power, and sometimes in his first power. Sometimes, also, there is only a participation, at other times there is also communion, and sometimes a union of these divine inspirations. Again, either the soul alone enjoys the inspiration, or the soul receives it in conjunction with the body, or it is also shared by the common animal <note>the composite of soul and body</note>.

Because of these things, therefore, the signs of those who are inspired take many forms. For the inspiration is indicated by the motions of the whole body and of certain parts of it, by the perfect rest of the body, by harmonious orders and dances, and by elegant sounds, or the—

<summary>The author explains that divine inspiration varies according to which god is inspiring, the level of divine power being accessed, and whether the soul or the body is participating. These different modes of possession result in various physical signs, ranging from coordinated movement and dance to complete stillness.</summary>
