## CHAPTER VII.

It is not, however, sufficient to learn these things alone, nor will he who only knows these become perfect in divine science. But it is also necessary to know what enthusiasm is, and how it is produced. It is falsely, therefore, supposed to be a movement of <term>*dianoia*</term> <note>the discursive intellect</note>, in conjunction with demonic inspiration. For human <term>*dianoia*</term> is not moved, if it is affected enthusiastically in this way; nor is the inspiration produced by daemons, but by the Gods. Neither is enthusiasm simply an ecstasy; for it is an uplifting and transition to a more excellent condition of being. But delirium and ecstasy demonstrate a perversion to that which is worse. Hence, he who is an advocate for the latter speaks, indeed, of things which happen to those who act enthusiastically, yet does not teach that which is prior. But this consists in being wholly possessed by divinity, which is afterward followed by mental alienation. No one, therefore, can justly understand that enthusiasm is something pertain-

<summary>This chapter defines divine enthusiasm as a state of being wholly possessed by the Gods, distinguishing it from delirium, ecstasy, or the movements of the human intellect. It argues that true enthusiasm is an elevation to a superior state of being rather than a demonic or psychological perversion.</summary>
<keywords>enthusiasm, dianoia, divine science, ecstasy, delirium, daemons, divine possession, mental alienation</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VII regarding the nature of divine enthusiasm, specifically refuting the idea that it originates from human weakness or psychological states.</meta>

pertaining to the soul, or to one of its powers, or to the intellect or energies, or to bodily weakness; nor should it be thought that it cannot exist without the infirmity of the body. For the work of divine inspiration is not human, nor does the whole of it depend on human powers and energies; instead, these effectively serve as a subject, and divinity uses them as instruments. God accomplishes, however, the entire work of divination through himself; being separated in an unmixed manner from other things, with neither the soul nor the body being moved at all, he acts by himself. Hence, when divinations are rightly performed in the way I have mentioned, they exist without falsehood. But when the soul has been previously disturbed, or is moved in the meantime, or the body intervenes and disrupts the divine harmony, then divinations become turbulent and false, and the enthusiasm is no longer true or genuine.

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the therapeutic power of music, specifically detailing how the Pythagorean school used harmony and specific instruments to regulate the soul and behavior.</meta>

likewise, how the differences of these accord with the various dispositions of the soul and the disciples of Pythagoras. Further still, the whole Pythagorean school produced, by certain appropriate songs, what they called *exartysis*, or adaptation; *synarmoga*, or elegance of manners; and *epaphe*, or contact, usefully guiding the dispositions of the soul to passions contrary to those which it previously possessed. For when they went to bed, they purified the reasoning power from the disturbances and noises to which it had been exposed during the day by certain odes and peculiar songs, and by this means secured for themselves tranquil sleep and a few good dreams. But when they rose from bed, they again freed themselves from the sluggishness and heaviness of sleep by songs of another kind. Sometimes, also, by musical sounds alone, unaccompanied by words, they healed the passions of the soul and certain diseases, truly enchanting them, as they say. And it is probable that from this, the name *epode*—that is, enchantment—came to be generally used. In this manner, therefore, Pythagoras used music to produce the most beneficial correction of human manners and lives.”

Proclus also, in his *MS.* Commentary on the First Alcibiades of Plato, observes, “that of musical instruments, some are repressive and others motive; some are adapted to rest and others to motion. The repressive instruments, therefore, are most useful for education, bringing our manners into order, restraining the turbulence of youth, and bringing its agitated nature to quietness and temperance. But the motive instruments are adapted to enthusiastic energy; and hence, in the mysteries and mystic sacrifices, the pipe is useful; for its motive power is employed for the purpose of exciting the reasoning power to a divine nature. For here it is necessary that the irrational part should be laid asleep, and the rational part excited. Thus, those who instruct youth use repressive instruments, but initiators use those that are motive. For that which is disciplined is the irrational

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between music and divine inspiration, following the analysis of how specific melodies and rhythms are connected to the divine orders.</meta>

Conformably, therefore, to such adaptations of melodies to the Gods, the Gods themselves become present. For there is nothing which obstructs them; so that whatever has even an *incidental resemblance to them, directly participates in them.* A perfect possession, likewise, immediately takes place, along with a fullness of a more excellent essence and power. This is not because the body and the soul are in each other, and sympathize, and are mutually affected by the melodies; but because the inspiration of the Gods is not separated from divine harmony, but is originally adapted and allied to it, and on this account it is shared by it in appropriate measures. Hence also, it is excited and restrained according to the several orders of the Gods. But this inspiration must by no means be called a removal, a cleansing, or a medicine. For it is not primarily implanted in us because of a certain disease, or excess, or redundancy; but the entire principle and participation of it are derived supernally from the Gods.

Neither is it proper to say that the soul primarily consists of harmony and rhythm. For if that were so, divine inspiration would be suited to the soul alone. It is better, therefore, to deny this, and to assert that the soul, before she gave herself to the body, was a listener to divine harmony; and that hence, when she proceeded into the body,

<note>part; but it is reason which is initiated, and which operates with divine inspiration.”

### CHAP. X.

LET us, however, discuss what pertains to divination more particularly; not asserting that nature leads each thing to its like, for the enthusiastic energy is not the work of nature; nor again asserting that the temperature of the air, and of that which surrounds us, produces also a different temperature in the body of those who act with divine enthusiasm; since inspiration, which is the work of the Gods, is not changed by bodily powers or temperaments. Nor must we say that the much-celebrated inspiration of divinity is adapted to passions and generated natures. It is acknowledged then by all men, that the oracle in Colophon gives its answers through the medium of water. ### CHAPTER XII.

It appears, therefore, that the divination of oracles agrees with all the hypotheses we have previously presented concerning prediction. For if a power of this kind were inseparable from the nature of places and the bodies that are its subjects, or if it proceeded* according to a motion defined by number, it would not be able to foreknow, with invariable sameness, things that exist everywhere and always. But being separate and liberated from places and things that are measured by the cycles of time, and also from those that are confined in place, it is equally present with all things wherever they may be, and exists simultaneously with all the natures that are pro-

<note>* Instead of ἡ προιουσα here, it seems necessary to read ἡ προ-ιουσα.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the description of the ritual at Branchidae, explaining that the divine source of prophecy is a transcendent God separate from the physical site. It begins Chapter XII by arguing that true divination must be independent of specific locations and time to accurately foresee all events.</summary>

<keywords>divination, oracles, Branchidae, prophecy, divine transcendence, metaphysics, time and space, theology</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the omnipresence and indivisibility of divine prophecy, concluding Chapter XII and introducing Chapter XIII, which shifts focus to private forms of divination.</meta>

duced according to time. It likewise comprehends in one the truth of all things, through its separate and transcendent essence.

Hence, if this is rightly asserted by us, the prophetic power of the Gods is not partially contained by any place, or any divisible human body, nor by the soul, which is confined in one certain species of divisible natures; but being separate and indivisible, it is wholly present everywhere with the natures which are capable of receiving it. It likewise externally illuminates and fills all things, pervades through all the elements, encompasses earth and air, fire and water, and leaves nothing destitute of itself—neither animals nor any of the productions of nature—but imparts from itself a certain portion of foreknowledge to some things in a greater, and to others in a lesser, degree. Moreover, existing itself prior to all things by its own separate nature, it becomes sufficient to fill all things, so far as each is able to partake of it.

---

Instead, it produces a certain movement of the soul which is opposed to the Gods, and attracts from them an obscure and shadowy representation which, through the fleeting nature of its power, is usually disturbed by depraved demonic spirits. That, however, which is truly a representation of the Gods, is in other respects genuine and pure, unchanging and true, and is inaccessible to, and unimpeded by, spirits of a contrary nature. For, as darkness is not suited to withstand the splendor of the glittering light of the sun, but suddenly becomes totally invisible, entirely recedes, and immediately vanishes; so also, when the power of the Gods, which fills all things with good, abundantly shines forth, no place is left for the chaos of evil spirits, nor can it present itself to the view. But, as if it were nothing, it departs into non-existence, not being able to be moved at all when more excellent natures are presented according to time. <note>The text concludes "presented" based on the context of the broken word "pre-".</note>

* Proclus, in his manuscript commentary on the *First Alcibiades* of Plato, observes, “that in the mysteries, one of the more imperfect <term>demons</term> assumes the appearance of one that is more perfect; he draws down to himself souls that are not yet purified and separates them from the Gods. Hence, in the most holy of the mysteries <note>that is, in the Eleusinian mysteries</note>, prior to the manifest presence of the God <note>who is invoked</note>, certain earthly <term>demons</term> present themselves to view, disturbing those who are initiated, tearing them away from undefiled good, and inciting them toward matter. For this reason, the Gods <note>in the Chaldean Oracles</note> order us not to look at them until we are protected by the powers imparted by the mysteries. For they say,

->Ου γαρ χρη κεινους σε βλεπειν πριν σωμα τελεσθεις.<-

*that is,* It is not appropriate for you to behold them until your body is purified by initiation. And they add the reason,

->Οτι τας ψυχας θελγοντες αει τελετων απαγουσι,<-

*that is,* For these <term>demons</term>, alluring souls, always draw them away from the mysteries.

That mitred sophist, Warburton, as I have called him elsewhere, because he did not understand the first part of this last extract from Proclus, ridiculously translates the words
->πολυειδέσι καὶ πολυμόρφοις τῶν θεῶν προβεβλημένοις γένεσιν,<-

See his *Divine Legation of Moses*, book 2, p. 152, 8vo, a work full of distorted concepts and inaccurate translations. And yet, as great a sophist as Warburton was, and despite the fact that the work I just mentioned is full of false and most harmful opinions, he is nevertheless compelled by the truth to acknowledge in book 2, page 172, "that the wisest and best men in the Pagan world are unanimous in this, that the mysteries were instituted pure, and proposed the noblest end by the worthiest means." But this is by the way.

Sometimes, likewise, they use arrangements of such things as are adapted to the Gods who are about to descend, or they employ incantations or compositions, and these are appropriately prepared for the reception, presence, and manifestation of the Gods. But at other times, they cause light to shine forth on a wall, having previously prepared the wall for the reception of light in the best manner by the sacred descriptions of characters; and, at the same time, they fix the light in a certain solid place, so that it may not be widely scattered.

Many other modes, also, of introducing light might be mentioned; but all of them may be referred to one mode, that of irradiation, wherever it may be effected, and through whatever instruments the Gods may illuminate. ### CHAP. XV.

Let us, therefore, pass on to the mode of divination which is achieved through human art, and which involves a great deal of conjecture and opinion. Concerning this, you say the following:

“*Some also establish the art of investigating the future through the viscera, through birds, and through the stars.*” And there are, indeed, many other arts of this kind, but the ones mentioned above are sufficient to represent the entire category of artificial divination. Generally, therefore, this art uses certain divine signs which find their fulfillment from the Gods in various ways. From these divine portents, and according to the relationship between certain things and the signs exhibited, the art makes a judgment in a certain respect and predicts through conjecture based on certain probabilities. The Gods, therefore, produce the signs, either through nature—which is subordinate, both generally and particularly, to the creation of effects—or through <term>genesiurgic</term> <note>Relating to the forces of generation or the production of physical existence.</note>

<summary>The text transitions from discussing divine divination to examining "artificial" divination, which relies on human interpretation and conjecture. It explains that while the Gods provide the signs through nature or generative forces, human art predicts the future based on the probability of these omens.</summary>

<keywords>divination, human art, conjecture, viscera, omens, portents, genesiurgic, divine signs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of signs and the role of daemons in artificial divination, following the explanation of how these arts rely on conjecture based on divine portents.</meta>

daemons, who, presiding over the elements of the universe, partial bodies, and everything contained in the world, conduct the phenomena with ease, in conformity to the will of the Gods. But these signs symbolically pre-manifest the decrees of divinity and of the future, as Heraclitus says, "neither speaking nor concealing, but signifying <note>These words of Heraclitus are also quoted by Plutarch in his treatise *De Defectu Oraculorum*.</note>;" because they express the mode of fabrication through pre-manifestation. As, therefore, the Gods generate all things through forms <note>For *eikonon* (εικονων) here, I read *eidon* (ειδων).</note>, in a similar manner they signify all things through signs, impressed as if by a seal (dia synthēmatōn <note>δια συνθηματων</note>). Perhaps, likewise, they make our intelligence more acute by this means. And this is all that we have said generally concerning this entire kind of human art.

If, also, the power of the Gods proceeds in its prior manifestation as far as inanimate things, such as pebbles, rods*, pieces of wood, stones, corn, or wheat, this very thing is most admirable in the fore-signifying of divine prophecy; because it imparts soul to inanimate things and motion to immovable things. It makes all things clear and known, so that they partake of reason and are defined by the measures of intellection, even though they possess no portion of reason in themselves. Another divine miracle

* <note>Gale observes that this appears to have been a very ancient mode of divination, and it does not differ from that which is categorized under the term wood. Hence the Scholiast, in *Nicander’s Theriaca*, says, “that the Magi and Scythians predicted the future from the wood of the tamarisk. For in many places they predict from rods. And that Dinon, in the first book of his third *Syntaxis*, observes, ‘that the Median diviners predict from rods.’” The Scholiast likewise adds the testimony of Metrodorus, who says, “that the tamarisk is a most ancient plant, and that the Egyptians, in the solemnity of Jupiter, were crowned with the tamarisk, as were the Magi among the Medes.” He adds, “that Apollo also ordained that prophets should predict from this plant, and that in Lesbos he wears a tamarisk crown, has often been seen thus adorned, and that in consequence of this he was called by the Lesbians *Muricaion* [from *myrike*, the tamarisk].” What the Scholiast says here is confirmed by Herodotus, in book IV and elsewhere. To this also belongs what is everywhere mentioned regarding prediction from the laurel. For if the leaves of the laurel made a noise when thrown into the fire, it was considered a good omen, but if they made no noise, it was a bad one.</note>

Divinity also appears to me to reveal itself through signs in these things. For, just as he sometimes makes a foolish man speak wisely—making it clear to everyone that this is not a human work but a divine one—he also reveals, through things that lack knowledge, concepts that precede all knowledge. At the same time, he declares to men that the signs exhibited are worthy of belief and that they are superior to nature, from which he is exempt. Thus, he makes things known that are naturally unknown and makes things that lack knowledge <term>gnostic</term>. Through them, he also instills wisdom in us, and through everything in the world, he rouses our intellect toward the truth of real beings, of things in the process of <term>generation</term>, and of future events. From these things, therefore, I think it is clear that the mode of divination is completely contrary to what you suspected it to be. For it is of a ruling and primordial nature, of an unrestrained power, and a transcendent nature, encompassing all things within itself, but not being encompassed by anything, nor enclosed by those who participate in it. For it ascends into and rules over all things simultaneously and without circumscription, and collectively signifies future events. Hence, from what has

## CHAP. XVIII.

Another challenge, however, awaits us, no less significant than that in which we were previously engaged, and which you at once announce, concerning the causes of divination: "whether a *God*, an *angel*, or a <term>daemon</term>, or some other power, is present in *manifestations*, or *divinations*, or certain other *sacred energies*." But our reply to your question is simply this: it is not possible for anything to be performed in a manner suited to sacred concerns in divine works without the presence of one of the more excellent natures, acting as an overseer and bringing the sacred energy to completion. And where the successful operations are perfect, self-sufficient, and <term>unindigent</term>, the Gods are the leaders of these. But where they are intermediates, and in a small degree fall short

<summary>The author explains that divinity is not restricted by the signs used in divination and moves to discuss whether Gods, angels, or daemons oversee sacred acts. He asserts that all sacred works require the presence of a higher power, with the Gods leading those operations that are perfect and self-sufficient.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of superior beings—Gods, angels, and daemons—involved in divine works and divination, specifically addressing their roles in perfecting and manifesting these operations.</meta>

of the extremes, they have angels as the powers that perfect and unfold them into light. And it is the province of <term>daemons</term> to carry out those operations which rank as the last. But the correct performance of actions which are carried out in a divine manner is entirely to be ascribed to one of the more excellent natures. For since it is not possible to speak rightly about the Gods without the Gods, much less can anyone perform works which are of an equal dignity with divinity, and obtain the foreknowledge of everything without the inspiring influence of the Gods. For the human race is weak and of little importance, sees but a little, and possesses an innate nothingness; and the only remedy for its inherent error, disturbance, and unstable change is its participation, as much as possible, in a certain portion of divine light. But he who excludes this does the same thing as those who attempt to produce a soul from inanimate things, or to generate intellect from unintelligent things. For without the cooperation of a cause, he constitutes divine works from things which are not divine.

ledged, “*that they produce these effects as a result of being drawn toward us by the necessities that accompany invocation.*” For divinity is superior to necessity, and this is also the case with the entire choir of more excellent natures that depends on him. Nor is he only exempt from the necessity introduced by men, but also from that which encompasses the world itself; because it is not the nature of an immaterial being—one which does not receive any external order—to be subservient to any necessity introduced by anything else. And in the next place, invocation, and the things performed by a scientific operator, approach and are united to more excellent natures through similarity and alliance, and they do not achieve their energies through violence. Hence, the effects which are seen to take place in diviners do not happen, as you think, because the scientific <term>theurgist</term> is being passively affected; nor is <term>divination</term> thus carried out through necessity, with passion overwhelming the predictor; for these things are foreign and inconsistent with the essence of more excellent natures.

### CHAP. XIX.

But neither does the cause [of the energies] of more excellent natures exist as a certain middle instrument <note>Gale, in his translation, has totally mistaken the meaning of the original in this place, and it is not unusual for him to do so. <meta>This page continues the argument regarding the supreme deity as the only object truly worthy of divine honor, while explaining the necessity of honoring intermediate powers (the Gods and demons) as the essential links between humanity and the ineffable First Cause.</meta>

<language>English</language>

...happy honor; that which is human being vile, of no account, and ridiculous, when compared with that which is divine. Hence I laugh when I hear it said that divinity is spontaneously present with certain persons or things,

For progressions are consistent with the characteristics of the natures from which they proceed. Hence the cooperating energy of his first offspring is necessary for the development of things into expressible, essential, and distinct existence. *The supreme God, therefore, is, as Iamblichus rightly observes, alone worthy of diligent attention, esteem, the activity of reason, and happy honor;* but this does not exclude paying appropriate attention and honor to other powers that are subordinate to him, who participate greatly in his divinity and are more or less allied to him. For in reverencing and paying attention to these appropriately, we also attend to and reverence him. For that which we diligently attend to, honor, and esteem in them is that alone which is of a divine nature, and is therefore a portion, as it were, of the ineffable principle of all things.

Gale, from not understanding this, exclaims, “if these things are true (namely, that God is alone worthy of diligent attention, etc.), as they are indeed most true, to what purpose, O Iamblichus, is all that great study and labor concerning demons and other spirits?” But the answer to this, by considering what has been said above, is easy. For great study and labor concerning these intermediate powers is necessary in order for our union with their ineffable cause. For as we are but the dregs of the rational nature, and the first principle of things is something so transcendent as to be even beyond essence, it is impossible that we should be united to him without mediators; namely, without the Gods and their perpetual attendants, who are on this account *the true saviors of souls*. For our true salvation consists in a union with the supreme deity.

### CHAP. XXI.

Is, therefore, what you add in the third place more true; namely, "*that there is a certain mixed form of <term>hypostasis</term>, consisting of our soul and divine inspiration derived from the outside?*" Consider this then more accurately, lest we should be deceived by it, being hindered by its plausibility. For wherever one thing is produced from two, this one thing is entirely of a similar species, nature, and essence. Thus the elements which combine in the same thing produce one specific thing from many, and many souls merge into one total soul. That, however, which is perfectly separate, can never become one

<note>* This assertion—that the nature which is perfectly exempt can never become one with that which departs from itself—is opposed by Gale, who says that man is composed of soul and body, and yet the latter is far inferior to and less excellent than the former. But in providing this example, he clearly shows that he does not understand what Iamblichus says. For the human soul, being a medium between a certain indivisible and divisible essence, has a certain alliance with the body insofar as it partakes of the divisible essence and is not perfectly exempt from it. But this is not the case with divine inspiration and our soul; for the former transcends the latter in a perfectly exempt manner. Let it, therefore, be granted to him that, as Psellus says, “<term>hypostatic union</term> brings different essences or natures to one <term>hypostasis</term>,” yet such a union can never take place between two things when one of them has no relationship, proximity, or alliance to the other. Gale was led into this mistake by not properly attending to the words *perfectly exempt*, τὸ παντελῶς ἐξῃρημένον, which are used here by Iamblichus. But such mistakes are common for Gale, due to his inaccurate and rambling way of thinking. He also forgot, at the time he was writing notes on Iamblichus, that he was the master of a grammar school and not a philosopher.</note>

From what has been said, the absurdity of their opinion is also immediately obvious—those who imagine that the divine essence can be mingled and united with the mortal nature. For if such a union were possible, it would benefit and exalt the latter but injure and degrade the former. Just as in the union of the rational soul with the body (as Proclus beautifully observes in *Timaeus*, page 339), “the former, by turning toward a material life, indeed kindles a light in the body, but she herself becomes situated in darkness; and by giving life to the body, she destroys both herself and her own intellect [to the extent that these are capable of being destroyed].”

To admit that an eternal nature is produced through generation is absurd; it is even more absurd to suppose that anything consisting of eternal natures can be dissolved. Therefore, this opinion regarding divination is not by any means reasonable; furthermore, it is paradoxical, whether it is considered as a single supposition or as two.

## CHAP. XXII.

> You say, therefore, "that the soul generates the power which has an imaginative perception of the future, through motions of this kind, or..."

---

...derived. But he will not easily prove that inspiration is something eternal, because it is a transient energy of God.” Gale is correct in his conjecture that Iamblichus, by the word *both* in this place, means the soul and divine inspiration derived from an external source; for it can mean nothing else. But when he adds that inspiration cannot be something eternal because it is a transient energy of divinity, he shows himself to be as poor a theologian as he is a philosopher. For God being an eternal—or rather, a super-eternal—nature, His energies have nothing to do with time and its transient progressions, but are stably simultaneous. Thus, transition does not exist in His inspiring influence, but in the recipients of it, as they are of a temporal and mutable nature. Hence, it is just as absurd to call any energy of divinity transient as it would be to say that the light of the sun is transient because it shines through transparent, but not through opaque, substances.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that eternal natures cannot be created or dissolved and begins a new chapter questioning if the soul itself generates the power of divination. The commentary critiques Gale's view on divine inspiration, arguing that God's energy is eternal and simultaneous even when its effects appear transient to human recipients.</summary>
<keywords>divination, eternal nature, Iamblichus, Thomas Gale, divine inspiration, super-eternal, theology, imaginative perception</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the idea that divine inspiration or demonic entities can be generated from human or material sources, specifically addressing the claim that demons are created from matter or animal remains.</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>
<sig>N</sig>

> “*that the things which are derived from matter constitute demons through the powers that are inherent in them, and especially things derived from the matter which is taken from animals.*”

It appears to me, however, that what you are now asserting exhibits a grave violation regarding the whole of theology and theurgic energy. For one absurdity in it—and the first that presents itself to view—is this: that it makes demons to be capable of being generated and corrupted. And another, which is more serious than this, is that things which are prior would be produced from things which are posterior to themselves. For demons exist prior to the soul, and prior to the powers which are distributed throughout bodies. In addition to these things, how can the energies of a divisible soul which is confined in a body become an essence, and exist by themselves separately outside of the soul? Or how can the powers which are divided among bodies be separated from them, even though they have their very being in bodies? And who is it that, liberating them from a physical state of existence, again collects the physical dissolution and causes it to merge into one thing? For in this way, such a thing would be a demon who has an existence prior to being established. This assertion, likewise, is accompanied by certain common doubts.

<summary>The author refutes the argument that demons are generated from material or animal substances, pointing out that demons must exist prior to the physical bodies from which they are allegedly derived. He argues that it is logically impossible for eternal, prior entities like demons to be produced from the dissolution of inferior, material things.</summary>
<keywords>demons, theurgy, theology, soul, matter, generation and corruption, physical existence, essence</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical critique of the theory that divine or demonic entities can be created from material sources, specifically animal matter, by arguing that an effect cannot exceed its cause in perfection.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>

For how can divination be produced from things which have no divining power? And how can a soul be generated from things which are without a soul? And, in short, how can things which are more perfect be the offspring of those that are more imperfect? Likewise, the mode of production appears to me to be impossible. For it is impossible that essence should be produced through the motions of the soul and through the powers which are in bodies. For it is impossible for essence to be generated from things which are without essence.

From where, also, does the imagination—receiving a divining power from a certain thing—become prophetic of the future? For we do not see that any of the things which are produced through generation possess anything more than what is imparted to them by their first generating cause. But, in the present instance, the imagination would receive a certain more excellent addition from that which has no existence. Unless someone should say that demons preside over the matter which is derived from animals, and that when this matter is brought forward, the presiding demon is sympathetically moved toward it. Such, therefore, are the corrections to this opinion.

### CHAP. XXIII.

The observations that are brought forward after this at first doubt the mode of divination, but as they proceed, they endeavor to entirely subvert it. We shall, therefore, discuss both of these. And, in the first place, we shall begin to resolve the first of these doubts. "*For in sleep, when we are not busy with anything, we sometimes obtain a knowledge of the future.*" This does not mean that the cause of divination is derived both from us and from an external source: for in things where the principle definitely resides within us, and that which follows is derived externally—if these two have a mutual arrangement and connection with each other—in this case, the works of the two are definitely

<summary>The author argues that foreknowledge of the future cannot come from material or localized powers, but only from those liberated from the body. He then begins to address skeptics' doubts about divination, specifically the phenomenon of prophetic dreams occurring during sleep.</summary>

<keywords>divination, demons, the future, foreknowledge, dreams, philosophy, materialism</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that divination, especially in dreams, is not a product of human effort but is externally granted by divine powers. It concludes Chapter 23 and begins Chapter 24, which critiques the notion that divination arises from irrational states of the soul.</meta>

accomplished, and the things which are dependent on them follow their preceding causes. But when the cause is independent of us and exists beforehand by itself, the outcome is not defined because of us, but the whole depends on things external to us. Now, therefore, since the truth found in dreams does not entirely correspond with our own actions but frequently shines forth on its own, it shows that divination is derived externally from the Gods, that it possesses an independent power, and that it benevolently reveals the future when it pleases, and in such a way as it pleases. These matters, therefore, should be answered in this way.

### CHAP. XXIV.

In what follows, while you try to explain divination, you completely undermine it. And is it not rather an impediment to a truer <term>intellection</term> <note>Intellectual understanding or grasp.</note> of things? Furthermore, if the things contained in the world were formed through passions, then in this case passions, through their similarity, would have a certain connection to them. But if they are produced through reasons and through forms, there will be another foreknowledge of them, which is freed from all passion. Again, passion alone perceives that which is present and which now exists; but foreknowledge grasps things which do not yet exist. Hence, to foreknow is different from being passively affected.

### CHAPTER XXV.

What follows next descends from a divine alienation of mind to an <term>ecstasy</term> of the reasoning power which leads it to a worse condition, and absurdly says, "*that the cause of divination is the <term>mania</term> which happens in diseases.*" For, as we may guess, it compares <term>enthusiasm</term> to an excess of <term>black bile</term> <note>In ancient medicine, an excess of this humor was thought to cause melancholy or madness.</note>, to the aberrations of intoxication, and to the madness that comes from rabid dogs. ### CHAP. XXVI.

There are many other controversial innovations as well that may be a cause for wonder. But one may rightly be astonished by the conflict of opinions produced by admitting either that the truth of divination lies with enchanters—the whole of which consists of mere appearances alone and has no real existence—or that it lies with those who are driven by passion or disease, since everything they have the audacity to speak is a fraudulent assertion. For what principle of truth, or what aid to intelligence, whether small or great, can there be in those who are thus insane? It is necessary, however, not to accept the truth of

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, distinguishing the divine truth of divination from accidental or natural forms of foreknowledge.</meta>

...such a kind as that which might be accidental; for this, it is said, can happen to those who are carried along recklessly. Nor should we accept the kind of truth that exists between agents and patients when they are concordantly <term>homologous</term> with each other; for truth of this kind is present in the senses and imaginations of animals. Therefore, this truth has nothing unique, divine, or superior to ordinary nature. But the truth of <term>divination</term> is established in activity with unchanging consistency, has the entire knowledge of beings present within it, and is <term>connascent</term> with the essence of things. It also employs stable reasons and knows all things perfectly, appropriately, and definitely. This truth, therefore, is suited to <term>divination</term>.

Hence, it is very far from being a certain natural <term>prescience</term>, such as the <term>pre-perception</term> inherent in some animals of earthquakes and rain. For this arises from <term>sympathy</term>, when certain animals are moved in conjunction with specific parts and powers of the universe; or when, through the sharpness of a particular sense, they perceive beforehand things which happen in the air before they reach places on the earth.

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, distinguishing divine foreknowledge from the fallible conjectures found in human arts and natural instincts.</meta>

contact with the future, it is not proper to consider an impression of this kind as prophetic foreknowledge. Instead, it is indeed similar to this knowledge, yet falls short of it in stability and truth; it is concerned with that which frequently, but not always, happens, and it grasps the truth in certain things, but not in all. Hence, if there is a discipline that foresees the future in the arts—as, for instance, in the art of navigation or medicine—this does not pertain at all to divine foreknowledge. For such a discipline conjectures the future by certain signs, and these are signs that are not always credible, nor are they connected with the things they signify with invariable consistency. But with divine providence, a stable knowledge of the future comes first; <insert>and this is accompanied by</insert> an unchanging faith dependent upon causes; an indissoluble comprehension of all things in all; and a perpetually abiding and unchanging knowledge of all things as present and definite.

### CHAP. XXVIII.

You mention, however, as something by no means to be ignored, “*the creators of effective images.*” But I would be surprised if these were accepted by any of the theurgists who look upon the true forms of the Gods. For why should anyone exchange truly existing beings for images, and descend from the first of things to the last? Or do we not know that all things produced by a shadowy representation of this kind have an obscure existence, are only phantasms of that which is true, and appear to be good, but are not so in any respect? Other things of this kind that follow are carried along in a changing state of being, but they achieve nothing genuine, perfect, or manifest. This is evident from the way they are produced: for not a divinity, but man is their maker. Nor are they produced from uniform and intelligible essences, but from matter, which is taken up for this purpose. What good, therefore, can grow from matter, and from the material and bodily powers that are within physical objects? Or is not that which derives its existence from human art weaker than the men themselves who give it existence? By what kind of art, likewise, is this image

Hence, the image-producing art is separated by a great distance from the seminal production of realities. Besides, it does not maintain any analogy with divine fabrication. For divinity does not create all things through celestial physical motions, partial matter, or through powers divided in such a way; rather, he produces the worlds through conceptions, will, and immaterial forms, and through an eternal and supermundane soul. The maker of images, however, is said to craft them by means of the revolving stars.

For if the makers of these images know that the fictions about which they are busily employed are nothing more than the formations of passive matter, the evil arising from paying attention to them will be simple. But in addition to this, these idol-makers are similar to the images in which they trust. And if they pay attention to these idols as if they were Gods, the absurdity will be so great that it can neither be expressed by words nor endured in deeds. For a certain divine splendor never illuminates a soul of this kind, because it is not suited to be imparted to things which are entirely repugnant to it; neither do those things which are held by dark phantasms <note>illusory mental images or apparitions</note> have a place for its reception. This delusive formation of phantasms, therefore, will be concerned with shadows, which are very remote from the truth.

<summary>This page argues that artificial idols are transient and lack any genuine value or divine presence. It concludes that those who create or worship these material fictions become as empty as the images themselves, remaining trapped in a world of shadows far from the truth.</summary>
<keywords>idols, idol-makers, passive matter, divine splendor, phantasms, delusions, truth, shadows</keywords>

## CHAP. XXX.

You say, however, “ *that the makers of images observe the motion of the celestial bodies, and can tell from the alignment of one star with another star or stars, whether predictions will be true or false; and also whether the things that are performed will be meaningless, or significant and effective.*” But these phantoms will not, for this reason, possess anything divine. For the lowest of things in the realm of generation move in conjunction with the celestial paths, and are affected by the outflows that descend from the heavenly bodies. Moreover, if anyone considers these things accurately, he will find that they demonstrate the opposite of what is asserted here. For how is it possible that things which are in every respect changeable—and easily so—and which are turned in various ways by external motions (becoming ineffective, or prophetic, or significant, or effective, or different at different times), should contain within themselves, through participation, even the smallest portion of divine power? What then? Are the powers inherent in matter the elements of demons? By no means: for no individual physical bodies generate demons; but much more are these

<summary>This page argues that idols created according to astrological alignments do not possess divine power because material things are inherently unstable and easily manipulated by external forces. It asserts that the physical elements of matter cannot be the source or components of divine demons.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, celestial bodies, images, divine power, generation, demons, mutability, matter</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the relationship between physical bodies and the demonic entities that oversee them, further distinguishing between true spiritual beings and artificial idols.</meta>

generated and guarded by demons. Nor is any man able to fashion, as if by a machine, certain forms of demons; but, on the contrary, he is rather fashioned and formed by them, insofar as he participates in a sensible body <note>A body perceived by the senses; a physical body.</note>. Neither is a certain demonic multitude generated from the elements of sensible things; since, on the contrary, this multitude is simple and acts uniformly upon composite natures. Therefore, it will not have sensible things that are prior to or more stable than itself; instead, being more excellent than sensible things in both dignity and power, it imparts to them the permanence which they are able to receive. Unless, indeed, you call idols "demons," which would be an incorrect use of such a title. For the nature of demons is one thing, and that of idols is another. The order of each, likewise, is very different. Moreover, the leader of idols is different from the great leader of demons. And this, also, you admit. For you say, "that no God or demon is drawn down by idols." What, therefore, will be the value of a holy rite, or of the foreknowledge of the future, if it is entirely devoid of divinity and a demon? Thus, it is necessary to understand the nature of this wonder-working art, but by no means to use or rely on it.

<summary>The text asserts that demons are superior to and independent of the material world, acting upon it rather than being created by it. It concludes that artificial idols lack true divine or demonic power, making the "wonder-working art" of creating them spiritually empty.</summary>

## CHAP. XXXI.

AGAIN, therefore, still worse than this is the explanation of sacred operations which assigns as the cause of divination, “ *a certain genus of demons, which is naturally fraudulent, omniform <note>Having many forms or shapes.</note>, and various, and which assumes the appearance of Gods and demons, and the souls of the deceased.* ” I shall, therefore, relate to you, in answer to this, what I once heard from the prophets of the Chaldeans.

Such Gods as are truly divinities are alone the givers of good; they alone associate with good men, and with those who are purified by the sacerdotal art <note>The priestly or sacred art; religious ritual.</note>, and from these they cut away all vice and every passion. When these Gods also impart their light, that which is evil—and at the same time demonic—vanishes from before more excellent natures, in the same manner as darkness vanishes when light is present. Nor is it able to disturb theurgists <note>Those who practice theurgy, a system of ritual magic intended to invite the presence of the divine.</note> in the smallest degree, who receive from this light every virtue, obtain worthy manners, become orderly and elegant in their actions, are liberated from passions, and are purified from every disorderly motion and from atheistical and unholy conduct. But those who are themselves wicked, and who leap, as it were, toward things of a divine nature in

<summary>The author refutes the claim that divination is the work of deceptive spirits, arguing instead that true divine light purifies the practitioner and dispels all evil or fraudulent influences. He asserts that genuine contact with the Gods removes vice and grants virtue to the theurgist.</summary>
<keywords>divination, demons, Chaldeans, sacerdotal art, theurgists, purification, divine light, virtue</keywords>

These people, therefore, being full of passions and vice, attract depraved spirits to themselves through an alliance, and are excited by them to every kind of iniquity. They are also increased in wickedness by each other, like a circle joining the beginning to the end, and similarly making an equal exchange. # SECTION IV.

## CHAP. I.

Let us then, in the next place, consider the opposing arguments: what they are and what reasoning they contain. And if we discuss certain things more extensively, as a result of speaking freely and at length, it is necessary that you listen carefully to and bear with what we say.

For great effort must be devoted to the highest disciplines, and they must be accurately explored for a long time if you intend to know them perfectly. Therefore, in accordance with the current hypothesis, present the arguments that cause you to doubt, and I will answer you.

Say then, “*it greatly perplexes me to understand how superior beings, when called upon, are commanded by those who invoke them, as if they were their inferiors.*” But I will unfold to you the entire classification—which is worthy of consideration—regarding the powers that are invoked; from this, you will be able to clearly define what is possible

But the middle genera are the watchful guardians of judgment. These inform us what ought to be done and what it is proper to avoid. They also assist just works but impede those that are unjust; and whenever many people endeavor to unjustly take the property of others, or basely injure or destroy someone, these guardians cause them to suffer the same things they have done to others. ## CHAP. II.

But we sometimes see that which is now proposed for consideration take place. For it happens that spirits are commanded [to do this or that] who do not use their own reason and do not possess the principle of judgment. Nor does this occur without reason. For our <term>dianoia</term>, naturally possessing the power of reasoning about and judging things as they are, and containing within itself many powers of life, is accustomed to command the most irrational spirits—those that derive their perfection from a single energy alone. Therefore, it invokes these as more excellent natures because it endeavors to attract things that contribute to wholes<note>And these irrational spirits, insofar as they contribute to wholes, are more excellent than we are, though by being irrational, they are inferior to us.</note> from the whole world in which we are contained toward particular things. And it commands them as inferior natures because, frequently, certain parts of things in the world [such as our reasoning power] are more pure and perfect than things which extend themselves to the whole world. Thus, for example, if one thing is intellectual [as is the case with our <term>dianoia</term>], while another is entirely inanimate or physical, then

<summary>This chapter explains that human reason (dianoia) can command irrational spirits because, while those spirits serve the whole universe, they lack the complex judgment and intellectual purity found in the human soul. Thus, these spirits are superior in their universal function but inferior in their lack of rational faculty.</summary>

<keywords>dianoia, irrational spirits, reasoning power, hierarchy of souls, universal vs. particular, intellectual nature, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of why the theurgist possesses both human and divine authority, allowing him to both petition and command different spiritual entities depending on the context of the ritual.</meta>

That which extends to a lesser degree has a more fundamental power than that which is more widespread, even though the former is much smaller in magnitude and in the scope of its dominion than the latter. For these things, another reason may also be assigned, which is as follows: in all theurgical operations, the priest maintains a twofold character. One is as a man, which preserves the rank held by our nature in the universe. The other, however, is strengthened by divine signs; through these, he is joined to more excellent natures and is elevated to their rank by a refined progression. In this state, he is rightly capable of being clothed in the external form of the Gods. Accordingly, due to this distinction, the priest appropriately invokes the powers derived from the universe as superior natures, insofar as the one who invokes is a man. Yet, on the other hand, he commands these powers because, through secret symbols, he is in a certain sense invested with the sacred form of the Gods.

---

For if the communion of harmonious friendship, and a certain indissoluble connection of union, are the bonds of <term>sacerdotal</term> operations, in order that these operations may be truly divine and may transcend every common action known to men, no human work will be suited to them. Nor will the invocations of the priest resemble the manner in which we draw toward ourselves things that are distant; nor are his mandates directed toward things separated from him, in the way in which we transfer one thing from another. Instead, the energy of divine fire shines forth voluntarily and universally; and being self-invoked and self-active, it works through all things with invariable consistency, both through the natures which give and those that are able to receive its light. This mode of explanation, therefore, is far superior, as it does not suppose that divine works are carried out through opposition or disagreement, in the way that <term>generated natures</term> <note>Beings or things brought into existence through natural birth or creation.</note> are usually produced; but it asserts that every such work is rightly accomplished through sameness, union, and consent. ## CHAP. IV.

### CHAP. VI.

Therefore, in order that we may argue against the objection now presented with an abundance of arguments, we will grant, if you please, the opposite of what we have asserted: *namely*, that certain unjust things are performed in this business of invocations. That the Gods, however, are not to be accused as the causes of these things is immediately obvious. For those who are good are the causes of good, and the Gods possess goodness essentially. They do nothing, therefore, that is unjust. Therefore, other causes for these guilty deeds must be investigated. And if we are not able to discover these causes, it is not proper to throw away

For it is much better to acknowledge the insufficiency of our power to explain how unjust actions are perpetrated than to admit anything impossible and false regarding the Gods; since all the Greeks and Barbarians truly hold the opinion that the contrary is the case with divine natures. In this manner, therefore, the truth regarding these particulars remains.

contradiction of this kind usually happens, will from this point appear to flow. *For an evil daemon requires that his worshipper should be just, because he assumes the appearance of one belonging to the divine genus; but he is subservient to what is unjust, because he is depraved.* The same thing, likewise, that is said of good and evil may be asserted of the true and the false. Therefore, just as in divinations we attribute true predictions to the Gods alone, but when we detect any falsehood in predictions we refer this to another genus of cause, namely, that of daemons; thus, also, in things just and unjust, the beautiful and the just are to be alone ascribed to Gods and good daemons; but such daemons as are naturally depraved, perpetrate what is unjust and base. And indeed, that which agrees and accords with itself, and always exists with invariable sameness, pertains to more excellent natures; but that which is hostile to itself, which is discordant, and never the same, is the characteristic in the highest degree of daemoniacal dissension, about which it is not at all wonderful that things of an opposing nature should exist; but perhaps the very contrary—that this should not be the case—would be more wonderful.

<summary>The author explains that evil daemons mimic divine beings by demanding justice from worshippers, yet their naturally depraved state leads them to promote injustice and falsehood. He concludes that while the Gods are defined by consistency and truth, discord and contradiction are the natural characteristics of lower, depraved daemons.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine and daemonic entities, shifting from the contradictions of lower spirits to a broader physical and metaphysical hypothesis regarding the harmony of the universe.</meta>

### CHAP. VIII.

We may, however, beginning from another hypothesis, demonstrate the same thing. We must admit that the physical parts of the universe are neither sluggish nor lacking in power; instead, just as much as they exceed our own concerns in perfection, beauty, and magnitude, so also is the power present within them much greater. Each part, likewise, is capable by itself of accomplishing different things and produces certain distinct energies. They are also capable of exerting even more numerous effects upon one another. Besides this, a varied form of production extends from the wholes to the parts—partly through sympathy, by the similarity of powers, and partly from the suitability of the agent to the patient. If, therefore, certain evils and destructions happen to parts, they are beneficial and good in reference to the wholes and the harmony of the universe; however, for the parts, they introduce a necessary corruption. This occurs either because the parts cannot withstand the energies of the wholes, or because of some other mixture and temperament arising from their own weakness, or, in the third place, from a lack of symmetry between the parts.

<summary>The author argues that while individual parts of the universe may experience "evil" or "corruption," these events are actually beneficial to the harmony of the whole universe. This destruction at the local level is caused by the overwhelming power of the whole, the inherent weakness of the parts, or a lack of symmetry between them.</summary>

<keywords>universal harmony, physical parts, energies, sympathy, agent and patient, corruption, weakness, symmetry, Neoplatonism, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of universal harmony, moving from the general influence of physical parts to the specific ways that universal energies manifest as fragmented passions and material deficiencies within individual parts.</meta>

### CHAP. IX.

**After** the body of the universe, many things are also generated by its nature. For the harmony of similar things and the opposition of dissimilar things produce many effects. Furthermore, the assembly of many things into the single living being of the universe, and the powers within the world—whatever their number and quality may be—produce, in short, one result in wholes and another in parts, due to the fragmented weakness of those parts. Thus, for example, the friendship, love, and conflict that exist as active energies in the universe become passions in the specific natures by which they are shared. Likewise, those things that are pre-established as forms and pure rational principles in the nature of wholes take on a certain material poverty and a lack of <term>*morphe*</term> (form) when they exist within a part. And things that are joined together in wholes are separated in parts. Hence, divisible natures, which participate in wholes in conjunction with matter, fall away from them in all respects, as well as from what is beautiful and perfect. But some parts are corrupted so that the wholes may be preserved in a condition

<summary>The author explains how universal principles like love and harmony exist perfectly in the whole universe but become passions or deficiencies when manifested in individual parts. This degradation occurs because parts are inherently weak, fragmented, and subject to material limitations compared to the perfect whole.</summary>

<keywords>universal nature, harmony, contrariety, living being of the universe, passions, forms, morphe, divisible natures, corruption, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, explaining that parts of the universe may suffer corruption or pressure to maintain the integrity of the whole, and then begins Chapter X, which discusses the use of universal powers in theurgy.</meta>

conformable to nature. Sometimes, likewise, parts are compressed and weighed down, though at the same time wholes remain unaffected by a disturbance of this kind.

## CHAP. X.

WE shall gather, therefore, what follows from these conclusions. For if certain invocators employ the physical or corporeal powers* of the universe, an involuntary gift of energy <insert>from these powers</insert> takes place, which is itself without vice. He, likewise, who uses this gift <insert>sometimes</insert> perverts it to things of a contrary nature and to base purposes. And the gift, indeed, is moved in a contrary way along with the passions, and sympathetically through similarity; but he who uses the thing which is imparted deliberately draws it, contrary to justice, toward what is evil and base. And the gift, indeed, causes things which are most remote to cooperate through the single harmony of the world. But if someone, understanding this to be the case, should wickedly endeavor to draw certain portions of the universe to other parts,

---

<note>* See chapters 40, 41, 42, of Ennead IV, book iv of Plotinus, from which the doctrine of this chapter is derived.</note>

<summary>The author explains that the physical powers of the universe provide neutral energy through natural harmony, but an individual practitioner can wickedly redirect this energy for base or unjust purposes. This corruption stems from the user’s intent rather than the nature of the universal gift itself.</summary>

<keywords>invocators, physical powers, corporeal powers, Plotinus, Enneads, universal harmony, sympathy, vice, theurgy, energy</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, asserting that the Gods are not the source of evil, and begins Chapter XI regarding the apparent contradictions in divine response to human purity and lust.</meta>

these parts are not the cause of the evil that follows; but the audacity of men, and the transgression of the order in the world, pervert things that are beautiful and lawful. Hence, the Gods do not cause what appears to be base, but this is accomplished by the natures and bodies that proceed from them; nor do these very natures and bodies impart wickedness from themselves, as it is thought they do; instead, they send their proper <term>effluxions</term> to places around the earth for the preservation of wholes, and those who receive them change them by their mixture and perversion, and transfer what is given to a purpose different from that for which it was shared. From all these details, therefore, it is demonstrated that a divine nature is not the cause of evils and unjust deeds.

---

### CHAP. XII.

It is necessary, however, to discuss these things in detail, and to show how they exist, and what reason they possess. It is required, therefore, to understand that the universe is one living being; and that the parts within it are, indeed, separated by locations, but through the possession of one nature, they hasten toward each other*. The whole collective power, however, and the cause of mixture, spontaneously draws the

in short, it explores the essence and power of every thing. From this, therefore, it produces its effects. It forms statues that provide health, makes various figures, and objects that become tools of disease. This curious passage sheds light on the following extract from the first book of the *Metamorphoses* of Apuleius: “*Magico susurramine, amnes agiles reverti, mare pigrum colligari, ventos inanimes expirare, solem inhiberi, lunam despumari, stellas evelli, diem tolli, noctem teneri.*” That is, “By magical incantation, rapid rivers may be made to run back to their sources, the sea be frozen, winds become lifeless, the sun be held back in his course, the moon be forced to scatter her foam, the stars be torn from their orbits, the day be taken away, and the night be detained.” For it may be inferred from Psellus that witches formerly were able to cause the *appearance* of all this to take place. It must also be observed that this manuscript of Psellus *On Demons* forms no part of his treatise *On the Energy of Demons*, published by Gaulminus; for it was never published.

## CHAPTER XIII.

CONSIDER, therefore, another genus of causes as well; how a stone or an herb frequently possesses within itself a corruptive nature, or again, a collective nature of <term>generated natures</term>. For this is not only the case with these, but this physical power is also found in greater natures and greater things; those who are unable to infer this through a reasoning process will perhaps attribute the works and energies of nature to more excellent beings <note>that is, to Gods, angels, and <term>demons</term></note>. Now, therefore, it is acknowledged that the tribe of <term>evil demons</term> has a very extended

For not everyone is able to distinguish a good from an evil <term>demon</term>, or by what specific characteristics the one is separated from the other. Thus, those who are not able to perceive the difference between the two reason absurdly concerning their cause, and attribute this cause to classes of beings superior to nature and the <term>demonic</term> order. If, also, certain powers of a <term>partial soul</term> are assumed in order to effect these things—whether such a soul is held within a body, or has left the <term>testaceous</term> <note>shell-like or physical</note> and <term>terrestrial body</term>, but wanders about the places of <term>generation</term> in a clouded and moist spirit—this will indeed be a true opinion, but it places the cause of these things at the greatest distance from more excellent natures. By no means, therefore, is that which is divine, or any good <term>demon</term>, subservient to the unlawful desires of men in <term>venereal concerns</term>. For there are many other causes for these things.

# SECTION V.

## CHAP. I.

THE doubt you mention next is, as I might say, an inquiry commonly made by both the learned and the unlearned; I mean concerning sacrifices: “*what utility or power they possess in the universe and among the Gods, and for what reason they are performed—appropriately indeed to the powers who are honored by them, but also usefully to those by whom the gifts are offered.*” In the same place, another objection also occurs, namely: “*that the interpreters of prophecies and oracles ought to abstain from animals, lest the Gods be polluted by the vapors arising from them. For this is contrary to the assertion that the Gods are especially attracted by the vapors of animals.*”

---

THE hostile opposition, therefore, in the matters now proposed, may be easily resolved by demonstrating the dignity of wholes with

---

<note>* Hence Iamblichus (in Stobaeus, *Eclogues of Physics*, p. 114) says: Ουχ η αυτη εσι πασων ψυχων κοινωνια προς τα σωματα. αλλ' η μεν ολη ωσπερ Πλωτινῳ δοκει, προσιον εαυτῃ το σωμα εχει εν εαυτῃ, αλλ' ουκ αυτη προσεισι τῳ σωματι, ουδε περιεχε-ται υπ' αυτου. αι δε μερισαι προσερχονται τοις σωμασι, και των σωματων γιγνονται. i. e. “There is not the same communion of all souls with bodies; but the soul which ranks as a whole (as it also appeared to Plotinus), approaching itself, contains the body within itself, but does not itself approach the body, nor is it contained by it. <note>In accordance with this, Porphyry also, in his *Auxiliaries to the Perception of Intelligible Natures*, No. 30, says: “No whole and perfect essence is turned toward its own progeny; but all perfect natures are led back to the causes by which they were generated, even as far as the mundane body. For this body, being perfect, is raised to the mundane soul which is intellectual, and through this it is moved in a circle. But the soul of this body is raised to intellect, and intellect to that which is first. All things, therefore, extend themselves toward this, beginning from that which is last, according to the unique ability of each. But the return to that which is first is either direct or remote. Hence these are not only said to aspire after divinity, but also to enjoy him as far as they are able. But in partial natures, and which are able to turn toward many things, a turning toward their progeny belongs. Hence in these, guilt and disgraceful betrayal are found. Matter, therefore, defiles these because they lean toward it, at the same time that they possess the power of turning themselves back to a divine nature.”</note>

### CHAP. IV.

NOR is that which so greatly disturbs you, and for which you so strenuously contend, attended with any difficulty—I mean the abstinence from animals <note>Iamblichus here alludes to the excellent treatise of Porphyry, *On Abstinence from Animal Food*, from which work the English reader will find several admirable extracts in one of the Introductory Dissertations added to my translation of Proclus on Euclid.</note>—if it is rightly understood. For those who worship the Gods do not abstain from animals out of fear that the Gods might be defiled by the vapors arising from them. For what exhalation from bodies can approach those who, before anything material can come into contact with their power, intangibly cut away all matter? Nor is it the power of the Gods only that abolishes all bodies and causes them to vanish,

<summary>Iamblichus explains that sacrificial vapors are contained by the Gods rather than the reverse, and argues that the divine cannot be defiled by material substances, rendering the strict rules of animal abstinence unnecessary for protecting the Gods.</summary>

<keywords>sacrifice, exhalation, divine nature, abstinence, Porphyry, animal food, defilement, matter, transcendence</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that celestial bodies are immutable and unmixed with material elements, explaining why terrestrial vapors cannot affect or nourish them.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>

without any similarity to them; but a celestial body is also unmixed with all the material elements; nor does it receive into itself anything extraneous, nor impart any portion of itself to things of a foreign nature. How, therefore, can any terrestrial vapor—which is not raised even five stadia from the earth before it flows down to the earth again—either nourish a circulating and immaterial body or, in short, produce in it a certain defilement or any other affectation? For it is acknowledged that an ethereal body is void of all contrariety, is liberated from all change, is entirely pure from the possibility of being transformed into anything else, and is perfectly free from a tendency toward or away from the center, because it either lacks any such tendency or revolves in a circle. Hence, it is not possible that bodies—which consist of different powers and motions, are changed in various ways, and are moved either upward or downward—should have any commonality of nature or power with celestial bodies, or that any exhalation from the former should be mixed with the latter. As the former, therefore, are entirely separated

---

<note>A celestial body, as is beautifully shown by Proclus in the third book of the *Timaeus*, contains the summits of all the elements but is characterized by life-giving, unburning fire; so that, in short, it is vitalized extension.</note>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that celestial bodies are immutable, unmixed, and move in circles, making them entirely immune to any influence or defilement from terrestrial vapors. He contrasts the changing, linear motion of earthly elements with the eternal, circular motion of the heavens to prove their lack of physical communion.</summary>

<keywords>celestial body, material elements, terrestrial vapor, ethereal body, Proclus, Timaeus, unburning fire, immutability, circular motion, physics, transcendence</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that celestial bodies and the Gods are entirely separate from and superior to material elements, explaining why they cannot be affected by earthly vapors.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>

from the latter, they will not effect anything in them. For celestial bodies, being unbegotten, are not capable of receiving any change from generated natures. How, therefore, can the Gods be defiled by such vapors, who suddenly—as I may say—at one stroke, cut off the vapors ascending from all matter and material bodies?

This, therefore, it is not appropriate to suspect of the Gods <note>namely, that they can be defiled by vapors</note>; but it is much more necessary to think that things of this kind are foreign to us and to our nature. For things which are divided, and also material and related natures, are able to have a certain communion with each other in acting and suffering; but things which are essentially different, and those that are entirely transcendent and which employ other natures and powers, cannot act upon or receive anything from each other. The defilement, therefore, produced by material natures, falls on things which are held by a material body; and from these it is necessary that those should be purified who are capable of being defiled by matter. But how can those beings be defiled by material essences who neither have a divisible nature nor possess the power of receiving the passions of matter within themselves? How, likewise, can divinity, who has nothing in com-

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the Gods and celestial bodies are immutable and transcendent, meaning they cannot be defiled or affected by material vapors. He explains that only material and related natures can interact through "acting and suffering," while divine essences remain pure and untouched by the passions of matter.</summary>

<keywords>celestial bodies, unbegotten, mutation, Gods, defilement, vapors, material bodies, communion, transcendent, material essences, passions of matter, divinity</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Gods, being superior to human weakness, cannot be polluted by human passions or material influences.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>

common with us, in consequence of existing prior and superior to human weakness, be polluted by my passions, or by those of any other man?

Neither of these, therefore, pertains to the Gods at all: neither our being filled with material bodies (for there is nothing, in short, of this kind among them, nor are they defiled by our stains, since they are entirely pure and incorruptible), nor the existence of certain material vapors of bodies which are emitted around the earth; for these vapors are most remote from the essence and power of the Gods. Hence the whole hypothesis of contrariety is overturned if no part of it pertains to the Gods. For how, in short, can that which does not exist possess in itself a certain conflict <note>with anything</note>? You therefore wrongly suspect these things to be absurd, and you bring forward doubts unworthy of the Gods, since they cannot be reasonably applied even to good men. For no man who possesses intellect, and is free from passion, would ever permit himself to be enticed by the exhalation of vapors, and much less would any of the beings more excellent than man suffer himself to be thus enticed. These things, however, will be discussed shortly. But now, since this contrariety is, through many solutions, overturned, we shall here finish what we have to say about the first doubt.

<vocab>Gods, material bodies, vapors, contrariety, intellect, passion</vocab>

<summary>Iamblichus concludes that the Gods cannot be defiled by material bodies or earthly vapors because they are essentially pure, incorruptible, and far removed from such influences. He argues that if a rational and passionless man would not be swayed by material vapors, it is illogical to suggest that divine beings could be affected by them.</summary>
<keywords>Gods, material bodies, vapors, contrariety, intellect, passion, divine purity, human weakness</keywords>
