<meta>This page continues the transition from discussing the nature of the Gods to addressing the specific nature and purpose of sacrifices, responding to the recipient's inquiries.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>

## CHAP. V.

Your next inquiry is of greater consequence and concerns things of a greater nature. How, therefore, will I be able to briefly and sufficiently give you an answer to a question which is extremely difficult and requires a long explanation? Nevertheless, I will answer it, and without any lack of eagerness. I will also endeavor to follow what you have concisely indicated and silently suggested. But I will unfold to you my dogma <note>doctrine</note> concerning sacrifices <note>which is as follows</note>. It is by no means necessary that sacrifices should be offered for the sake of honor alone, in the same manner as we honor benefactors; nor for the sake of returning thanks for the goods the Gods have granted us; nor even for the sake of first fruits, or as a repayment with certain gifts for the more venerable goods the Gods have bestowed on us. For these things are also common to men and are taken from the common customs of mankind, but they by no means preserve the transcendence of the Gods and their order as exempt causes.

<vocab>sacrifices, dogma, Gods, exempt causes, transcendence</vocab>

<summary>Iamblichus begins to address the nature of sacrifices, arguing that they should not be understood as mere human-like honors or exchanges for divine favors. He asserts that such views fail to recognize the transcendence of the Gods as causes that exist apart from human social structures.</summary>
<keywords>sacrifices, dogma, Gods, exempt causes, transcendence, divine honor, offerings</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of common views on sacrifice, moving toward a deeper explanation of their spiritual and physical efficacy.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>

## CHAP. VI.

BUT the greatest thing in sacrifices—namely, their effective power—and why they are so uniquely beneficial that without them we are neither freed from plague, nor famine, nor the infertility of crops, nor obtain timely showers of rain, nor things of much greater consequence than these (I mean those things that contribute to the purification of the soul or an escape from the cycle of birth <note>emancipation from generation</note>); these are not at all explained by the types of sacrifices you mention. Therefore, no one can rightly approve of them, because they assign a cause for the works performed in sacrifices that is unsuited to their dignity. And if someone should approve of them, it would only be in a secondary way, as something dependent on primary, more ancient, and venerable causes.

---

## CHAP. VII.

THE discussion, therefore, requires that we should show what it is through which sacrifices are effective, and how they are dependent on the Gods, the preceding causes of

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the true power of sacrifices—both for physical survival and spiritual liberation—is not explained by the common views previously discussed. He asserts that sacrifices must be understood as being dependent on primary, ancient divine causes rather than just human-centered reasons.</summary>

<keywords>sacrifices, efficacious power, purification of the soul, emancipation from generation, divine causes, Neoplatonism, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that sacrifices cannot be explained solely by natural laws or universal sympathy, emphasizing the transcendence of the Gods.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>

effects. If we then say that the connection of similar powers, the conflict of opposites, or a certain suitability of the agent to the patient in the universe—viewed as a single animal that possesses one and the same life everywhere—stimulates corresponding likes, pervading everything with an unchanging sameness according to a single sympathy, and existing closest in things that are most remote: if we were to say this, we would be asserting something true that necessarily accompanies sacrifices, yet we would not be demonstrating the true mode of their existence. For the essence of the Gods is not located in nature or in physical necessities, such that it would be stimulated by physical passions or by the powers that extend through all of nature; but independently of these, it is defined by itself, having nothing in common with them, neither in essence, nor in power, nor in any other way.

sixty in the crocodile <note>The number sixty is no less evident in the crocodile than in the sun. For according to Aristotle (in *History of Animals, book 5*) the crocodile lays sixty white eggs and sits on them for sixty days.</note>, as adapted to the sun; or physical reasons, such as the powers and energies of animals—for instance, those of the dog <note>“Isis,” says Gale, “is the moon. And a dog accompanied Isis when she was diligently searching for her husband Osiris. But the moon perpetually seeks the sun, and therefore that sagacious animal, the dog, matches with Isis. In the ceremonies of Isis, dogs also led the procession.” In this manner others besides Gale, who have not penetrated the depths of the philosophy and theology of Plato, would undoubtedly explain what is mythologically said of Isis. In reality, however, Isis is not the moon, but one of the divinities that revolve in the lunar sphere as an attendant to the moon, and who, in modern language, is one of the satellites of that planet. For, as I have shown from Proclus in the introduction to my translation of the *Timaeus* of Plato, every planetary sphere is a <term>holotēs</term> <note>ranking as a whole</note>, or a part of the universe having a total subsistence—that is, ranking as a whole—and is surrounded by a number of satellites similar to the choir of the fixed stars. Of these satellites, whose leaders are the planets, the first in order are Gods; after these, <term>daemons</term> revolve in bright spherical bodies; and these are followed by partial souls, such as ours. See Proclus in *Tim.* p. 275 and p. 279. This theory, as I have observed elsewhere, is the master key to the theology and mythology of the ancients, as it shows at a glance why the same God is so often celebrated with the names of other Gods; which led Macrobius to think that all the Gods were nothing more than different powers of the sun. The English reader will find an abundant confirmation of what is said here in the fourth book of my translation of the aforementioned admirable work of Proclus.</note>, the

and all the males emit their sperm into a spherical piece of earth, which they roll about, thrusting it backward with their hind feet while they themselves move forward; just as the sun appears to revolve in a direction contrary to that of the heavens, as a result of moving from west to east. They also compared the asp to a star, as being free from old age and performing its motions with agility and ease, unassisted by limbs. Nor was the crocodile honored by them without a likely cause; it is said to have been considered by them as a resemblance of divinity, being the only animal that is without a tongue. For divine reason does not need a voice, and proceeding through a silent path, accompanied by justice <note>Instead of *kai dikes* [and justice], I read *kai meta dikes* [and with justice].</note>, it governs mortal affairs according to it. They also say it is the only animal living in water that has its vision covered with a thin and transparent film which descends from its forehead, so that it sees without being seen, which is likewise the case with the primary God. 

But in whatever place the female crocodile may lay her eggs, this may be concluded with certainty to be the boundary of the rising of the Nile. For because they are unable to lay their eggs in the water and fear to lay them far from it, they have such an accurate foreknowledge of the future that, though they enjoy the benefit of the river as it approaches during the time of their laying and hatching, they still keep their eggs dry and untouched by the water. They also lay sixty eggs and spend the same number of days hatching them, and those that live the longest among them live for exactly that many years; this number is the first of the measurements used by those who are familiar with the heavenly bodies.

Those priests, also, who are most attentive to the laws of sacred rites, when they consecrate water for lustration, fetch it from the place where the ibis has been drinking; for it will neither drink nor go near unwholesome or infected water; but with the distance between its feet and its beak, it forms an equilateral triangle. "We should not, however, wonder if the Egyptians love such subtle resemblances, since the Greeks also, in both their paintings and statues, employ many similar representations of the gods. Thus, in Crete, there was a statue of Jupiter without ears. For it is appropriate that he who is the ruler and lord of all things should listen to no one*. Phidias also placed a dragon by the statue of Minerva, and a snail by that of Venus at Elis, to show that virgins require a guard, and that staying at home and silence are becoming for married women. But the trident of Neptune is a symbol of the third region of the world, which the sea occupies, being ordered after the heavens and the air. For this reason, they also named Amphitrite and the Tritons in this way. The Pythagoreans, likewise, gave the names of the gods to numbers and figures. For they called the equilateral triangle Minerva Coryphagenes (or 'born from the summit') and Tritogeneia because it is divided by three perpendicular lines drawn from the three angles. But they called the *one* Apollo, being led to this by the obvious meaning of the word Apollo [which signifies a lack of multitude] and by the simplicity of the monad†. They named the duad 'strife' and 'audacity,' and the triad 'justice.' For since inflicting injury and suffering injury are two extremes existing according to excess and deficiency, justice, through equality, holds a position in the middle. But what is called the tetractys,

<meta>This page continues the philosophical justification for Egyptian animal worship, moving from the mathematical symbolism of the Pythagoreans to the superiority of living beings over inanimate statues as reflections of the divine.</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>

animals, or of other things which are offered;

...being the number 36, was, as it is reported, their greatest oath, and was called the "world." For this number is formed from the combination of the first four even and the first four odd numbers, added together into one sum*. If, therefore, the most respected of the philosophers did not think it proper to neglect or despise any hidden meaning of a divine nature when they perceived it even in things which are inanimate and without a body, it seems to me that they, to an even greater degree, respected those traits depending on behavior which they saw in such natures as had senses, and were endowed with a soul, with feeling, and with moral habits. We must accept, therefore, not those who honor these things themselves, but those who revere divinity through them, as if through very clear mirrors, which are produced by nature in an appropriate manner; we should view them as the instruments or the handiwork of the God by whom all things are constantly beautified. But we ought to think that no inanimate being can be more excellent than one that is living, nor a senseless being more excellent than a feeling one, not even if someone should gather together all the gold and emeralds in the universe. For divinity is not produced in colors, or shapes, or smoothness; but such things as neither ever did, nor are naturally suited to participate in life, have a destiny more lowly than that of dead bodies. But the nature which lives and sees, and has the principle of motion from itself, and a knowledge of things appropriate and foreign to its being, has certainly derived an outflow and portion of that wisdom which, as Heraclitus says, considers how both itself and the universe is governed. Hence, the divinity is not worse represented in these animals than in works made of copper and stone, which in a similar way suffer corruption and decay, but are naturally deprived of all sense and consciousness. This, then, I consider the best defense that can be given for the worship of animals by the Egyptians.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of sacrifices, moving from the comparison of living beings and inanimate objects to the specific physical elements used in rituals and their relationship to divine causes.</meta>

<page-num>240</page-num>

...or a specific organ, such as the heart of a rooster <note>The rooster was sacred to Apollo, and therefore its heart was believed to be the instrument of divination in sacrifices. The chemist Olympiodorus says, “that the rooster obscurely signifies the essence of the sun and moon.” See, in the additional notes, what is said by Proclus concerning the rooster, in his treatise *On Magic*.</note>; or other things of a similar kind that are observed in nature, if they are considered to be the causes of the effectiveness of sacrifices. For by these things, the Gods are not shown to be supernatural causes, nor are they, as such, moved to action by sacrifices. Instead, they are considered as physical causes held by matter and physically involved in bodies, being activated or becoming still along with them, as these things also exist within nature. If, therefore, anything of this kind takes place in sacrifices, it acts as a co-cause, having the status of that without which a thing is not accomplished; and thus it is dependent on primary causes.

It is better, therefore, to assign as the cause of the effectiveness of sacrifices friendship and familiarity, and a relationship which binds creators to the things they have created, and parents to the—

things generated. Therefore, when this common principle precedes us and we take a certain animal, or anything that grows from the earth, which genuinely and purely preserves the will of its creator, then, through such a thing, we appropriately move the demiurgic cause that presides over it in an undefiled manner.

However, as these causes are many—some, like the daemonic causes, having a proximate arrangement, while others, as divine causes, are ranked above them, and further still, one most ancient and venerable cause acting as their leader—all these causes are moved together by a perfect sacrifice. Likewise, each thing is adapted to the sacrifice in a kindred manner, according to the order assigned to it.

But if any sacrifice is imperfect, it proceeds to a certain extent but is not capable of going any further. Consequently, many believe that sacrifices are to be offered to good daemons, many to the lowest powers of the Gods, and many to the worldly or earthly powers of daemons or Gods. Therefore, these things, being a part of sacrifices, are not falsely stated; however, they do not encompass the full power of sacrifice and all the benefits it contains, which extend to everything divine.

### CHAP. X.

WE, however, admit all these assertions: physical essences are indeed activated together as in a single animal, according to aptitude or sympathy; in another respect, they act as subjects, following and being subservient to the cause of the effectiveness of sacrifices. Meanwhile, <term>daemons</term>, and earthly or worldly divine powers, are primarily connected to our own order. Nevertheless, we must say that the most perfect and leading cause of the effectiveness of sacrifices is to be linked to <term>demiurgic</term> and the most perfect powers.

But since these powers encompass within themselves all the causes of sacrifice, we say that all its effective causes are activated together along with them. From all of these, a common benefit is granted to the whole of creation; sometimes through cities and peoples, or various nations, or regions more or less expansive than these. At other times, through households or an individual, these causes bestow good with an ungrudging and exuberant will, free from passion. They confer their benefits with an impassive intellect, according to suitability and alliance—a single friendship which simultaneously and cohesively contains all

<summary>Chapter X explains that while physical sympathy and lower spirits play a role in rituals, the true efficacy of sacrifice stems from the highest demiurgic powers. These powers provide universal benefits to nations and individuals alike through a passionless, divine friendship that connects all of creation.</summary>

<keywords>sacrifice, demiurgic powers, daemons, physical essences, sympathy, creation, impassive intellect, divine benevolence</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter X, following the discussion of the "single friendship" and "ineffable communion" that binds all aspects of creation through the power of sacrifices.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>
<sig>R 2</sig>

things, producing this bond through a certain ineffable communion.

For these assertions are much more true, and more characteristic of the essence and power of the Gods, than what you suspect to be the case—namely, "that the Gods are especially attracted by the vapors produced in animal sacrifices." For even if <term>daemons</term> are endowed with a certain body (which some think is nourished by sacrifices), that body is nonetheless immutable and impassive, <term>luciform</term>, and not in need; so that nothing flows out of it, nor does it require any external influx to be introduced. And if someone should admit that this influx exists, yet since the world and the air contained within it have a never-failing abundance of exhalations from earthly places, and since an outflow of this kind is equally diffused on all sides, what use can there be for sacrifices to <term>daemons</term>?

But neither do the inflows fill the place of the outflows so equally and proportionately that excess would ever predominate nor deficiency be produced, but rather so that there is a perfect equality and similarity in the bodies of <term>daemons</term>, remaining invariably the same. For the <term>Demiurge</term> of the universe has not provided such abundant and easily obtained nourishment for all the animals on the earth and in the sea, but has instead made

<summary>This page refutes the idea that Gods or daemons are attracted to the physical vapors of sacrifices, arguing that daemons possess immutable, light-filled bodies that do not require external nutrition. It suggests that even if such spirits required environmental exchange, the natural world already provides an abundance of earthly exhalations far beyond what rituals could offer.</summary>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter X, refuting the claim that daemons depend on human sacrifices for sustenance, and explores the hierarchical nature of causality and nourishment in the universe.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>

the beings superior to us to be in need of it; nor has he granted to other animals a natural abundance of what is daily required, while giving to <term>daemons</term> nourishment that is supplemental <note>adscititious: added from an external source rather than being innate</note> and obtained by us humans; so that if we, through laziness or some other excuse, should neglect an offering of this kind, the bodies of <term>daemons</term> would be in need of food and would share in a lack of proportion and disorder. Why, therefore, do not the authors of these claims overturn the whole order of things, so as to make us a better and more powerful class of beings? For if we supply <term>daemons</term> with nourishment, we shall much more be the causes of their existence. For everything receives nourishment and perfection from that by which it was generated. And this, indeed, may be seen in the visible generation of things; but it may also be observed in the heavens and the earth. For terrestrial things are nourished by celestial natures. But this becomes most clearly manifest in invisible causes. For the soul, indeed, is perfected by the intellect; but nature by the soul. And other things are nourished by their causes in a similar manner. If, therefore, it is impossible that we should be the primary causes of <term>daemons</term>, it is, for the same reason, impossible that we should be the causes of their nourishment.

<summary>The author argues that it is logically impossible for humans to provide essential nourishment to daemons, as this would falsely imply that humans are the creators and superior causes of these higher beings. He asserts that in the natural hierarchy, lower beings are always nourished and perfected by the higher causes that generated them, not the other way around.</summary>

<keywords>daemons, sacrifice, hierarchy of being, causality, nourishment, soul, intellect, celestial natures</keywords>

### CHAP. XI.

It also appears to me that the current question is mistaken in another respect. For it ignores the fact that offering sacrifices through fire has the power to consume and destroy their material form to a greater degree; that fire converts this matter into its own nature, but is not itself changed into the matter; and that it raises things toward divine, celestial, and immaterial fire, rather than descending toward matter and generation. For if the enjoyment of vapors from matter attracted <term>daemons</term>, it would be necessary for the matter to remain pure and whole, since there would then be a more abundant flow from it to those receiving it. But as it is, all the matter is ignited and consumed, and is changed into the purity and thinness of fire; this in itself clearly indicates the opposite of what you claim. For superior beings <note>that is, daemons</note> are unaffected by external influences <note>impassive</note>, and they take pleasure in removing matter through fire and making us unaffected as well. They likewise make whatever is within us like the Gods, in the same way that fire<note>*</note> makes all solid and resistant sub-

---

<note>* It is well observed by Ficinus, in book 1, Ennead 2 of Plotinus, "that the fire which we ignite is more similar to the heavens than other earthly substances,"</note>

<summary>The author explains that sacrificial fire does not feed daemons but instead destroys material substance, converting it into a refined, immaterial state. This process demonstrates that superior beings use fire to purify human nature and elevate it toward the divine rather than being dependent on material vapors.</summary>
<keywords>sacrifices, fire, matter, daemons, purification, impassive, Ficinus, Plotinus, divine elevation, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that daemons do not consume material sacrifices, shifting from the purifying nature of fire to the immaterial nature of the "vehicles" (spiritual bodies) that daemons inhabit.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>

stances to luminous and refined bodies. And they elevate us through sacrifices and the sacrificial fire to the fire of the Gods, in the same manner as fire elevates to fire, and draws upward heavy and resisting substances toward divine and celestial natures.

<note>Hence it partakes of light, which is something incorporeal; it is the most powerful of all things and is, as it were, vital; it is perpetually in motion; it divides all things without being itself divided; it absorbs all things into itself and avoids any foreign mixture; and lastly, when its fuel is consumed, it suddenly flies back again to the celestial fire, which is everywhere latent.</note>

---

### CHAP. XII.

For, in short, the vehicle* which serves <term>daemons</term> consists neither of matter, nor of the elements, nor of any other bodies known to us. What perfect supply of food, therefore, can there be from one essence to another <note>specifically different</note>? Or what enjoyment can arise from one foreign nature to another? There can be none. But much more, as the Gods, by the fire of lightning, divide matter and separate from it things which are essen-

<note>* For this vehicle is luciform, and consists of pure, immaterial, unburning, and life-giving fire. See the fifth book of my translation of Proclus on the Timæus.</note>

<summary>The text explains that the spiritual "vehicles" of daemons are made of an immaterial, luciform fire, making it impossible for them to consume or benefit from material food. It further illustrates how divine fire serves to separate essential, immaterial qualities from physical matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how divine fire separates immaterial essence from material substance during sacrifices, drawing parallels to the purification of the soul and the role of the "telestic art."</meta>

tially immaterial, but which are vanquished and bound by it, and render them impassive instead of being passive; thus also the fire among us, imitating the energy of divine fire, destroys everything which is material in sacrifices, purifies the things which are offered, liberates them from the bonds of matter, and renders them, through purity of nature, adapted to communion with the Gods. It likewise liberates * us in the same manner from the bonds of generation, makes us like the Gods, causes us to be adapted to their friendship, and conducts our material nature to an immaterial essence.

<note>* Proclus in **Tim. lib. v.** observes concerning the <term>telestic art</term>—or the art which operates through mystic ceremonies—“that, as the oracles teach, it obliterates through divine fire all the stains produced by generation.” Η τελεστικη δια του θειου πυρος αφανιζει τας εκ της γενεσεως απασας κηλιδας, ως τα λογια διδασκει. Hence another Chaldean oracle says, τῳ πυρι γαρ βροτος εμπελασας θεοθεν φαος εξει. That is, “The mortal who approaches fire will have a light from divinity.” Hercules, as we also learn from Proclus, was an example of this telestic purification. For he says, Ηρακλης δια τελεστικης καθηραμενος, και των αχραντων καρπων μετασχων, τελειας ετυχε εις τους θεους αποκαταστασεως, in **Plat. Polit.** p. 382. That is, “Hercules, being purified through the telestic art and participating in undefiled fruits, obtained a perfect restoration to the Gods.”</note>

<summary>This page describes how fire acts as a purifying agent in sacrifices, stripping away material bonds to allow for communion with the divine. It draws a parallel between this physical process and the "telestic art," which uses divine fire to purify the human soul and restore it to the Gods.</summary>

## CHAP. XIII.

Having overturned the common, absurd opinions regarding sacrifices in this manner, we will introduce true concepts about them in their place. We will omit a detailed discussion of every specific type of sacrifice, which a thorough and distinct study of the subject would require, because that belongs to a different investigation. Furthermore, any intelligent person should be able to accomplish this based on what has already been said; they can extend their reasoning from one point to many others and easily understand what has been omitted based on what was discussed. I believe these matters have been sufficiently explained, both in general and because the explanation appropriately respects the purity of the Gods. However, because these ideas might seem unbelievable or unclear to others, and their truth might be doubted because they do not immediately stimulate the active power of reason, I wish to examine these matters a bit more fully. If possible, I will add arguments even more evident than those already presented.

---

<note>Proclus, in his commentary on the *Timaeus*, book 5, observes regarding the <term>telestic art</term>—the art that works through mystic ceremonies—that "as the oracles teach, it wipes away all the stains produced by the process of generation through divine fire." Η τελεστικη δια του θειου πυρος αφανιζει τας εκ της γενεσεως απασας κηλιδας, ως τα λογια διδασκει. Therefore, another Chaldean oracle says, τῳ πυρι γαρ βροτος εμπελασας θεοθεν φαος εξει. This means, "The mortal who approaches fire will receive light from the divine." Hercules, as we also learn from Proclus, was an example of this telestic purification. He says, Ηρακλης δια τελεστικης καθηραμενος, και των αχραντων καρπων μετασχων, τελειας ετυχε εις τους θεους αποκαταστασεως, in his commentary on Plato’s *Republic*, p. 382. This means, "Hercules, having been purified through the telestic art and sharing in undefiled fruits, achieved a perfect restoration to the Gods."</note>

### CHAP. XIV.

We will begin clarifying this subject in the best way possible by demonstrating that the sacred law of sacrifices is connected with the order of the Gods. First, therefore, we say that some of the Gods are material, while others are immaterial. The material Gods are those who contain matter within themselves and organize it; the immaterial are those who are perfectly exempt from and transcend matter. According to the <term>sacrificial art</term>, it is necessary to begin sacred operations with the material Gods, for the ascent to the immaterial Gods will not otherwise be achieved. The material Gods, therefore, have a certain communion with matter insofar as they preside over it. Hence, they have dominion over things that occur within matter, such as the division, percussion, repercussion, change, generation, and decay of all material bodies. Therefore, anyone who wishes to worship these <term>theurgically</term>, in a manner adapted to them and to the dominion assigned to them, should use a material mode of worship, since they are material. For in this way, we shall be entirely led to a familiarity with them and worship them in an allied and

<summary>This chapter explains that sacrifices are governed by the divine hierarchy, distinguishing between material and immaterial Gods. It argues that theurgic worship must begin with material rituals to the material Gods to enable a successful ascent to higher, immaterial deities.</summary>
<keywords>material Gods, immaterial Gods, sacrificial art, theurgy, matter, divine order, theurgical worship</keywords>

--- Page 278 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the Gods and matter, specifically why material sacrifices are appropriate for material Gods, and begins a new chapter on the dual nature of human existence as both soul and body.</meta>

appropriate manner. Dead bodies, therefore, and things deprived of life, the slaughter of animals, and the consumption of sacrificial victims, and, in short, the change of the matter which is offered, pertain to these Gods—not because of the Gods themselves, but because of the matter over which they preside. For although they are separate from matter to the highest degree, they are simultaneously present with it. And although they contain matter within an immaterial power, they still coexist with it. Also, the things that are governed are not foreign to their governors; and the things that serve as instruments are not unsuited to those who use them. Therefore, it is foreign to the immaterial Gods to offer matter to them through sacrifices, but this is most appropriate for all the material Gods.

---

### CHAP. XV.

Let us then, in the next place, direct our attention to that which agrees with what has been said before, and with our twofold condition of being. For there is a time when we become entirely soul, are outside of the body, and revolve sublimely on high in conjunction with all the immaterial Gods. And there is also a

It also introduces the concept of the human "twofold condition," where the self can exist either as a pure soul among the immaterial Gods or as a being joined to a body.</summary>
<keywords>material Gods, immaterial Gods, sacrifice, matter, divine governance, soul, twofold condition of being, Iamblichus, theurgy</keywords>

time when we are bound in the shell-like body, are held by matter, and are of a physically-formed nature. Again, therefore, there will be two modes of worship. For one mode will be simple, non-physical, and pure from all physical creation, and this mode belongs to pure souls. But the other is filled with bodies and everything of a material nature, and is adapted to souls which are neither pure nor liberated from all physical creation. We must admit, therefore, that there are two species of sacrifices; one kind, indeed, belonging to men who are entirely purified—which, as Heraclitus says, rarely happens to one man, or to a few of mankind who can be easily counted—but the other kind, being material and physically-formed, and consisting of change, is adapted to souls that are still held by the body. Hence, for cities and people not yet liberated from <term>genesiurgic</term> fate and the hindering association with bodies, if such a mode of sacrifice as this latter one is not permitted, they will stray from both immaterial and material good. For they will not be able to receive the former, and they will not offer what is appropriate to the latter. At the same time, likewise, everyone who sacrifices performs the sacrifice with reference to what he is, and not with reference to what he is not. It is not

<summary>This page describes a dual system of worship and sacrifice: a spiritual, non-physical mode for purified souls and a material, physical mode for those still bound to the body. <keywords>worship, sacrifice, pure souls, material nature, Heraclitus, genesiurgic fate, physical body, Neoplatonism, theurgy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dual nature of worship, moving from the philosophical justification of material sacrifice to its practical application for the health and maintenance of the physical body.</meta>

It is proper, therefore, that the sacrifice should transcend the proper measure of the one by whom it is offered. I will also say the same thing concerning the connection which appropriately aligns the men who worship and the powers that are worshiped. For this connection requires that a mode of worship should be chosen that is adapted to itself; *namely*, an immaterial connection requires a mode of worship immaterially blended, purely uniting incorporeal natures to themselves through pure incorporeal powers; but a physically-formed connection requires a physically-formed mode which depends on bodies and is blended with the essences that preside over bodies.

---

### CHAP. XVI.

Furthermore, we must not neglect to add what follows: that we frequently perform certain acts for the Gods who are the watchful guardians of the body, and for good demons, for the sake of the body’s necessary needs. This occurs, for instance, when <insert>by sacrifices</insert> we purify it from ancient stains, or free it from diseases and fill it with health, or remove from it heaviness and sluggishness, or procure for it any other good. In this case, therefore, we evidently

<summary>The text argues that the mode of worship must match the nature of the connection—whether immaterial or physical—between the worshiper and the divine. It also notes that material sacrifices are necessary for the purification and health of the physical body under the care of guardian deities and demons.</summary>
<keywords>worship, sacrifice, incorporeal, physical body, Gods, demons, purification, health, Neoplatonism, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that because the body is physical, it requires physical rituals for its purification and maintenance, rather than purely intellectual ones.</meta>

must not occupy ourselves with the body in an intellectual and incorporeal manner. For the body is not suited to participate in modes of this kind; instead, by obtaining things that are related to itself, it is improved and purified by bodies. The rites of sacrifices, therefore, will necessarily—for a purpose of this nature—be physically-formed; partly removing what is excessive in us; partly supplying us with that which we lack; and partly bringing into balance and order those things in us that are excessively disturbed. We also frequently engage in sacred operations, entreating higher beings to grant us such things as are suited to the needs of human life. And these are the types of things that preserve the body in health, or pertain to those things which we obtain for the sake of the body.

---

### CHAP. XVII.

What, therefore, shall we receive from the Gods who are entirely exempt from all human generation, with respect to barrenness, or abundance, or anything else pertaining to <note>the mortal</note> life? Nothing at all. For it is not the role of those who are liberated from all things to interfere with gifts of this kind.

<summary>The author explains that physical sacrifices are necessary to purify and balance the human body because the body cannot respond to purely intellectual worship. It further notes that the highest, most transcendent Gods do not involve themselves in the material needs or reproductive concerns of mortal life.</summary>
<keywords>sacrifice, physical body, purification, higher beings, divine providence, mortal life, Neoplatonism, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of physical sacrifices, distinguishing between the roles of immaterial, transcendent Gods and those who oversee material concerns and the human race.</meta>

But if someone should say that the perfectly immaterial Gods include within themselves the material Gods, and that through this they also contain their gifts according to one first cause, such a person would also say that, as a consequence of this, an abundance of divine gifts descends from the immaterial Gods. However, it must not be allowed for anyone to say that the immaterial Gods bestow these gifts by directly interfering with the actions of human life. For such a management of our affairs is divisible, is accomplished with a certain turning <note>toward the subjects of its care</note>, is not entirely separate from bodies, and is incapable of maintaining a pure and undefiled authority. 

Would not, therefore, that mode of sacrifice in works of this kind be most appropriate which is mixed with bodies and adheres to the realm of physical birth, rather than that which is entirely immaterial and incorporeal? For the pure mode of sacrifice is perfectly transcendent and incomparable <note>with our concerns</note>. But the mode which employs bodies, and the powers that exist through bodies, is most closely allied to human affairs. It is also capable of producing a certain prosperous condition of things, and of giving balance and harmony to the mortal race.

<summary>The author argues that while immaterial Gods are the ultimate source of divine gifts, they do not directly manage human affairs; instead, physical sacrifices are necessary for human concerns because they are compatible with the material realm. Such rituals are uniquely suited to bringing balance and prosperity to the mortal race.</summary>
<keywords>immaterial Gods, material Gods, sacrifice, human affairs, generation, divine providence, harmony, Neoplatonism</keywords>

### CHAP. XVIII.

According to another division, therefore, the numerous herd <note>or the great mass</note> of men is arranged under nature, is governed by physical powers, looks downward to the works of nature, and carries out the administration of <term>Fate</term> and things pertaining to <term>Fate</term>, because it belongs to that order. This group always employs practical reasoning only about such specific things as exist according to nature. But there are a certain few who, by employing a supernatural power of <term>intellect</term>, are indeed removed from nature and are led to a separate and unmingled <term>intellect</term>; and these, at the same time, become superior to physical powers. Others again, who are the <term>media</term> between these, incline toward things that exist between nature and a pure <term>intellect</term>. And of these, some indeed equally follow both nature and an immaculate <term>intellect</term>; others embrace a life which is mingled from both; and others are liberated from subordinate things and betake themselves to those which are more excellent.

This division, therefore, having been made, what follows will take place most clearly. For those who are governed by the nature of

<summary>The author divides humanity into three categories: the majority who are governed by nature and Fate, a select few who transcend nature through pure intellect, and a middle group that exists between these two states. For this purpose, they should choose appropriate places, air, matter, the powers of matter, bodies, and the habits of bodies, qualities, and proper motions, the changes of things in generation, and other things connected with these, both in other parts of piety and in 그that part of it which pertains to sacrifice. But those who live according to intellect alone, and to the life of intellect, and are liberated from the bonds of nature, should exercise the intellectual and incorporeal mode of worship in all parts of <term>theurgy</term>. And those who are the <term>media</term> <note>intermediaries</note> between these should work differently in the paths of piety, according to the differences of this middle condition of life—either by embracing both modes of piety, or separating themselves from one of the modes <note>and adhering to the other</note>, or receiving both these modes as the foundation for things of a more honorable nature. For without these, they can never arrive at things that are supereminent. Or, in some other way, they should thus work in the paths of sanctity in a fitting manner.

## CHAP. XIX.

ON this subject, however, there is also the following division. Of divine essences and powers, some have a <term>genesiurgic</term> <note>generative</note> soul and nature that is subject to and serves their creations whenever they wish to use them. But others are entirely separate from soul and nature—I mean from a divine soul and nature, and not only from a mundane and generative soul and nature*. And others are the <term>media</term>† <note>intermediaries</note> between these, and provide the extremes a communion with each other, either according to an abundant participation in a greater good, or according to an unimpeded reception of a lesser good, or according to a harmony which binds both extremes together. When, therefore, we worship the Gods who

<note>† These intermediaries consist of the order of Gods called <term>ἀρχαὶ</term> <note>archai</note>, or *rulers*, and of those called <term>ἀπόλυτοι</term> <note>apolytoi</note>, or *liberated*; the former are also called *supermundane*, and the latter *supercelestial*, because they exist immediately above the celestial Gods. Hence, intellectual gifts are suited to these, and things which pertain to an incorporeal life, together with the fruits of virtue and wisdom, and whatever perfect and total goods of the soul there may be. ## CHAPTER XXI.

I THINK, therefore, that all who are lovers of
the contemplation of theurgic truth will acknow-
ledge this: that the piety which pertains to
divine natures ought not to be exercised to-
wards them partially or imperfectly. Hence,
since prior to the appearance of the Gods, all
such powers as are subordinate <note>presubjacent</note> to them are
moved, and when the Gods are about to de-
scend to the earth, precede them as in a solemn
procession*; he who does not distribute to all
these powers that which is adapted to them,

* <note>Proclus on the First Alcibiades observes "that around every God there is an innumerable multitude of demons <note>dæmons</note>, who have the same names as their leaders. And that these are delighted when they are called by the names of Apollo or Jupiter, because they express in themselves the characteristic peculiarity of their leading Gods." In the same admirable commentary, also, he says "that in the most holy of the mysteries <note>that is, in the Eleusinian mysteries</note>, before the appearance of divinity, the incursions of certain terrestrial demons present themselves to view, alluring the souls of the spectators from undefiled good to matter."</note>

<summary>This page argues that religious piety toward the Gods must be complete and inclusive of the subordinate powers that precede them in ritual processions. It cites Proclus to explain that these subordinate demons share the names of their leading Gods and may appear during mysteries to test the souls of participants.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, piety, Proclus, demons, Eleusinian mysteries, Apollo, Jupiter, divine procession</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the necessity of a complete and comprehensive approach to divine worship, specifically addressing the theurgist's role in managing multiple divine powers to achieve full communion with the Gods.</meta>

and does not honor each in an appropriate manner, will depart imperfect and deprived of participation in the Gods. But he who propitiates all of them, and offers to each one acceptable gifts—those which are, to the utmost of his power, adapted to them—will always remain secure and blameless, properly completing the perfect and entire receptacle of the divine choir. Since this, therefore, is the case, is it necessary that the mode of sanctity be simple and consist of only a few things, or should it be multiform, all-harmonious, and composed, as I might say, of everything contained in the world? If, indeed, the power which is invoked and excited in the performance of sacred rites were simple, the mode of sacrifice would necessarily be simple. But if the multitude of powers which are excited when the Gods descend and are moved cannot be understood by anyone except theurgists alone—who accurately know this through experience in sacred operations—if this is the case, they alone are capable of knowing what the perfection of the sacrificial art is. They also know that the omission of even a few things subverts the whole work of religion, just as in a musical harmony, from the snapping of one

### CHAP. XXII.

### CHAP. XXIV.

The same principles may also be learned from the distribution of the Gods according to their locations; and from this, and the divisible dominion over each particular thing, it may be seen how many allotments—greater or less—superior beings are assigned according to their different orders. For it is evident that for the Gods who preside over certain places, the things produced by them are most appropriately offered in sacrifice; and that what pertains to the governed is most suitable for sacrifice to the governors. For their own works are always particularly pleasing to creators; and to those who primarily produce certain things, such things are primarily acceptable. Whether, therefore, certain animals, plants, or any other productions of the earth are governed by superior beings, they simultaneously participate in their watchful care and grant to us an indivisible communion with the Gods. Therefore, some things of this kind, if they are carefully preserved, increase the familiarity of those who keep them with the Gods; and these are such things that, by remaining whole, preserve the communion between Gods and men. Of this kind are some of the animals in Egypt, and man,

For the more perfect this alliance is, the more perfect always is the good which is imparted by it.

---
## CHAP. XXV.
---

If, therefore, these things were human customs alone, and derived their authority through our legal institutions, it might be said that the worship of the Gods was the invention of our own conceptions. Now, however, divinity is the leader of it, who is thus invoked by sacrifices, and who is surrounded by a numerous multitude of Gods and angels. Under him, likewise, a certain common presiding power is allotted dominion according to each nation of the earth. And a peculiar presiding power is allotted to each temple. Of the sacrifices, also, which are performed to the Gods, the overseeing guardian is a God; but an angel oversees those which are performed to angels; and a <term>daemon</term> oversees those which are performed to <term>daemons</term>. In the same manner, also, in other sacred

<summary>This text argues that consecrated objects facilitate a sacred alliance with superior causes and asserts that religious worship is divinely ordained, led by divinity and overseen by a hierarchy of Gods, angels, and daemons.</summary>

<keywords>consecration, divinity, sacrifices, Gods, angels, daemons, presiding powers, divine hierarchy, religious institutions</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XXV regarding the divine hierarchy's role in rituals and begins Chapter XXVI, which explores the essential role of prayer within sacrifices.</meta>

operations, the presiding power is assigned dominion over each in a way related to his specific genus. Therefore, when we offer sacrifices to the Gods, accompanied by the presiding Gods who bring sacred operations to completion, it is necessary at the same time to venerate the sacred law of divine sanctity in those sacrifices. At the same time, we should also be confident, as we are sacrificing under the Gods who are the rulers of such works. We ought, likewise, to be very cautious, lest we should offer any gift unworthy of, or foreign to, the Gods. And, as a final admonition, we should pay attention in an entirely perfect manner to all that surrounds us, and to the Gods, angels, and <term>daemons</term> that are distributed according to their genera in the universe. To all these, in a similar manner, an acceptable sacrifice should be offered; for only in this way can sanctity be preserved in a way worthy of the Gods who preside over it.

---

Since, however, prayers are not the smallest <note>but on the contrary, a very great</note> part of sacrifices, and especially since they bring them to completion, and through them their whole operation is strengthened and carried out; and since, besides

For this is, in itself, a subject worthy of being known, and it makes the science concerning the Gods more perfect. I say, therefore, that the *first kind* of prayer is *collective*; it is also the leader toward contact with, and a knowledge of, divinity. The *second kind* is *the bond of harmonious communion*, which calls forth—prior to the energy of speech—the gifts granted by the Gods, and perfects the whole of our operations prior to our intellectual conceptions. And the *third* and *most perfect kind* of prayer is *the seal of inexpressible union with the divinities*, in whom it establishes all the power and authority of prayer; and thus it causes the soul to rest in the Gods, as if in an unfailing harbor. But from these three terms, in which all the divine measures are contained, humble adoration not only secures for us the friendship of the Gods, but also extends three fruits to us from above, being, as it were, three Hesperian apples of gold* <note>* This detail regarding the apples of gold is added from the version by Scutellius, who appears to have translated this work from a more complete manuscript than the one used by Gale.</note>. # SECTION VI.

---

## CHAP. I.

It is now, however, time for me to move on to the next doubt which you propose, namely, “ *Why is it necessary that the inspector [who presides over sacred rites] should not touch a dead body, even though most sacred operations are performed through dead bodies?* ” Therefore, once again, so that we may resolve this doubt, we shall direct our attention to this apparent contradiction; for in reality there is none, but these things only seem to exist in opposition. For if the laws of sacred rites ordered that the same dead bodies should both not be touched and be touched, this would be a contradiction in itself. But if they command that some dead bodies should be avoided as impure, while others which are consecrated should be touched, this does not involve any contradiction. Furthermore, it is not lawful to touch human bodies once the soul has left them, since a certain vestige, image, or representation of divine

<summary>The author begins addressing the question of why those presiding over sacred rites must avoid touching dead bodies. He argues that there is no contradiction in avoiding impure corpses while using consecrated ones in specific religious operations.</summary>
<keywords>sacred rites, inspector, dead bodies, purity, soul, ritual law, consecration</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter I regarding the touching of dead bodies in sacred rites and begins Chapter II, which explains why dead bodies affect humans but not incorporeal beings like daemons.</meta>

life is extinguished in the body by death. But it is no longer unholy to touch other dead bodies, because they did not <note>when living</note> participate in a more divine life. To other Gods, therefore, who are pure from matter, our not touching dead bodies is appropriate; but to those Gods who preside over animals, and are closely connected with them, invocation by means of animals is properly performed. According to this, therefore, no contradiction occurs.

---

## CHAP. II.

This doubt may also be resolved in another way. For in humans, indeed, who are confined in matter, bodies deprived of life produce a certain stain; because that which is not alive introduces a certain defilement into that which is living, in the same way as the impure does to the pure, and as privation does to a <term>habit</term>; and also because that which is dead produces a certain pollution through a physical tendency toward a worse condition, as a consequence of having possessed the capacity to die. But a dead body cannot produce any defilement in a daemon who is perfectly incorporeal and does not undergo any corruption. For it is necessary that

<summary>The author explains that while human corpses are avoided due to their former connection to divine life, touching animal bodies in ritual is acceptable because they lacked that same divinity. He also argues that dead bodies only defile material humans, not incorporeal daemons.</summary>
<keywords>divine life, Gods, matter, daemon, incorporeal, defilement, ritual purity, habit, privation</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding why incorporeal beings are not defiled by dead bodies and begins Chapter III, which explores the role of sacred animals in divination.</meta>

he should transcend a corruptible body and not participate in any representation of corruption from it. And this is enough in answer to the contradiction of the doubt.

---

### CHAP. III.

Next, we shall explain how divination is performed through sacred animals, such as hawks. We must never say, therefore, that the Gods approach through bodies that are used in this way. For they do not preside over animals either partially, or directly, or materially, or with a certain disposition toward them. But such contact with the organs of divination must be attributed to daemons—specifically those who are highly divided, where different animals are assigned to different orders of them. These daemons directly exercise a government of this kind and do not possess their own dominion in a way that is perfectly independent and immaterial. Or, if someone is willing to admit it, a seat must be attributed to them through which we may be able to associate with and use them. It is necessary, therefore, to think that this seat should be free from bodies. For there can be no communion whatever between the pure

<summary>The author concludes that daemons are not defiled by physical corruption and argues that divination through sacred animals involves lower, material daemons rather than the Gods.</summary>
<keywords>divination, sacred animals, hawks, Gods, daemons, materiality, incorporeal, dominion, ritual purity</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the seat of divination connects human and daemonic realms through the souls of animals, and begins Chapter IV regarding the soul's active role in this process.</meta>

and its opposite; but it is reasonable to admit that this seat is joined with men through the souls of animals. For this soul has a certain alliance with men through a homogeneity of life; but it is allied to daemons because, being liberated from the body, it has in a certain respect a separate existence. Hence, being a medium between both, it is subservient to its presiding daemon, but it announces to those who are still detained in the body that which its prefect commands. And it imparts to both of these a common bond with each other.

### CHAP. IV.

However, it is necessary to think that the soul which uses this kind of divination not only becomes a listener to the prediction, but also contributes in no small degree from itself to its fulfillment and to what pertains to its operations. For this soul is co-excited and cooperates, and at the same time foreknows, through a certain necessary sympathy. Therefore, such a mode of divination as this is entirely different from the divine and true mode, being only able to predict regarding small and daily concerns—namely, regarding those that, being placed in a divided nature, are carried

<summary>The author explains that the souls of animals act as a bridge between humans and daemons due to their intermediate nature. He also notes that in lower forms of divination, the soul actively participates and sympathizes with the predictions, which are limited to minor, daily matters.</summary>
<keywords>animal soul, daemons, divination, homogeneity of life, sympathy, divided nature, prediction, spiritual hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page concludes the critique of lower, material forms of divination and begins Chapter V, addressing the "violent threats" used in certain ritualistic practices.</meta>

along with generation, and which impart motions from themselves to those things that are able to receive them, and produce various passive states in things which are naturally adapted to be affected in return. Perfect foreknowledge, however, can never be achieved through such passive states. For that which is itself especially unchangeable, immaterial, and entirely pure is accustomed to understand the future; but that which is mixed with the most irrational and dark nature of a physically-formed and material essence is filled with abundant ignorance. An artificial system, therefore, of this kind does not deserve to be called divination; nor is it proper to give much attention to it, nor to believe anyone else who uses it, as if it possessed in itself a certain clear and known indication of truth. And so much for divination of this kind.

---

## CHAP. V.

Let us, therefore, now discuss another type of doubt, the cause of which is hidden, and which, as you say, is accompanied by "*violent threats*." But there are various divisions regarding the many kinds of threats. "*For it threatens either to burst the heavens, or to reveal the*"

Therefore, by the "secret in the sanctuary" (*arcanum* in the *adytum*), he means the deity who exists at the furthest limit of the intelligible order (that is, *Phanes*). Of this deity, it is said in the *Chaldean Oracles* that "he remains in the paternal depth, and in the sanctuary, near to the god-nourished silence."</note>, or to stop the ship *Baris*, or to scatter the members of *Osiris* to *Typhon*, or to do something else of that kind.”

Men do not, however, as you think, threaten the sun, the moon, or any of the celestial Gods with such words; for if they did, more terrible absurdities would follow than those you complain about. But, as we observed before, there is a certain class of powers in the world that is divisible, thoughtless, and highly irrational. It receives reason from another source and is obedient to it; it neither uses its own intelligence nor distinguishes between truth and falsehood, or what is possible or impossible. Therefore, when threats are made, a class of this kind is immediately excited and astonished because, it seems to me, it is naturally suited to be led by appearances and to attract other things through a shocked and unstable imagination.

<summary>The author explains that the ritual threats used in theurgy are not directed at the celestial Gods, but at a lower class of irrational spirits that are easily influenced by imagination and appearances. ## CHAP. VI.

THESE things also allow for another explanation of the following kind. The theurgist, through the power of arcane signatures, commands mundane natures, no longer as a man, nor as one employing a human soul; but as someone existing superior to them in the order of the Gods, he makes use of greater mandates than those that pertain to himself, insofar as he is human. This, however, does not take place as if he actually performed everything which he vehemently threatens to accomplish; rather, he teaches us by such a use of words the magnitude and quality of the power which he possesses through a union with the Gods, and which he obtains from the knowledge of arcane symbols. This, likewise, may be said: that the demons who are distributed according to parts, and who guard the parts of the universe, pay so much attention to the parts over which they preside that they cannot endure a word contrary <note>to the safety of these</note>, but they preserve the permanency of mundane natures as immutable. They preserve this permanency, therefore, in an unchanged condition because the order of the Gods remains invariably the same. Hence, they cannot endure even to hear threats against the very things in which the aerial and terrestrial demons have their existence.

<summary>The author explains that a theurgist commands lower spirits by acting from a divine rank rather than a human one, using arcane symbols to demonstrate their power. These spirits, who protect specific parts of the universe, are compelled to obey because they cannot tolerate threats to the cosmic order they are assigned to maintain.</summary>

<keywords>theurgist, arcane signatures, mundane natures, demons, divine order, cosmic stability, ritual threats, aerial demons, terrestrial demons</keywords>

## CHAP. VII.

OR this matter may also be explained as follows: Daemons preside with a protective power over arcane mysteries, and they do so to such a remarkable degree because the orderly distribution of things in the universe is primarily maintained by daemons. For the parts of the universe remain in order because the beneficial power of **Osiris** continues to be sacred and pure, and is not mixed with any opposing error or disturbance. The life of all things likewise remains pure and incorruptible because the hidden life-giving beauties of the productive principles in **Isis** do not descend into the body which is transient*, and which is visible. But all things continue immovable and eternal because the course of the sun is never stopped. And all things remain perfect and whole because the secrets in the *adytum*† are never disclosed. Hence, in those details where the safety of all things resides—I mean in the secrets being always preserved hidden, and in the inexpressible essence of the Gods never experiencing an opposite state—terrestrial daemons cannot endure, even

## SECTION VII.

### CHAP. I.

**THE** doubts that follow also require the assistance of the same divinely wise Muse for their solution. But before this, I desire to explain to you the unique character of the theology of the Egyptians. For they, imitating the nature of the universe and the creative energy of the Gods, display certain images through symbols of mystical, hidden, and invisible intellectual perceptions; just as nature, in a certain way, expresses invisible principles [or productive powers] through visible forms. But the creative energy of the Gods outlines the truth of forms through visible images. Therefore, the Egyptians—perceiving that all superior beings delight in the likeness that inferior beings have to them, and wishing to fill the latter with goodness through the greatest possible imitation of the former—quite appropriately present a method of theology adapted to the mystical doctrine hidden within the symbols.

<vocab>Egyptians, theology, creative energy, Gods, symbols, mystic, intellectual perceptions, theologizing</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that Egyptian theology uses symbols to represent divine and invisible truths, imitating how nature uses visible forms to express invisible principles. This method is based on the idea that superior beings delight in the likeness that inferior beings have to them.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian theology, symbolism, creative energy, divine imitation, mystical doctrine, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues Section VII, detailing the specific symbolic meaning of "mire" (mud or slime) in Egyptian theology and defining the nature of the God who transcends the material world.</meta>

## CHAP. II.

HEAR, therefore, the intellectual interpretation of symbols according to the conceptions of the Egyptians; at the same time, remove from your imagination and your ears the image of symbolical things, and elevate yourself to intellectual truth. By “mire,” therefore, understand everything that is corporeal and material; or that which is nutritive and prolific; or the material species of nature which is carried along in conjunction with the unstable flux of matter; or a thing of the kind that the river of generation receives and which subsides along with it; or the primordial cause of the elements and of all the powers distributed among the elements, which must be previously conceived to exist as a foundation. Being, therefore, of this kind, the God who is the cause of generation, of all nature, and of all the powers in the elements—as he transcends these and is immaterial, incorporeal, and supernatural, unbegotten and indivisible <note>impartible</note>, wholly derived from himself, and concealed within himself—this God precedes all things and comprehends all things in himself. And because, indeed, he comprehends all things and imparts himself to all

<summary>The author explains the Egyptian symbolic meaning of "mire" as representing the material world and the flux of nature, contrasting it with God, who is the immaterial and self-derived cause of all things.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian symbols, mire, materiality, generation, divine transcendence, immateriality, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Egyptian God as an immaterial and transcendent cause, moving from his relationship with material "mire" to the symbolic meanings of the lotus and the solar ship.</meta>

mundane natures, he is revealed into light from these. However, because he transcends all things and is expanded above them by himself, for this reason he presents himself to the view as separate, exempt, elevated, and expanded by himself above the powers and elements in the world. 

The following symbol also testifies to the truth of this. For by the God “*sitting above the lotus,*” a transcendence and strength which in no way come into contact with the mire are obscurely signified; it also indicates his intellectual and empyrean empire. For everything belonging to the lotus is seen to be circular—namely, both the form of the leaves and the fruit—and circulation alone is allied to the motion of the intellect, which acts with invariable sameness, in one order, and according to one reason. 

But the God is established by himself, above a dominion and energy of this kind, venerable and holy, superexpanded, and abiding in himself, which his being seated is intended to signify. 

When the God is also represented as “*sailing in a ship*” <note>In accordance with this, Martianus Capella also, in book 2 of *The Marriage of Philology and Mercury*, etc., speaking of the sun, says, “There he perceives a certain ship, governing the courses of all nature with diverse desire, completely full of an accumulation of flames, and surrounded by blessed merchandise. To which...”</note>, it shows us the power which

And as the pilot directs everything from the stern, giving from himself a small principle of motion to the vessel, so also, by a much greater priority, the God indivisibly imparts from above—starting from the first principles of nature—the primordial causes of motion. These details, therefore, and even more than these, are indicated by the symbol of the God sailing in a ship.

## CHAP. III.

SINCE, however, every part of the heavens, every sign of the zodiac <note>In the original <term>pan zodion</term>, which Gale erroneously translates as *all animals*.</note>, all the motion of the heavens, every period of time according to which the world is moved, and all things contained in the totalities of the universe, receive the powers which descend from the sun—some of which are interwoven with these totalities, while others transcend any mixture with them—the symbolic mode of representation depicts these also, indicating “ *that the sun is diversified according to the signs of the zodiac, and that every hour he changes his form.* ” At the same time, it also indicates his immutable, stable, never-failing, and simultaneously unified communication of good to the whole world. But since the recipients of the indivisible gift of the God are variously affected by it, and receive many forms of power from the sun according to their own specific motions, the symbolic doctrine demonstrates through the multitude of the gifts that the God is one, and it reveals his single power through these many forms of power. Hence, likewise, it says that he is one and the

<summary>This chapter explains how the sun's singular, unchanging power is represented symbolically through the diverse signs of the zodiac and the changing hours, reflecting how one divine source provides varied gifts to different recipients.</summary>
<keywords>zodiac, solar symbolism, divine unity, multiform powers, celestial motion, Iamblichus, Thomas Gale, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 317 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on Egyptian solar symbolism and transitions to Chapter IV, which begins an inquiry into the nature and power of non-meaningful or "barbarous" names used in theurgy.</meta>

same, but that the changes of his form and his configurations must be admitted to exist in the recipients. On this account, it asserts "that he is changed every hour, according to the signs of the zodiac," in consequence of these being variously changed around the God, according to the many modes by which they receive him. The Egyptians use prayers to the sun that conform to these assertions, not only in visions seen by physical eyes, but also in their more common supplications; all of these have a meaning such as this and are offered to the God in accordance with a symbolic and mystic doctrine of this kind. Hence, it would not be reasonable for anyone to undertake a defense of them.

> “As with statues established by the <term>telestic art</term>, some things pertaining to them are manifest, but others are inwardly concealed, being symbolic of the presence of the Gods, and which are only known to the mystic artists themselves; after the same manner, the world being a statue of the <term>intelligible</term>, and perfected by the father, has indeed some things which are visible indications of its divinity; but others, which are the invisible impressions of the participation of being received by it from the father, who gave it perfection, in order that through these it may be eternally rooted in real being. Heaven, indeed, and the world are names significant of the powers in the universe; the latter, so far as it proceeds from the <term>intelligible</term>; but the former, so far as it is converted to it. It is, however, necessary to know that *the divine name* of its abiding power, and which is a symbol of the impression of the <term>Demiurgus</term>, according to which it does not proceed out of being, is ineffable and secret, and known only to the Gods themselves."

symbolical character of divine likeness must be admitted to have an existence in names. And, moreover, even if it should be unknown to us, yet this very circumstance is what is most venerable in it, for it is too excellent to be analyzed by human knowledge. But in those

thus also the names of the one are different from those of the other; since divine names reveal the whole essence of the things named, but those of men only partially come into contact with them. Plato, therefore, knowing that this existed previously in the world, omits the divine and ineffable name itself, which is different from the visible name, and with the greatest caution introduces it as a symbol of the divine impression which the world contains. For the words, "*or whatever other name*" and "*it may receive*," are a hidden hymn to the name of the world, as something ineffable and granted a divine essence, so that it may correspond to what is signified by it. Hence, also, divine names for the world are provided by Theurgists; some of which are called ineffable by them, while others are speakable; some being significant of the invisible powers in the world, while others signify the visible elements from which it derives its completion. Through these causes, therefore, acting as hypotheses, the form of the world, the demiurgic cause and model, and the visible and invisible names of the world are presented. And the former name, indeed, is twofold (dyadic), but the latter is single (monadic). For the words "whatever other" signify oneness. You may also consider the ineffable name of the universe as signifying its remaining in the father; but the name *world*, as indicating its progression; and *heaven* of its conversion. But through the three, you have the final cause, on account of which it is full of good; remaining ineffably, proceeding perfectly, and converting itself to the good as the primary object of desire.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine names, transitioning from the idea of the world's ineffable name to the specific use of foreign (Barbaric) names in sacred rites, explaining their superior connection to the divine.</meta>

names which we can scientifically analyze\*, we possess a knowledge of the whole divine essence, power, and order, contained within the name. And further still, we collectively preserve in the soul the mystic and secret image of the Gods; through this, we elevate the soul to the Gods and, once elevated, join it as closely as possible with them. But you ask, “Why, among meaningful names, do we prefer those that are *Barbaric to our own?*” There is also a mystical reason for this. Because the Gods have shown that the entire language of sacred nations, such as those of the Egyptians and Assyrians, is suited to sacred matters, we should therefore consider it necessary that our communication with the Gods be in a language allied to them. This is also because such a mode of speech is the first and most ancient. And especially because those who first learned the names of the Gods, having combined them with their own native tongue, passed them down to us so that we might always keep the sacred law of tradition unchanged, in a language unique and suited to them.

## CHAPTER V.

You object, however, “*that he who hears words looks to their meaning, so that it is enough if the concept remains the same, regardless of which words are used.*” But the matter is not as you suspect it to be. For if names existed by mere agreement\* it would be of no consequence whether some were used instead of others. But if they are derived from the nature of things, those names which are better suited to that nature will also be more precious to the Gods. From this, therefore, it is clear that the language of sacred nations is quite reasonably preferred to that of other men. To this may be added that names do not entirely preserve the same meaning when translated into another language; instead, there are certain unique expressions

---

<note>\* See the additional notes at the end of volume 5 of my translation of *Plato*, and also the notes to my translation of Aristotle’s *On Interpretation* (*de Interpretatione*), in which the reader will find a treasury of profound information concerning names from Proclus and Ammonius.</note>

<summary>This page argues that divine names are not arbitrary human conventions but are naturally linked to the divine essence, meaning that the specific languages of sacred nations are more effective for religious rites than translated ones.</summary>
<keywords>divine names, sacred languages, translation, convention vs nature, theurgy, Proclus, Ammonius, Aristotle, de Interpretatione</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that sacred names lose their inherent power when translated and explains why specific "barbarous" (non-Greek) languages, particularly Egyptian, are preferred in divine invocations.</meta>

in each nation that cannot be communicated by language to another nation. Furthermore, even if it were possible to translate them, they no longer preserve the same power once translated. <term>Barbarous names</term>, likewise, have much emphasis and great conciseness, and possess less ambiguity, variety, and complexity. Therefore, for all these reasons, they are suited to more excellent beings. Discard, therefore, entirely those suspicions of yours which deviate from the truth, namely: “*if he who is invoked is either an Egyptian or uses the Egyptian language.*” But rather consider that, as the Egyptians were the first of men* who

---

<note>* Most historians attribute the prize for antiquity to the Egyptians. And Lucian, in his book *On the Syrian Goddess*, says, “that the Egyptians are said to be the first among men to have a conception of the Gods and a knowledge of sacred matters. They were also the first to have a knowledge of sacred names.” Αιγυπτιοι πρωτοι ανθρωπων λεγονται θεων τε εννοιαν λαβειν και ιρα εισασθαι——πρωτοι δε και ονοματα ιρα εγνωσαν. In accordance with this, an oracle of Apollo, quoted by Eusebius, says that the Egyptians were the first who revealed, through infinite actions, the path that leads to the Gods. This oracle is as follows:

-> Αιπεινη γαρ οδος μακαρων, τρηχειατε πολλον, <-
-> Χαλκοδετοις τα πρωτα διοιγομενη πυλεωσιν. <-
-> Ατραπιτοι δε εασσιν αθεσφατοι εγγεγαυιαι, <-
-> Ας πρωτοι μεροπων επ’ απειρονα πρηξιν εφηναν, <-
-> Οι το καλον πινοντες υδωρ Νειλωτιδος αιης. <-</note>

<summary>The author argues that sacred names are more powerful in their original, concise foreign forms and should not be translated. He defends the use of the Egyptian language by citing the nation's historical priority in religious knowledge and divine names.</summary>

Again, however, if all these were the fraudulent devices of enchanters, how is it possible that things which are in the highest degree united to the Gods—which also join us with them, and have powers nearly equal to those of superior beings—could be imaginary devices, even though without them no sacred operation can be performed?
