ascribes to a *divine nature*." For we begin not from our own passions, but, on the contrary, from things allied to the Gods, and so we make use of words adapted to them. Instead, conformably to the nature which it possesses, and to the truth concerning it which was obtained by those who first established the laws of sacred religion, we persist in our conceptions of divinity. *For if anything else in religious legal institutions is suited to the Gods, it must certainly be immutability. # SECTION VIII.

## CHAP. I.

LEAVING, therefore, these details, you wish in the next place that I would explain to you "What the Egyptians consider the first cause to be; whether it is intellect, or above intellect; whether it exists alone, or with some other or others; whether it is incorporeal, or corporeal; and whether it is the same as the <term>Demiurgus</term>, or is prior to the <term>Demiurgus</term>? Likewise, whether all things are from one principle, or from many principles; whether they have knowledge of matter, or of primary corporeal qualities; and whether they admit matter to be unbegotten, or to be generated?" I, therefore, will first relate to you the reason why in the books of the ancient writers on sacred matters many and various opinions concerning these things are circulated, and also why among those who are still living, and are renowned for their wisdom, the opinion on this subject is not simple and uniform. I say then, that as there are

<summary>The author begins a detailed investigation into Egyptian theology, addressing questions about the nature of the first cause, the Demiurgus, and the origins of matter. He intends to explain why ancient texts and contemporary wise men hold such varied and differing opinions on these metaphysical subjects.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptians, first cause, intellect, Demiurgus, principle, matter, unbegotten, sacred matters, theology, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the diversity of Egyptian theological principles, noting that different ancient priests and authors, such as Seleucus and Manetho, attributed a vast number of books to Hermes regarding these principles.</meta>

many essences, and since these differ from each other, the diverse multitude of their principles—which belong to different orders—were handed down by various ancient priests. As *Seleucus*\* relates, therefore, *Hermes* described the principles that rank as wholes in two *myriads*† of books; or, as we are informed by *Manetho*‡, he fully explained these principles in three myriads, six thousand five hundred and twenty-five *volumes*. However, different ancient writers explained the *partial* principles of *essences* in different ways. It is necessary, however, through *investigation* to discover the truth about all these principles and to explain it to you as concisely as possible. And, first of all, hear about the *first subject* of your inquiry.

## CHAP. II.

Prior to truly existing beings and universal principles <note>or principles that rank as wholes</note>, there is one God, prior to <note>that deity who is generally believed to be</note> the first God and king*, immovable, and dwelling in the solitude of his own unity. For neither is the intelligible connected with him, nor anything else; instead, he is established as the paradigm of the God who is his own father, is self-begotten, is father alone, and is truly good. For he is something even greater and prior to this, being the fountain of all things and the root of the first intelligible forms. But from this one deity, the God who is self-sufficient unfolds himself into the light. For this divinity, also, is the principle and God of gods, a monad from *the one*, existing prior to essence and serving as the principle of essence. For from him entity and essence are derived; and for this reason, he is also called the principle of intelligibles. These, therefore, are the most ancient principles of all things, which Hermes

<summary>This chapter defines the supreme, solitary God who exists before all other beings and principles, serving as the source of the self-begotten God and all intelligible forms. ### CHAP. III.

According to another order, however, he arranges the god *Emeph*\* before and as the leader of the celestial gods. Besides these, other leaders also preside over the creation of visible natures. the Egyptian tongue Amon; but because he perfects all things with truth and craftsmanship, he is called Ptha. The Greeks, however, take Ptha to be Vulcan, focusing their attention solely on the god's creative craftsmanship. Insofar as he is also effective of good, he is called Osiris; and he has other names based on other powers and energies. Among the Egyptians, therefore, there is another dominion over all the elements in generation and the powers contained within them; four of these powers are male and four are female, which they attribute to the sun. And there is, likewise, another government over the whole of nature regarding generation, which they assign to the moon<note>Hence the moon is said by Proclus to be the self-visible statue or image of nature.</note>. And over all these they place one ruler, who transcends all the rest. ## CHAP. VI.

You say, therefore, “ *that according to many of the Egyptians, that which is within our power depends on the motion of the stars.* ” However, what the truth is regarding this, it is necessary to reveal to you from the <term>Hermaic</term> conceptions. For man, as these writings say, has two souls. One, indeed, is derived from the first intelligible and participates in the power of the <term>Demiurgus</term>; but the other is imparted from the circulation of the celestial bodies, to which the soul that sees God returns. These things, therefore, being thus, the soul that descends to us from the worlds follows the periods of the worlds; but that which is intelligibly present from the intelligible transcends the <term>genesiurgic</term> motion, and through this, a liberation from fate and the ascent to the intelligible Gods are achieved. Such <term>theurgy</term>, likewise, as leads to an unbegotten nature is perfected in accordance with a life of this kind.

<vocab>Hermaic, Demiurgus, genesiurgic, theurgy, Egyptians, fate, soul, intelligible</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VI, addressing the Egyptian belief that human agency is governed by the stars. It explains the Hermaic doctrine of the dual soul, where one part is tied to celestial cycles while the other provides a path to transcend fate through theurgy.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, dual soul, Hermaic doctrine, Demiurgus, fate, theurgy, celestial bodies, intelligible gods</keywords>

### CHAP. VII.

THEREFORE, that which you are doubtful about is not true: "*that all things are bound by the unbreakable bonds of Necessity,*" which we call Fate. For the soul has its own principle of turning toward the intelligible world, and of separation from generated natures, and also of contact with real being and that which is divine. "*Nor should we attribute fate to the Gods, whom we worship in temples and through statues as the dissolvers of fate.*" For the Gods indeed dissolve fate; but the lowest natures that proceed from them, which are entwined with the generation of the world and with the body, bring fate to fulfillment. Therefore, we very properly worship the Gods with all possible holiness and the observance of all religious rites, so that they may free us from the evils threatened by fate, as they alone rule over necessity through intellectual persuasion. But not all things are contained<note>For the word *echetai* in this place, I read *periechetai* (meaning "contained").</note> within the nature of fate; rather, there is another principle of the soul that is superior to all nature and generation, and through which we are capable of being united to the Gods and of transcending

<summary>This chapter argues that fate is not absolute because the soul possesses a divine principle that allows it to transcend the physical world and connect with the Gods. It explains that while lower emanations fulfill fate, the Gods themselves have the power to dissolve it through religious devotion.</summary>

<keywords>Fate, Necessity, Soul, Gods, intelligible world, generation, religious rites, transcendence</keywords>

life and the energy of the supercelestial Gods.
Through this principle, therefore, we are able
to liberate ourselves from fate. For when our

### CHAP. VIII.

WHAT then, is it not possible for a man to

<meta>This page continues Chapter VIII, investigating whether the celestial Gods, despite their role in the visible heavens, can also be the agents of the soul's liberation from the bonds of fate.</meta>

those that bind our lives with indissoluble bonds? Perhaps nothing prevents this from being the case. For if the Gods encompass within themselves many essences and powers, there are also in them other immense differences and oppositions. Moreover, this may also be said: that in each of the Gods, even those who are visible, there are certain intelligible principles through which a liberation of souls from mundane generation is achieved. But if someone admits only two kinds of Gods, *namely* the mundane and supermundane, the liberation of souls will be achieved through the supermundane Gods. These matters, therefore, are more accurately discussed in our treatise *Concerning the Gods*, in which it is shown who the anagogic Gods are, and according to what kind of powers they are so; how they liberate from fate, and through what sacred regressions; and what the order of mundane nature is, and how the most perfect intellectual energy rules over it. So, regarding what you add from Homer—"that the Gods are flexible"—it is not holy to assert this. For the works of the sacred ceremonies of religion have long since been defined by pure and intellectual laws. Subordinate natures are also liberated through a greater order and power; and when we abandon inferior natures, we are transferred into a more excellent allotment.

<summary>This text explains that even visible Gods contain intelligible principles that allow souls to be liberated from fate and the cycle of mundane generation. It rejects the idea that the Gods are "flexible" or easily swayed, asserting that religious ceremonies are governed by immutable, intellectual laws.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, mundane Gods, supermundane Gods, anagogic Gods, fate, Homer, sacred ceremonies, liberation, generation, intellectual energy, Concerning the Gods</keywords>

--- Page 340 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, clarifying that sacred operations and the soul's return to the divine do not alter the immutable nature of the Gods, but rather fulfill an original divine purpose.</meta>

ment. This, however, is not carried out contrary to any original sacred law, such that the Gods are changed by a sacred operation being performed afterward; but from the beginning, divinity sent souls here so that they might return to him again. Therefore, no change is produced through a reascent of this kind, nor do the descents and ascents of souls oppose one another. For as generation and this universe are suspended from an intellectual essence, so also, in the orderly distribution of souls, the liberation from generation accords with the care they employ concerning generation.

<vocab>souls, generation, intellectual essence, divinity, sacred operation</vocab>

<summary>This text explains that the soul's ascent back to the divine through sacred operations does not change the Gods, as their descent into the world was part of a divine plan for their eventual return. # SECTION IX.

## CHAP. I.

Let us now, therefore, to the best of our ability, endeavor to discuss the many-faceted doubt concerning the individual <term>dæmon</term>, which is also open to various objections. However, to speak briefly, the consideration of the individual <term>dæmon</term> is twofold: one is <term>theurgic</term>, while the other is artificial. One draws this <term>dæmon</term> down from <term>supernal causes</term>, while the other derives it from the visible cycles of <term>generation</term>; one makes no use at all of the calculation of <term>nativities</term>, while the other involves itself with methods of this kind. Furthermore, one worships this <term>dæmon</term> in a more universal and supernatural way, while the other does so partially, in accordance with nature. Since this is the case, you seem to me to have absurdly applied a more perfect <term>sacred operation</term> to one that is merely human, and to have conducted your inquiries based on that mistake.

<summary>The author distinguishes between two ways of considering the individual guardian spirit: a superior, theurgic method and a lesser, astrological method based on birth charts. He argues that it is a mistake to confuse these two approaches or to judge sacred, supernatural operations by human standards.</summary>

<keywords>dæmon, theurgic, artificial, supernal causes, nativities, sacred operation, generation, astrology</keywords>

### CHAP. II.

In the next place, you also appear to me to have restricted yourself to only a small part of the discussion concerning the <term>peculiar dæmon</term>. For since it is customary for those who technically work with nature to invoke this <term>dæmon</term> in an orderly manner—from the <term>decans</term>, from the <term>dispensators of influxes</term>, from the signs of the <term>zodiac</term>, the stars, the sun and moon, from the <term>greater and lesser bear</term>, from all the elements, and from the world itself—this being the case, you do not act rightly in assuming only one, and the smallest part of all these, namely the <term>lord of the geniture</term>, and making your inquiries about this alone. 

Here, likewise, once again from one of the matters proposed for consideration, you inquire “*how the <term>lord of the geniture</term> gives the <term>peculiar dæmon</term>; and according to what kind of efflux, or life, or power, it descends to us from him.*” You also speak concerning the <term>calculation of nativities</term>, and ask “*whether there is any reality in it or not;*” and likewise concerning the discovery of the <term>lord of the geniture</term>, “*whether it is impossible to be found, or possible.*” In what respect, however, do these things pertain to the authority of the <term>dæmon</term>? For it is evident that our knowledge

### CHAP. III.

### CHAP. IV.

If, however, it is necessary, setting these details aside, to speak what appears to me to be the truth, you do not correctly conclude “*that a knowledge of this mathematical science* <note>That is, the science of calculating nativities.</note> *cannot be obtained, because there is much disagreement concerning it, or because Chæremon, or someone else, has written against it.*” For if this reasoning were accepted, all things will be incomprehensible. For all sciences have countless debaters, and the doubts with which they are attended are innumerable. Therefore, as we are accustomed to say in opposition to those who are argumentative, that opposing views regarding things that are true are naturally discordant, and that it is not only falsehoods that are hostile to each other; so, too, we say regarding this mathematical science, that it is indeed true; but that those who stray from its scope, being ignorant of the truth, contradict it. ## CHAPTER V.

You say then, in your letter, “*that the discovery of the lord or lords of the geniture, if there are more than one in a nativity, can scarcely be achieved, and is confessed by astrologers themselves to be unattainable; and yet they say that the personal daemon is to be known from this source.*” But how can astrologers admit that the knowledge of the lord of the geniture cannot be obtained by them, when they provide clear methods for its discovery and teach us rules by which we may resolve doubts—some, indeed, giving us five\*, others more, and others fewer than five rules? Omitting this, however, let us direct our attention to a matter of greater consequence, namely the accidents per-

## CHAP. VI.

If, however, it is necessary to reveal to you the truth concerning the personal daemon, we must say that he is not assigned to us from one part of the heavens, or from some one of the visible elements; but that from the whole world, the diverse life contained in it, and the diverse body through which the soul descends into birth, a certain personal portion is assigned to each of the parts within us, according to a specific governance. This daemon, therefore, is established in the paradigm before the soul descends into birth; and when the soul has received him as its leader, the daemon immediately presides over the soul, brings its lives to fulfillment, and binds it to the body when it descends. He likewise governs the common animal nature of the soul, directs its personal life, and imparts to us the principles of all our thoughts and reasonings. We also perform such things as he suggests to our intellect, and he continues to govern us until, through priestly theurgy, we obtain a God for the overseeing guardian and leader of the soul. For then the daemon either yields or hands over his governance to a more excellent nature, or is

### CHAP. VII.

From these things, therefore, it is easy to answer your next question. For the personal <term>daemon</term> does not rule over just one of the parts within us, but, in short, over all the parts at once; he extends to every principle within us, in the same manner as he was assigned to us from the total orders in the universe. Regarding what you consider proper to add as an indication—" *that daemons preside over the parts of our body, so that one is the guardian of health, another of the form of the body, and another of the corporeal habits, and that there is one daemon who presides in common over all these;* "—you should consider this as an argument that there is one <term>daemon</term> who is the guardian and governor of everything that is in us. You must not, therefore, assign one <term>daemon</term> to the body, another to the soul, and another to the intellect: for it is absurd that the living being should be one, but the <term>daemon</term> that presides over it should be many-formed. For everywhere the natures that

<summary>This chapter argues that a single personal daemon governs the entire human being—including body, soul, and intellect—rather than having different daemons assigned to individual physical or mental functions.</summary>

But you also create conflict among them. For you speak as if “*some of them were good, but others bad.*” Evil <term>daemons</term>, however, nowhere have a ruling role, nor are they divided in opposition to those that are good with equal authority and power.

### CHAP. VIII.

AFTERWARDS, leaving these details behind, you move on to the philosophical opinion. But you undermine the whole hypothesis concerning the unique <term>daemon</term>. For if (as you say) “*this <term>daemon</term> is a part of the soul,*” such as, for instance, the intellectual part, “*and he is happy who possesses a wise <term>intellect</term>,*” then there will no longer be any other more excellent or daemonic order presiding over or transcending the human soul. Instead, certain parts of the soul, or a specific divided power, will have dominion over many of the forms of life within us; and these will rule over them, not as if they were innate, but as naturally exempt and as transcending our entire composition.

<summary>This chapter argues against the existence of conflicting good and evil daemons within an individual and critiques the philosophical theory that the daemon is merely an internal faculty of the soul like the intellect.</summary>

## CHAP. IX.

AFTER this, you also mention another inquiry concerning the <term>personal daemon</term>, which describes “*some as worshiping two, and others three, daemons of this kind.*” All of this, however, is mistaken. For it is a false way of proceeding to divide the causes that watch over us instead of relating them to one; for this strays from the unity that has authority over all things. Likewise, the opinion that divides this <term>daemon</term> between the body and the governing of the body reduces his power to a very small part. So what need is there for those who hold this opinion to focus on sacred rituals, if their primary principle is useless? Therefore, each of us has one <term>personal presiding daemon</term>; however, it is not right to think that this <term>daemon</term> is the same for everyone, or that he is a single entity that is simply present to each individual in a personal way. For divisions based on species and the differences in matter do not accommodate the shared nature and sameness of things that are essentially incorporeal. “*Why then [you ask] is the personal daemon invoked in a common way by all men?*” Because the invocation of him is carried out through one

<summary>The author argues that each individual has a single, unique presiding daemon rather than multiple or divided ones, emphasizing that incorporeal entities cannot be divided like material things. He critiques the idea that this daemon is shared universally or restricted only to governing the body.</summary>

For in the <term>theurgic</term> order, secondary natures are always invoked through primary ones. Therefore, among daemons, one common leader of the <term>cosmocrators</term> concerned with <term>generation</term> sends to each of us our own specific daemon. Thus, when the specific daemon is present with each of us, he then reveals the worship that is appropriate to be paid to him and his name, and likewise provides the proper way of invoking him.

# SECTION X.

---

## CHAP. I.

It now remains, finally, for us to speak about felicity, about which you make various inquiries—first by proposing objections, then by expressing doubt, and then by questioning. Citing, therefore, everything you have said, we shall answer it appropriately. You ask, then, “ *whether there is not some other hidden path to felicity.* ” But how, in a path that moves away from the Gods, is it likely that there can be an ascent to felicity? For if the essence and perfection of all good are contained within the Gods, and their primary and ancient power resides with us priests, and if the beginning and end of all good is earnestly pursued by those who similarly cling to more excellent natures and truly achieve a union with them—if this is the case, then it is here that the contemplation of truth and the possession

<summary>The author begins the final section on felicity, arguing that true happiness is found only through the Gods and the priestly path. He rejects the possibility of a hidden path to felicity that deviates from divine union and the contemplation of truth.</summary>

<keywords>felicity, Gods, priests, divine union, contemplation of truth, spiritual ascent, essence of good</keywords>

--- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition into the final discussion on felicity from Section X, Chapter I, and begins Chapter II, addressing doubts regarding human opinion and the nature of the soul's fictions.</meta>

of intellectual science are to be found*. And a knowledge of the Gods is accompanied by a turning toward, and the knowledge of, ourselves.

### CHAP. II.

Hence you doubt in vain, “ *that it is not proper to look to human opinions.* ” For what leisure can he have, whose intellect is directed toward the Gods, to look downward toward the praises of men? Nor do you rightly doubt what follows, namely, “ *that the soul devises great things from chance circumstances.* ” For what principle of fictions can there be in truly existing beings? Is it not the phantastic power within us which is the maker of images? But the phantasy is never active when the intellectual life operates perfectly. And is not truth essentially coexistent with the Gods? Is it not, likewise, harmoniously established among the intelligibles? It is in vain, therefore, that things of this kind are spread by you and others. Nor do those

<summary>The author argues that knowledge of the divine leads to self-knowledge and renders human praise irrelevant. He further asserts that the soul does not create fictions about divine reality, as the imagination is inactive when the intellect is fully engaged with truth.</summary>

And if certain things of this kind sprout in the sciences of divine matters, just as evil arts blossom forth in other arts, these are undoubtedly more contrary to such sciences than to anything else. For evil is more hostile to good than to that which is not good.

---

### CHAP. III.

I WISH, in the next place, to reply to such assertions as slander divine prediction. For you compare with it "*certain other methods which are concerned with the prediction of future events.*" To me, however, it does not appear to be anything honorable if a certain natural aptitude is generated in us for the indication of the future, just as in animals there is a foreknowledge of earthquakes, or winds, or storms. For an innate presage of this kind is the consequence of acuteness of sensation, or sympathy, or some other joint motion of the physical powers, and is not accompanied by anything venerable and supernatural. Nor if someone, by human reasoning,

<summary>The author argues that criticisms against theology and theurgy do not reflect their true nature and that corruptions of these sciences are most offensive to the sciences themselves. He begins a defense of divine prediction, distinguishing it from the natural, instinctual foreknowledge found in animals.</summary>
<keywords>theology, theurgy, divine prediction, natural aptitude, presage, sympathy, sensation, foreknowledge</keywords>

--- Page 358 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter III, distinguishing between natural, human conjecture and true divine divination. It concludes the critique of natural presentiment and transitions into Chapter IV, which defines the nature of divine divination.</meta>

or by artificial observation, conjectures from signs those things which the signs indicate (just as physicians foreknow that a fever will occur from the systole and torpor of the pulse), he does not appear to me to possess anything honorable and good. For he conjectures in a human manner and concludes by our reasoning power about things which are acknowledged to be produced naturally, and he forms a judgment not very distant from the physical order of things. Therefore, if there is in us a certain natural presentiment of the future, in the same way as in all other animals, this power is clearly seen to operate; however, this presentiment does not in reality possess anything that is truly blessed. For what is there among the things implanted in us by nature in the realms of generation that is a genuine, perfect, and eternal good?

### CHAP. IV.

Divine divination, therefore, which is joined with the Gods, alone truly imparts a divine life to us; since it participates in divine foreknowledge and divine intellections, and renders us in reality divine. It also causes us to be genuine participants of *the good*, be-

<meta>catchword: be-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that human conjecture based on natural signs is not truly honorable or blessed, as it belongs to the physical realm. He introduces divine divination as the only source of true divine life and participation in the good.</summary>
<keywords>divination, foreknowledge, human reasoning, systole, torpor, realms of generation, divine life, intellections</keywords>

--- Page 359 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine divination, completing the sentence from the previous page regarding how divination makes us participants of the good.</meta>

...cause the most blessed intellectual perception of the Gods is filled with all good. Hence, those who possess this divination "*do not*," as you conjecture, "*foresee future events and are nevertheless unhappy.*" For all divine foreknowledge is <term>boniform</term>. Nor "*do they foresee, indeed, what is future, but do not know how to use this knowledge properly.*" For, together with the foreknowledge, they receive the beautiful itself, and true and appropriate order; and utility is also present with it. For the Gods, in conjunction with it, deliver a transcendent power of defense against the inconveniences which arise from nature. And when it is necessary to exercise virtue, and the ignorance of future events contributes to this, then the Gods conceal what will be for the sake of making the soul better. But when the ignorance of what is future does not at all contribute to this, and foreknowledge is advantageous to souls, for the sake of their salvation and reascent <note>to divinity</note>, then the Gods insert the foreknowledge which pertains to divination in the <term>penetralia</term> of the essences of souls.

<summary>The author explains that divine foreknowledge is inherently good and purposeful, providing both defense against natural hardships and the wisdom to use such knowledge properly. He notes that the Gods reveal or hide the future based on what will best serve the soul's virtue and its eventual return to the divine.</summary>
<keywords>divination, foreknowledge, the Gods, virtue, the soul, salvation, boniform, penetralia</keywords>

--- Page 360 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from discussing the mechanics of divination to the ultimate goal of these practices: achieving union with the divine.</meta>

## CHAP. V.

BUT why am I so wordy about these details? For I have abundantly shown, in what has been previously said, the superiority of divine divination over human divination. It is better, therefore, in compliance with your request, "*to point out to you the way to happiness, and show you in what its essence consists.*" For from this the truth will be discovered, and at the same time all doubts may be easily resolved. I say, therefore, that the more divine <note>For *theotos* here, I read *theoteros*.</note> <term>intelligible man</term>, who was formerly united to the Gods by the vision of them, afterwards entered into another soul, which is adapted to the human form, and through this became bound by the chains of necessity and fate. Therefore, it is necessary to consider how he may be freed from these bonds. *There is, therefore, no other way to break them than the knowledge of the Gods.* For to know *the good* scientifically is the essence of happiness; just as the forgetting of good, and deception about evil, happen to be the essence of evil. The former, therefore, is present with divinity; but the latter, which is an inferior destiny, is inseparable from mortal nature.

<summary>The author explains that the soul, once united with the Gods, has become bound by fate and necessity through its human form, and can only be liberated through the scientific knowledge of the Gods. This knowledge of the good constitutes true happiness, whereas the forgetting of the good leads to evil.</summary>
<keywords>divination, happiness, intelligible man, fate, necessity, knowledge of the Gods, the good, divinity, mortal nature</keywords>

--- Page 361 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the paths to spiritual liberation, contrasting the divine path of the soul with the downward path toward the physical body.</meta>

And the former, indeed, measures the essences of <term>intelligibles</term>\* by sacred paths; but the latter, abandoning principles, gives itself up to the measurement of the idea of the body. The former is a knowledge of the father; but the latter is a departure from him, and a forgetting of the God who is a <term>superessential father</term>, and self-sufficient. The former, likewise, preserves the true life of the soul and leads it back to its father; but the latter draws down the <term>generation-ruling</term>† man as far as to that which is never permanent, but is always flowing. You must understand, therefore, that this is the first path to <term>felicity</term>, affording to souls an <term>intellectual plenitude</term> of <term>divine union</term>. But the <term>sacerdotal</term> and <term>theurgic</term> gift of <term>felicity</term> is called, indeed, the gate to the <term>Demiurgus of wholes</term>, or the seat, or palace, of the *good*. In the first place, likewise, it possesses a power of purifying the soul, much more perfect than the power which purifies the body; afterwards it causes a <term>coaptation</term> of the reasoning power to the participation and vision of the

---

<note>† That is, Man, considered as a <term>rational soul</term> connected with the <term>irrational life</term>; for this man has dominion in the realms of generation.</note>

<summary>The text contrasts the divine path toward the superessential Father with the material path that leads the soul away from God and into the changing world of the body. good, and a liberation from everything of a contrary nature; and, finally, it produces a union with the Gods, who are the givers of every good.

## CHAPTER VI.

**MOREOVER**, after it has joined the soul to the various parts of the universe, and to the total divine powers which pass through it; then it leads the soul to, and places it in, the whole <term>Demiurgus</term>, and causes it to be independent of all matter, and to be united with the <term>eternal reason</term> alone. But my meaning is, that it uniquely connects the soul with the self-begotten and self-moved God, and with the all-sustaining, intellectual, and all-adorning powers of the God, and likewise with that power of his which elevates to truth, and with his self-perfect, effective, and other <term>demiurgic powers</term>; so that the <term>theurgic soul</term> becomes perfectly established in the <term>energies</term> and <term>demiurgic intellections</term> of these powers. Then, also, it inserts the soul into the whole demiurgic God. And this is the goal, for the <term>Egyptians</term>, of the <term>sacerdotal elevation</term> of the soul to divinity.

<vocab>Gods, Demiurgus, eternal reason, theurgic soul, demiurgic intellections, Egyptians, sacerdotal elevation</vocab>

<summary>This passage describes how theurgy leads the soul to a state of independence from matter by uniting it with the Demiurgus and his divine, intellectual powers. For the Egyptians, this union represents the ultimate goal of the priestly elevation of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, Demiurgus, Egyptian religion, soul's ascent, divine union, Neoplatonism, sacerdotalism, eternal reason</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Egyptian theurgy, specifically addressing the nature of "the good" and rebutting claims that theurgists focus on trivial matters or are deceived by malevolent spirits.</meta>

## CHAPTER VII.

Regarding *the good*, they likewise conceive that one kind is divine, and this is the God who exists prior to the intelligible; but the other kind is human, and consists of a union with the former. Bitys has revealed these two kinds of good from the Hermaic books. This part, therefore, is not omitted by the Egyptians as you suspect, but is divinely delivered by them. Nor do “*theurgists disturb the divine intellect about trifling concerns;*” rather, they consult it about things which pertain to the purification, liberation, and salvation of the soul. Neither do they diligently occupy themselves with things which are indeed difficult yet useless to mankind; but, on the contrary, they direct their attention to things which are, above all others, most beneficial to the soul. Nor, finally, are “*they deceived by a certain fraudulent demon,*” for those who have conquered a deceptive and demoniacal nature ascend to an intelligible and divine essence.

<summary>This passage clarifies the Egyptian distinction between divine and human good and defends theurgists against accusations of focusing on trivialities or being misled by demons. It asserts that theurgy is dedicated to the soul's salvation and its ascent to the divine essence.</summary>
<keywords>the good, Bitys, Hermaic books, Egyptians, theurgists, purification, salvation of the soul, divine essence, demon, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 364 ---
<meta>This page concludes the response to the inquiries concerning Egyptian theurgy and divination, following the previous page's defense of theurgic practices and the definition of the divine good.</meta>

### CHAP. VIII.

AND thus we have answered, to the utmost of our ability, your inquiries concerning divination and theurgy. It remains, therefore, at the end of this discussion, that I should pray to the Gods to grant me an unchanging guard of true conceptions, to instill truth in me eternally, and to provide me abundantly with a share in more perfect conceptions of the Gods, in which the most blessed end of our good is situated, and the confirmation of our harmonious friendship with each other.

## ADDITIONAL NOTES.

---

**PAGE 9.** *Anebo.* Porphyry, in his *Life of Plotinus* and also in the second book of his *Treatise on Abstinence from Animal Food*, informs us that he was familiar with a certain Egyptian priest, who, as Gale conjectures, is probably the priest to whom Porphyry now writes. The style of language, indeed, as Gale observes, indicates that the person to whom this Epistle is addressed was a very great prophet, who, nevertheless, is later said to be a priest. This, however, is not anything new or inconsistent. For in the eleventh book of the *Metamorphoses* by Apuleius, the Egyptian Zaclas is said to be *propheta primarius et sacerdos*, a chief prophet and priest.

**Page 9.** *Hermes the God who presides over language.* The Egyptians honored two versions of Hermes; Iamblichus refers to the first one here. This deity is the source of invention, and thus he is said to be the son of *Maia*; because searching, which is implied by *Maia*, leads invention into the light. He also bestows <term>mathesis</term> on souls by unfolding the will of his father Jupiter; and this he accomplishes as the messenger of Jupiter. Proclus, in a manuscript commentary on the *Alcibiades*, observes, “that this deity is the watchful guardian of gymnastic exercises; and thus *hermae*, or carved statues of Mercury, were placed in the *palestrae*; over *music*, and thus he is honored as the *lyrist* λυραιος among the celestial constellations; and over *disciplines*, because the invention of geometry, reasoning, and discourse is attributed to this God. He presides, therefore, over every kind of learning, leading us toward an intellectual essence from this mortal abode, governing the various groups of souls, and dispersing the sleep and forgetfulness with which they are burdened. He is likewise the provider of recollection, the goal of which is a genuine intellectual understanding of divine natures.”

P. 15. *There is, therefore, the* **good** *itself which is beyond* **essence**, *and there is that* **good** *which exists according to* **essence**. *There are three orders of* **good**; *namely, that which is <term>imparticipable</term> and <term>superessential</term>; that which is <term>imparticipable</term> and <term>essential</term>; and that which is <term>essential</term> and <term>participable</term>. Of these, the last is the kind that our nature contains; the* **good** *which ranks among forms is essential; and that which is beyond* **essence** *is superessential. Or we may say that the* **good** *which exists in us may be considered as a habit, because it exists within a subject; the one next to this is ranked as* **essence**, *and a part of* **essence**—*I mean the* **good** *which is ranked among forms; and the* **good** *which is beyond* **essence** *is neither a habit nor a part. Regarding the* **good**, *also, which exists according to* **essence**, *it must be observed that since forms are of two kinds—some only distinguishing the* **essences** *of things shaped by form, but others defining their perfections—the category of* **essence**, *the "same" and the "different," and the form of animal, horse, and man, and everything of this kind, provide distinction to* **essence** *and subjects. However, the form of the* **good**, *the beautiful, and the just—and in like manner the form of virtue, of health, strength, and everything of a similar nature—are perfective of the beings to which they belong: and for some,* **essence** *is the leader, but for others, the* **good**. *For, as* Plato *says, everything except the* **one** *must necessarily participate in* **essence**; *and whatever preserves, perfects, or defends any being must be* **good**. *Hence, since these two are leaders—one of forms that give existence to things, and the other of those that are the sources of their perfection—it is necessary that one of these should be subordinate to the other. I mean that the* **good** *which is assigned a place among forms that are the sources of perfection should be subordinate to* **essence**, *which ranks among causes from which existence originates, if the* **good** *is being, and a certain kind of being. For it is either the same as, or different from,* **essence**, *which the Elean guest or stranger in the* **Sophista** *of* Plato *shows to be the category of being. And if the* **good** *is the same as*

But if genera are more ancient than forms, the *good* which ranks among forms, and is posterior to their genus, will not be the superessential good which reigns over intelligibles; instead, this must be asserted of that good under which this and every form is arranged, which possesses being, and which is the leader of the other genera of being.

P. 15. *But the other medium, which is suspended from the Gods, though it is far inferior to them, is that of daemons.* In addition to what is said in this work by Iamblichus concerning daemons, the following information about them from Olympiodorus, in his manuscript Scholia on the *Phaedo* of Plato, is well worthy of the attention of the philosophical reader:

> “Since there are things in the universe that exist differently at different times, and since there are also natures which are joined with the <term>superessential</term> unities, it is necessary that there should be a certain middle genus, which is neither immediately suspended from deity, nor exists differently at different times according to better and worse, but which is always perfect and does not depart from its proper virtue; and is immutable indeed, but is not joined with the <term>superessential</term> <note>which is the characteristic of deity</note>. The whole of this genus is daemonical. There are also different genera of daemons, for they are placed under the mundane Gods. The highest of these exists according to the ‘one’ of the Gods, and is called a unific and divine genus of daemons. The next exists according to the intellect which is suspended from deity, and is called intellectual. The third exists according to soul, and is called rational. The fourth exists according to nature, and is called physical. The fifth exists according to body, which is called corporeal-formed. And the sixth exists according to matter, and this is called material.” Olympiodorus adds, “or in another way it may be said that some of these are celestial, others ethereal, others aerial, others aquatic, others terrestrial, and others subterranean. Regarding this division also, it is evident that it is derived from the parts of the universe. But irrational daemons originate from the aerial governors; for this reason, the Chaldean Oracle says,
> 
> ->Ηεριων ελατηρα κυνων χθονιων τε και υγρων.<-

### *P. 22. One and the best solution will be obtained by surveying the mode of divine allotment.*

The manner in which divine allotments exist is admirably explained by Proclus in *Tim.* p. 43, as follows:

> “Since, according to a division of the universe into two parts, we have distributed allotments into the celestial and sublunary, there can be no doubt what the former are, and whether they possess an invariable sameness of existence. But the sublunary allotments are deservedly a subject of wonder, whether they are said to be perpetual or not. For since all things in the realm of generation are continually changing and flowing, how can the allotments of their providential rulers be said to be perpetual? For things in generation are not perpetual. But if their allotments are not perpetual, how is it possible to suppose that divine government can exist differently at different times? For an allotment is neither a specific separate energy of the Gods—such that, as sublunary natures change, we might say the allotment is exempt and remains immutable—nor is it only that which is governed, so that no absurdity would follow from admitting that an allotment is in a flowing condition and is occupied with various mutations; rather, it is a providential inspection and unrestrained government of divinity over sublunary concerns. Such being the doubts with which this subject is attended, the following appears to be a solution of the difficulty.
> 
> “We must say, then, that it is not proper to consider all the natures that are in the state of generation, and generation itself, as consisting only of things mutable and flowing; rather, there is also something immutable in these, which is naturally adapted to remain perpetually the same. For the space which receives and contains within itself all the parts of the world, and which is distributed through all bodies, is immovable, lest, by being moved, it should require another place, and thus proceed from one receptacle to another, *ad infinitum*. The ethereal vehicles, also, of divine souls, with which they are circularly clothed, and which imitate the lives in the heavens, have a perpetual

<summary>This page explains that guardian daemons are categorized under gods overseeing the movement of souls and introduces a commentary by Proclus on the permanence of divine government. Proclus argues that while the material world is in constant flux, the divine "allotments" or governing structures remain perpetual and immutable.</summary>

<keywords>guardian daemons, divine allotments, Proclus, Timaeus, sublunary realm, celestial realm, providence, ethereal vehicles, Neoplatonism, Plato</keywords>

--- Page 369 ---
<meta>This page continues the modernization of Proclus's commentary on the Timaeus, specifically his explanation of how divine allotments remain perpetual despite the changing nature of the sublunary world.</meta>

essence, and are eternally suspended from these divine souls themselves, being full of prolific powers and performing a circular motion according to a certain secondary revolution of the celestial orbs. And thirdly, the wholeness (ολοτης) of the elements has a permanent existence, even though their parts are corrupted in various ways. For it is necessary that every form in the universe should be unfailing, so that the universe may be perfect and, being generated from an immovable cause, may be immovable in its essence. *But every wholeness is a form, or rather, it is what it is said to be through the participation of one all-perfect form.*

> "And here we may see the orderly progression of the nature of bodies. For the space of the universe is immovable according to every kind of motion. But the vehicles of divine souls alone undergo a change according to place; for such a motion is most distant from essential change. And the wholeness of the elements allows for the other motions of bodies within its parts, but the whole remains perfectly immutable. The celestial allotments, which directly divide the space of the universe, also co-distribute the heavens themselves. But those in the sublunary region are primarily allotted the parts which are in the space of the universe, but afterwards they make a distribution according to the specific vehicles of souls. And thirdly, they remain perpetually the same, according to the complete parts of generation. The allotments of the Gods, therefore, do not change, nor do they exist differently at different times; for their existence is not directly rooted in that which can be changed.
>
> "How, then, do the illuminations of the Gods reach these things? How are the dissolutions of sacred rites brought about? And how is the same place at different times under the influence of different spirits? Might it not be said that since the Gods have perpetual allotments and divide the earth according to divine numbers—similar to the sections of the heavens—the parts of the earth are also illuminated to the extent that they participate in this aptitude? But the circulation of the heavenly bodies, through the patterns they possess, produces this aptitude; at the same time, divine illumination imparts a power more excellent than the nature present within these parts of the earth. This aptitude is also brought about by nature herself as a whole, inserting divine impressions into each of the illuminated parts, through which they spontaneously participate in the Gods. For as these

<summary>Proclus explains that while individual parts of the sublunary world change, the "wholeness" of the elements and divine allotments remain immutable and perpetual. He argues that divine illumination reaches the earth through the "aptitude" of its parts, which is influenced by celestial patterns and the inherent structure of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, divine allotments, sublunary region, celestial orbs, wholeness (ολοτης), divine souls, immutability, sacred rites, divine illumination, aptitude</keywords>

--- Page 370 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine allotments, explaining how the manifestation of divinity in the sublunary world depends on the "aptitude" of the recipient. It then transitions into Iamblichus's questions regarding the temporal descriptions of these allotments in Plato's Timaeus.</meta>

parts depend on the Gods, nature inserts in those that are different, different images of the divinities. Times also cooperate in producing this aptitude, according to which other things, also, are governed; the proper temperature of the air likewise; and, in short, everything by which we are surrounded contributes to the increase and decrease of this aptitude. When, therefore, in accordance with a concurrence of these many causes, an aptitude for the participation of the Gods is generated in one of the natures which are disposed to change, then a certain divinity is revealed, which, before this, was concealed through the inaptitude of the recipients. This divinity possesses his appropriate allotment eternally and always extends the participation of himself, similar to light from the sun, but is not always participated in by sublunary natures due to their inaptitude for such participation. For just as with respect to partial souls such as ours—which at different times embrace different lives—some choose lives suited to their appropriate Gods, while others choose foreign lives through forgetfulness of the divinities to whom they belong; so too, with respect to sacred places, some are adapted to the power which receives its allotment there, while others are dependent on a different order. And on this account, as the Athenian guest in Plato says, some places are more fortunate, but others are more unfortunate.

“ The divine Iamblichus, however, questions how the Gods are said to be allotted certain places according to definite times, as, by Plato in the *Timaeus*, Minerva is said to have been first allotted the guardianship of Athens, and afterwards of Sais. For if their allotment began at a certain time, it will also cease at a certain time. For everything which is measured by time is of this kind. And furthermore, was the place which they are allotted at a certain time without a presiding deity before this allotment, or was it under the government of other Gods? For if it was without a presiding deity, how can it be admitted that a certain part of the universe was once entirely destitute of divinity? How can any place remain without the guardianship of superior beings? And if any place is sufficient for its own preservation, how does it afterwards become the allotment of one of the Gods? But if it should be said that it is afterwards under the government of another God, whose allotment it becomes, this also is absurd. For the second God does not tear away the government and allot-

<summary>This page explains that divine presence in the physical world is limited by the changing aptitude of recipients, rather than any change in the Gods themselves. <meta>This page continues the explanation of how divine allotments remain constant while their participants change. It then transitions into a discussion of theurgy as the "art of divine works," citing various historical authorities and the provenance of a specific dissertation by Proclus.</meta>

ment of the former, nor do the Gods alternately occupy each other's places, nor do demons change their allotments. Since there are such doubts on this subject, he solves them by saying that the allotments of the Gods remain perpetually unchanged, but that those participating in them at one time enjoy the beneficent influence of the presiding powers, while at another they are deprived of it. He adds that *these are the mutations measured by time, which sacred institutes frequently call the birthday of the Gods.*

P. 23. *Which also the art of divine works perceiving, etc.* This art of divine works is called <term>theurgy</term>, into which Pythagoras was initiated among the Syrians, as we are informed by Iamblichus in his *Life* of that philosopher. (See p. 9 of my translation of that work.) Proclus was also skilled in this art, as may be seen in the *Life* of him by Marinus. Psellus, in his manuscript treatise on Demons, says, as we have observed before, “that magic formed the last part of the sacerdotal science;” in which context, by magic he doubtless means that kind of it which is called <term>theurgy</term>. And that <term>theurgy</term> was employed by the ancients in their mysteries, I have fully proved in my treatise on the *Eleusinian and Bacchic Mysteries*. This <term>theurgy</term>, too, is doubtless the same as the magic of Zoroaster, which Plato in the *First Alcibiades* says consisted in the worship of the Gods; on this passage, the following account of <term>theurgy</term> by Proclus was, I have no doubt, originally part of a commentary. For the manuscript commentary of Proclus, which survives on this dialogue, does not extend to more than a third part of it; and this *Dissertation on Theurgy*, which survives only in Latin, was published by the translator Ficinus immediately after his *Excerpta* from this commentary. So it seems highly probable that the manuscript from which Ficinus translated his *Excerpta* was much more complete than the one preserved for us, because it contained this account of the <term>theurgy</term> of the ancients.

“In the same way that lovers gradually advance from the beauty apparent in physical forms to that which is divine, so the ancient priests—when they considered that there is a certain alliance and sympathy between natural things, and between manifest things and hidden powers, and discovered that all things exist within all things—they

<meta>This page continues the discussion of theurgy and the concept of "sympathy" between the celestial and earthly realms, providing specific examples of how lower natures mirror higher ones.</meta>

...constructed a sacred science from this mutual sympathy and likeness. Thus they recognized supreme things in those that are lower, and lower things in the supreme: in the celestial regions, earthly properties existing in a causal and heavenly way; and on earth, heavenly properties, but according to an earthly condition. For how else shall we explain those plants called heliotropes—that is, attendants of the sun—moving in correspondence with the revolution of its orb, or selenitropes—attendants of the moon—turning in exact conformity to her motion? It is because all things pray and sing hymns to the leaders of their respective orders; but some do so intellectually, and others rationally; some in a natural way, and others through the senses. Hence the sunflower, as far as it is able, moves in a circular dance toward the sun; so that if anyone could hear the vibration caused by its movement through the air, he would perceive something composed of a sound of this kind in honor of its king, such as a plant is capable of creating. Hence, too, we may see the sun and moon on the earth, but according to an earthly quality; while in the celestial regions, all plants, stones, and animals possess an intellectual life according to a heavenly nature. Now the ancients, having contemplated this mutual sympathy of things, applied both heavenly and earthly natures for hidden purposes, by means of which—through a certain likeness—they drew down divine powers into this lower realm. For, indeed, likeness itself is a sufficient cause for binding things together in union and harmony. Thus, if a piece of paper is heated and then placed near a lamp, even though it does not touch the fire, the paper will be suddenly ignited, and the flame will move down from the higher to the lower parts. This heated paper we may compare to a certain relationship of lower things to higher ones; and its proximity to the lamp to the appropriate use of things according to time, place, and matter. But the procession of fire into the paper aptly represents the presence of divine light to that nature which is capable of receiving it. Lastly, the ignition of the paper may be compared to the divinization of mortals and to the illumination of material natures, which are afterwards carried upward, like the burning paper, from a certain participation in a divine seed.

" Again, the lotus, before the sun rises, folds its leaves into itself, but gradually expands them as it rises: unfolding them in proportion to the sun's ascent to the zenith; but as gradually contracting them as that luminary

<summary>This passage describes the theurgic principle of "sympathy," explaining how earthly objects like sunflowers and lotuses mirror celestial bodies. This natural correspondence was used by the ancients to draw divine influences into the material world, a process compared to the way heated paper catches fire from a nearby lamp.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how lower earthly forms—including plants, stones, and animals—correspond to and participate in higher celestial and divine natures through a system of "sympathy."</meta>

descends to the west. Therefore, this plant, by the expansion and contraction of its leaves, appears to honor the sun no less than men do by the movement of their eyelids and the motion of their lips. But this imitation and specific participation in heavenly light is visible not only in plants, which possess nothing more than a trace of life, but also in certain stones. For example, the sun-stone, with its golden rays, imitates those of the sun; however, the stone called the "eye of heaven," or the "eye of the sun," has a shape similar to the pupil of an eye, and a ray shines from the center of that pupil. Similarly, the lunar stone—which is shaped like the moon when it is a crescent—follows the moon's motion through its own internal changes. Finally, the stone called <term>helioselenus</term> (meaning "of the sun and moon") imitates, in its own way, the meeting of those celestial bodies, which it represents through its color. Thus, all things are full of divine natures; earthly natures receive the fullness of celestial ones, while celestial natures receive the fullness of super-celestial essences* ; meanwhile, every order of things proceeds gradually, in a beautiful descent, from the highest to the lowest. For whatever details are united as one above the order of things are later expanded as they descend, with various souls being distributed under their various ruling divinities.

In the next place, there are many solar animals, such as lions and roosters, which participate in a certain solar divinity according to their nature. Because of this, it is remarkable how much inferior beings yield to superior ones within the same order, even if they do not yield in size or physical power. Thus it is said that a rooster is greatly feared and, in a sense, revered by a lion. We cannot explain the reason for this through physical matter or the senses, but only by contemplating the heavenly order. For in this way, we find that the presence of solar virtue is more in harmony with a rooster than with a lion. This is evident when we consider that the rooster, as if with hymns, celebrates and calls to the rising sun when it begins its journey toward us from the antipodes; and that solar angels sometimes appear in forms of this kind, who, although they are formless, yet present themselves to us (who are bound by physical form) in a way that our senses can perceive. At times, there are also demons with the face of a lion who suddenly disappear when a rooster is placed before them, unless they belong to a solar order. This occurs because those natures which hold a lower rank within the same order

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how earthly beings, such as animals and plants, participate in a solar divinity and how this natural sympathy was used in ancient religious practices.</meta>

...always reverence their superiors; just as many, upon seeing the images of divine men, are accustomed, from the mere sight, to be afraid of committing anything dishonorable.

“In short, some things revolve corresponding to the movements of the sun, such as the plants we have mentioned, and others in a way imitate the solar rays, like the palm and the date; some imitate the fiery nature of the sun, like the laurel; and others imitate a different property. For, indeed, we may perceive that the properties gathered in the sun are distributed everywhere to subsequent beings established in a solar order—that is, to angels, demons, souls, animals, plants, and stones. Therefore, the founders of the ancient priesthood discovered the worship of superior powers from visible things, while they mixed some things and purified others. They indeed mixed many things together because they saw that some simple substances possessed a divine property (though not when taken individually) sufficient to summon that particular power in which they participated. Hence, by mixing many things together, they drew down upon us a heavenly influx; and by composing one thing from many, they produced a likeness to that one which is above the many; and they created statues from a mixture of various substances working together in sympathy and harmony. Besides this, through a divine art, they gathered blended scents into one, encompassing a multitude of powers and symbolizing the unity of a divine essence; they considered that division weakens each of these, but that mixing them together restores them to the form of their original model.

“But sometimes a single herb or stone is sufficient for a divine operation. Thus, a thistle is enough to bring about the sudden appearance of some superior power; but a laurel, *raccinum* (or a thorny kind of sprig), the land and sea onion, coral, diamond, and jasper act as a safeguard. The heart of a mole is useful for divination, but sulfur and seawater are used for purification. Therefore the ancient priests, through the mutual relationship and sympathy of things for each other, gathered their virtues into one but expelled them through repulsion and antipathy; purifying with sulfur and bitumen when necessary, and sprinkling with seawater. For sulfur purifies because of the sharpness of its smell, but seawater because of its fiery part. Besides this, in the worship of the gods, they offered animals and other substances suited to their nature; and they received, in the first place, the powers of

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's relationship with the body, comparing it to a reflection in a moving stream, and transitions into a philosophical exploration of the unity of the Gods as described by Proclus.</meta>

...self in the floating stream, he indeed will preserve his face unchanged; but the stream, being moved in various ways, will change the image, so that at different times it will appear different to him, slanted and upright, and perhaps scattered and continuous. Let us suppose too, that such a person, through being unaccustomed to the sight, should think that it was he himself who suffered this distortion as a result of observing his shadow in the water; and thus thinking, he should be afflicted and disturbed, astonished and hindered. In the same manner, the soul, observing the image of herself in the body—carried along in the river of generation and variously disposed at different times through inward passions and external impulses—is indeed herself unaffected, but thinks that she suffers; and being ignorant of this, and mistaking her image for herself, she is disturbed, astonished, and perplexed.”

P. 35. *Since, however, the order of all the Gods is profoundly united.— For the very existence in them, whatever it may be, is the "one" of their nature.*

The Gods are self-perfect superessential unities, so far as they are Gods. For the primary existence of every thing is according to the summit of its essence, and this in the Gods is "the one," through which they are profoundly united to each other and to "the one" itself, or the ineffable principle of things, from which they are ineffably revealed into light. Concerning this union of them with each other, Proclus admirably observes as follows, in his manuscript Commentary on the *Parmenides* of Plato.

> “All these unities are in, and are profoundly united to, each other, and their union is far greater than the communion and sameness which exist in beings. For in the latter there is indeed a mutual mixture of forms, similarity, and friendship, and a participation in each other; but the union of the Gods, as being a union of unities, is much more uniform, ineffable, and transcendent: for here all are in all, which does not take place in forms or ideas\*; and their unmingled purity, and the characteristic of each—in a manner far surpassing the diversity in ideas—preserves their natures unconfused and distinguishes their peculiar powers. Hence, some of them are more universal, and others more particular; some of them are characterized by permanency, others by progression, and others by conversion or regression. Some, again, are generative, others anagogic, or of an elevating nature,”

\* For in these, all are in each, but not all in all.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the nature of the Gods as superessential unities, drawing on the philosophy of Proclus to explain their union and their distinct characteristics.</meta>

and others demiurgic; and universally, there are different characteristics of different Gods, namely the connective, perfective, demiurgic, assimilative, and others that are celebrated after these; so that all are in all, and yet each is at the same time separate and distinct.

> "Indeed, we obtain this knowledge of their union and characteristics from the natures through which they are participated. For, regarding the visible Gods, we say that there is one soul of the sun and another of the earth, directing our attention to the visible bodies of these divinities, which possess much variety in their essence, powers, and dignity among wholes. As, therefore, we grasp the difference of incorporeal essences from sensory inspection, in the same way, from the variety of incorporeal essences, we are enabled to know something of the unmingled distinction of the first and superessential unities, and of the characteristics of each. For each unity has a multitude suspended from its nature, which is either intelligible alone; or intelligible and at the same time intellectual; or intellectual alone; and this last is either participated, or not participated; and this again is either supermundane or mundane. And this is how far the progression of the unities extends."

Shortly after, he adds, "As trees are rooted in the earth by their extremities and through this are earthly in every part, in the same manner divine natures are rooted by their summits in *the one*, and each is a *unity* and *one* through its unconfused union with *the one* itself." See more on this most important of all subjects in the notes to my translation of the *Parmenides*.

But that which is full is sufficient only to itself and is not yet prepared to communicate. plenary, or exuberantly full. Hence, if a divine nature fills all things from itself with the good which it contains in itself, it is exuberantly full. And if this is the case, first establishing in itself the characteristic which it imparts to others, it will extend to them the communications of super-plenary goodness.

P. 59. *It is also necessary to know what enthusiasm is, and how it is produced.*

The following account of enthusiasm, and of the different kinds of mania mentioned by Plato in the *Phaedrus*, from the Scholia of Hermeas on that dialogue, is extracted from the additional notes to my translation of Proclus on the *Timaeus*. It is provided here for the benefit of the English reader of Plato who may not have that translation in his possession, as a valuable addition to what Iamblichus says here on this subject.

> “Since Plato here describes four kinds of mania—by which I mean enthusiasm, and possession or inspiration from the Gods—namely the musical, the telestic, the prophetic, and the amatory, before the discussion of each, we must first speak about enthusiasm. We must show to what part of the soul the enthusiastic energy pertains; whether each part of it possesses this energy; if all enthusiasm is from the Gods; and in what part of the soul it is generated; or whether it exists in something else more excellent than the soul. Where, then, does that which is properly and primarily called enthusiasm exist, and what is it? Of the rational soul there are two parts: one of which is <term>dianoia</term> <note>discursive reasoning</note>, but the other is *opinion.* Again, however, of <term>dianoia</term>, one part is said to be the lowest, and is properly <term>dianoia</term>, but another part of it is the highest, which is said to be its intellect, according to which the soul especially becomes intellectual, and which some call intellect in capacity. There is also another thing above this, which is the summit of the whole soul, and most allied to *the one*. This likewise wishes well to all things, and always gives itself up to the Gods, and is readily disposed to do whatever they please. This, too, is said to be *the one* of the soul; it bears the image of the super-essential one, and unites the whole soul. But that these things necessarily exist in this way, we may learn as follows: The rational soul derives its existence from all the causes prior to itself, that is, from intellect and the Gods. But it also exists from itself, for it perfects itself. So far, therefore, as it exists from the Gods, it possesses *the one*, which unites all its powers, and

<meta>This page continues the discussion of enthusiasm and the soul's relationship to the divine, specifically examining the different levels of the soul—from the "one" of the soul to its rational and irrational parts—and how divine inspiration manifests in each.</meta>

all its own multitude, and joins them to *the one* itself, and is the first recipient of the goods bestowed by the Gods. It likewise makes the entire essence of the soul good-like (boniform), by which it is connected with the Gods and united to them. But so far as it exists from intellect, it possesses an intellectual nature, by which it perceives forms through simple intuitions rather than through discursive reasoning; and it is joined to the intellect that is above itself. And so far as it constitutes itself, it possesses the dianoetic power, by which it generates sciences and certain theorems, acts through discursive reasoning, and gathers conclusions from propositions. For the fact that it constitutes or gives existence to itself is evident from its imparting perfection to itself; since that which leads itself to perfection and imparts well-being to itself will much more impart existence to itself. For *well-being* is a greater thing than *being*. If, therefore, the soul imparts that which is greater to itself, it will much more impart that which is less. Hence, that which is primarily, properly, and truly enthusiasm from the Gods is achieved according to this *one* of the soul, which is above <term>dianoia</term> <note>discursive reasoning</note> and above the intellect of the soul; this *one* is at other times in a relaxed and dormant state. When this *one* becomes illuminated [by the Gods], the entire life of the soul is illuminated, as are the intellect, <term>dianoia</term>, and the irrational part, and the likeness of enthusiasm is transmitted even to the body itself.

> “Other forms of enthusiasm, therefore, are produced in other parts of the soul*, stimulated by certain daemons†, or even by the Gods, though not without the intervention of daemons. For <term>dianoia</term> is said to act enthusiastically when it discovers sciences and theorems in a very short space of time and to a greater degree than other men. Similarly, opinion and the imagination (phantasy) are said to act this way when they discover arts and accomplish admirable works, such as those Phidias achieved in the creation of statues, or others in different arts; as Homer also says‡ of the one who made the belt of Hercules, ‘that he neither did nor would artificially produce such another.’ Anger, likewise, is said

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how different parts of the soul participate in divine enthusiasm, moving from the rational faculties to the soul's highest essence and its relationship with the Gods.</meta>

to act enthusiastically, when in battle it acts supernaturally.

->Like Mars, when brandishing his spear, he raged *.<-

But if someone, yielding to desire, should eat what reason forbids and through this should unexpectedly become well, you might say that desire also, in this instance, acted enthusiastically, though obscurely; so that enthusiasm is likewise produced in the other parts of the soul. Enthusiasm, however, properly so called, occurs when the *one* of the soul, which is above the intellect, is excited toward the Gods and is inspired by them. But at different times, it is possessed by different Gods according to its own aptitudes; and it is more or less possessed when the intellect or <term>dianoia</term> <note>discursive reasoning</note> is what is moved. Therefore, just as when we ask what philosophy is, we do not always define it accurately, but frequently—through an improper use of the word—call mathematics or physics "philosophy" and "science," we do the same with respect to enthusiasm. For even if it is the <term>phantasy</term> <note>imagination</note> that is excited, we are accustomed to call that excitation enthusiasm. Moreover, those who attribute enthusiasm to the temperatures of bodies, or the excellent temperament of the air, or the rising of vapors, or the suitability of times and places, or the agency of the bodies that revolve in the heavens, speak more of the cooperating and material causes of the phenomenon than of its causes properly so called. You have, therefore, the Gods as the producing cause of enthusiasm; the enthusiastically acting soul itself, or external symbols, as the material cause; the inspiration of the Gods regarding the *one* of the soul as the formal cause; and the good as the final cause.
   “If, however, the Gods always wish what is good for the soul, why does the soul not always act enthusiastically? We might say that the Gods indeed always wish the soul what is good, but they also intend for the order of the universe to prevail, and the soul, because of many causes, is not always adapted to enthusiasm—which is why it does not always act enthusiastically. But some say that the <term>telestic art</term> <note>the art of sacred rituals</note> extends only as far as the <term>sublunary</term> <note>the region below the moon; the earthly realm</note> region. Therefore, if they mean that none of the superlunary and celestial natures act in the sublunary region, they are clearly asserting something absurd.

<summary>The author defines true enthusiasm as the divine inspiration of the soul's highest part by the Gods, distinguishing it from physical or material influences. He also explains that while the Gods always desire the soul's good, the soul acts enthusiastically only when it is properly adapted to receive divine influence within the cosmic order.</summary>
