--- Page 381 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine enthusiasm, specifically addressing the scope of a soul's activity and how inanimate objects like statues can participate in divine energy.</meta>

But if they mean that the <term>Telestæ</term> <note>mystic operators or priests</note>, or mystic operators, are not able to act above the lunar sphere, we say that if all the allotments of souls are sublunary, their assertion would be true. But if there are also allotments of souls above the moon, as there are (for some are the attendants of the sun, others of the moon, and others of Saturn, since the Demiurge scattered some of them into the earth, others into the moon, and others elsewhere), this being the case, it will be possible for the soul to act above the moon. For what the whole order of things grants to the soul for a very extended period of time, the soul is also able to grant to itself for a short space of time when assisted by the Gods through the <term>telestic art</term> <note>theurgy or ritual magic</note>. For the soul can never act above its own allotment, but it can act to the full extent of it. Thus, for instance, if the allotment of the soul reached as far as philosophy, the soul would be able—even if it should not choose a philosophic life but some other—to act in that life somewhat philosophically. There are also said to be certain supermundane souls. And thus we have shown how the soul acts enthusiastically.

But how are statues said to have an enthusiastic energy? May we not say that a statue, being inanimate, does not itself act toward divinity, but the telestic art, purifying the matter of which the statue consists and placing around it certain characters and symbols, in the first place renders it, through these means, animated, and causes it to receive a certain life from the world? And, in the next place, after this, it prepares the statue to be illuminated by a divine nature, through which it always delivers oracles as long as it is properly adapted. For the statue, when it has been rendered perfect by the telestic art, remains afterwards [gifted with a prophetic power] until it becomes entirely unsuited for divine illumination; but he who receives the inspiring influence of the Gods receives it only at certain times, and not always. The cause of this is that the soul, when filled with deity, acts in relation to it. Hence, in consequence of acting above its own power, it becomes weary. For it would be a God, and similar to the souls of the stars, if it did not become weary. But the statue, according to its participation, remains illuminated. Hence its unsuitability entirely leads to a state of privation, unless it is again, *anew*, perfected and animated by the mystic operator. We have sufficiently shown, therefore, that enthusiasm, properly so called, is effected within the *one* of the soul, and that it is an illumination of divinity.

->A A<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine inspiration and the soul's activity, moving from how the telestic art affects inanimate objects to the specific types of divine madness that guide the soul's restoration.</meta>

“Next, let us discuss the order and the use of the four <term>manias</term> <note>divine madnesses or inspirations</note>, and show why the philosopher mentions these alone. Is it because no others exist, or because these were sufficient for his purpose? Plato himself indicates as he proceeds that there are many other divine inspirations and <term>manias</term>; even before this, he mentions the inspiration from the Nymphs. There are also inspirations from Pan, from the Mother of the Gods, and from the Corybantes, which are mentioned elsewhere by Plato.

Here, however, he presents only these four <term>manias</term>. First, because these alone are sufficient for the soul to achieve its proper <term>apocatastasis</term> <note>restoration to its original state</note>, as we will show later; and second, because he is describing the immediate steps of the soul's ascent. For the gifts of the Gods to all beings are numerous and incomprehensible. But now he presents to us the divine energies that extend to souls. 

He presents these four <term>manias</term> not as if any one of them—especially the amatory—would be insufficient to lead the soul back to its original happiness, but because he is currently unfolding their sequence, regular progression, and the orderly perfection of the soul. For instance, while it is possible for a tyrannical life to be suddenly transformed into an aristocratic one through intense effort and divine favor, the gradual ascent moves from a tyrannical to a democratic life, then to an oligarchic one, followed by a timocratic life, and finally to an aristocratic life—with the descent and fall happening in the opposite order. Similarly, as the soul is about to ascend and be restored to its former happiness, it is first possessed by the musical <term>mania</term>, then by the telestic, then the prophetic, and finally the amatory <term>mania</term>. 

These inspirations, however, work together and rely on one another; their communion is that profound. For the telestic requires the prophetic* <term>mania</term> <note>*The original text says 'musical' here, but it should be read as 'prophetic.'</note>, since the latter† <note>†This is a correction of the Greek term for prophecy to its nominative form.</note> interprets many things pertaining to the former. And again, the prophetic requires the telestic <term>mania</term>. For the telestic <term>mania</term> perfects and establishes oracular predictions. Furthermore, the prophetic uses the poetic and musical <term>mania</term>. For prophets, one might say, always speak in verse. And again, as Plato says, the musical uses the prophetic <term>mania</term> spontaneously. But what need is there to speak about the amatory and musical

manias? For nearly the same people practice both of these, as, for instance, Sappho, Anacreon, and the like, because these cannot exist without each other. But it is very evident that the amatory mania contributes to all of these, since it is subservient to every kind of divine inspiration; for no divine inspiration can be achieved without amatory inspiration. And you may see how Orpheus appears to have applied himself to all of these, as they are necessary to and connected with each other. For we learn that he was most <term>telestic</term>, and most prophetic, and was inspired by Apollo; and besides this, that he was most poetic, on which account he is said to have been the son of Calliope. He was likewise most amatory, as he himself acknowledges to Musaeus, extending divine gifts to him and making him perfect. Hence he appears to have been possessed by all the manias, and this as a necessary consequence. For there is an abundant union, concord, and alliance with each other among the Gods who preside over these manias—namely, the Muses, Bacchus, Apollo, and Love.

“It remains, therefore, that we should reveal the nature of each of the manias, first observing that those which are internal—originating from the soul itself and bringing it to perfection—are of one kind; but their external energies, which preserve the outward man and our physical nature, are of another. The four external manias, however, are analogous to the four internal ones. Let us consider, therefore, in the first place, the internal manias, which alone originate from the soul itself, and let us see what they accomplish in the soul. In order that this may become clear, as well as their arrangement, let us survey from above the descent and the shedding of the soul's wings, as Plato describes. From the beginning, and at first, the soul was united to the Gods, and its own unity joined to their "One." But afterward, the soul, departing from this divine union, descended into intellect; it no longer possessed real beings in a unified way, but apprehended and surveyed them through simple projections and, as it were, the contacts of its intellect. Next, departing from intellect and descending into reasoning and <term>dianoia</term>, it no longer apprehended real beings through simple intuitions, but syllogistically and transitively, proceeding from one thing to another—from propositions to conclusions. Afterward, abandoning true reasoning and its analytical power, it descended into the world of generation and became filled with much irrationality and confusion. It is necessary, therefore, that the soul should return

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the four divine manias and their specific roles in the soul's restoration and its external expression in human life.</meta>

<page-num>356</page-num>

to its proper principles and return again to the place from which it came. To this ascent and <term>apocatastasis</term>, however, these four manias contribute. Indeed, the musical mania leads the agitated and disturbed nature of the parts of the soul—which were hurried away toward indefiniteness and unfitness and were filled with great tumult—toward symphony and harmony. But the <term>telestic mania</term> causes the soul to be perfect and entire, and prepares it to act intellectually. For the musical mania alone harmonizes and restrains the parts of the soul; but the telestic causes the whole of it to act and prepares it to become entire, so that its intellectual part may act. For the soul, by descending into the realms of generation, resembles something broken and weakened. And the circle of *the same*, or its intellectual part, is fettered; but the circle of *the different*, or its <term>doxastic</term> part, sustains many fractures and turnings. Hence, the soul acts partially, and not according to its whole self. Therefore, after the parts of the soul are harmonized together, the <term>Dionysiacal inspiration</term> renders it perfect and causes it to act according to its whole self and to live intellectually. But the <term>Apolloniacal mania</term> converts and arouses together all the multiplied powers and the whole of the soul toward *the one* within it. Hence Apollo is named as the one who elevates the soul from multitude to *the one*. And the remaining mania, the <term>amatory</term>, receiving the soul in its united state, joins this *one* of the soul to the Gods and to <term>intelligible beauty</term>. Since, therefore, the givers of these manias are transcendently united and exist within each other, the gifts also for this reason participate in and communicate with one another; and the recipient, which is the soul, possesses a fitness for all the gifts. This, then, is the order, and these are the energies and powers within the soul itself, of these four manias.

“But let us also consider their external energies on man, and what they outwardly effect within us. The musical mania, therefore, causes us to speak in verse, to act and be moved rhythmically, and to sing in meter of the splendid deeds of divine men and their virtues and pursuits; and, through these, it disciplines our life, just as the inward manias harmonize our soul. But the <term>telestic mania</term>, expelling everything foreign, contaminating, and harmful, preserves our life perfect and harmless; by banishing insane and diabolical fantasies, it causes us to be sane, entire, and perfect, just as the internal telestic mania makes the soul perfect and entire. Again, the pro-

--- Page 385 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four divine manias, completing the word "prophetic" from the previous page and describing the specific functions of prophetic, amatory, and poetic mania in elevating the soul.</meta>

Prophetic mania compresses the extension and infinity of time into one, and sees, as if in a single present moment, all things: the past, the future, and the existing time. Hence, it predicts what will be, which it sees as present to itself. It causes us, therefore, to pass through life in a blameless manner; just as the internal prophetic mania compresses and elevates all the multiple and various powers and lives of the soul to *the one*, so that it may be preserved and connected to a greater degree. But the <term>amatory mania</term> turns young persons toward us and causes them to become our friends, being instructive to youth and leading them from <term>sensible beauty</term> to our <term>psychical beauty</term>, and from there sending them to <term>intelligible beauty</term>; in the same way that the internal amatory mania unites *the one* of the soul to the Gods.

“All the above-mentioned manias, therefore, are superior to the prudent and temperate activities of the soul. Nevertheless, there is a mania which exists alongside temperance, and which we say has, in a certain respect, a priority above\* it. For certain inspirations are produced according to the middle and also the <term>doxastic</term> reasons of the soul, according to which artists achieve certain things and discover theorems beyond expectation—as Asclepius did in medicine, for instance, and Hercules in the <term>practical</term>† life.”

Afterward, in commenting on what Plato says of the mania from the Muses—namely, “that it adorns the infinite deeds of the ancients”—Hermeas observes, “that the inward activity of the <term>poetic mania</term> in the soul, by applying itself to superior and intelligible natures, imparts harmony and order to subordinate natures; but that external, divinely-inspired poetry celebrates the deeds of the ancients and instructs both its contemporaries and posterity, extending its influence everywhere.” But Plato says, “that he who, without the divinely-inspired mania of the Muses, expects to become a divine poet will, by thus imagining, become imperfect himself, and his poetry will be surpassed and obscured by the poetry which is the offspring of mania.” Hermeas adds, “For what similarity is there between the poetry of Chaerilus and Callimachus, and that of Homer and Pindar? For the divinely-inspired poets, being filled

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the poetic mania, specifically Hermeas's commentary on how the Muses inspire the soul and the distinction between truly inspired poetry and the more human-centric poetry of the Romans.</meta>

"...from the Muses, always invoke them, and extend to them all that they say.” For a fuller and most admirable account of the poetic mania, and of the different species of poetry by Proclus, see the notes on the tenth book of the *Republic*, in my translation of Plato, and also the Introduction to my translation of the *Rhetoric*, *Poetic*, and *Nicomachean Ethics* of Aristotle.

From what is said here by Hermeas about enthusiasm, the intelligent reader will easily see that none of the Roman poets whose works have been preserved for us possessed that which is primarily, properly, and truly enthusiasm—or that highest species of it in which the *one* of the soul is illuminated by a divine nature, and through transcendent likeness is united to it. As for Virgil, indeed, the foremost of these poets, though he invokes the Muse at the beginning of the *Æneid*, his invocation of her is only a partial and secondary thing. For he only calls on her to reveal to him the causes that involved a man of such remarkable piety as Æneas in so many misfortunes:

->Musa, mihi causa memora, etc.<-

And, trusting in his own genius, he begins his poem without requesting divine inspiration,

->Arma, virumque cano, etc.<-

To which it may be added that this placing of himself before the Muse resembles the *ego et meus rex* of Wolsey. On the contrary, divinely-inspired poets, as Hermeas well observes, knock, as it were, at the gates of the Muses, and thus being filled from there exclaim,

->Εσπετε νυν μοι Μουσαι—<-

And,

->Μηνιν αειδε θεα—<-

And,

->Ανδρα μοι εννεπε Μουσα.<-

For being always directed toward them, they arrange the whole of what they afterward say as being derived from their inspiring influence. With an arrogance, too, peculiar to the Romans, who, as a certain Greek poet <note>See Olympiodorus in Aristotle’s *Meteorology*.</note> says, were a people

->Beyond measure proud.<-

He associates himself, in his fourth Eclogue, with the Muses as their equal:

->Sicelides Musæ, paulo majora canamus.<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the arrogance of Roman poets compared to the divine inspiration of the Greeks, specifically examining how the Roman mindset lacked the true enthusiasm found in earlier poets.</meta>

This reminds me of what Suetonius relates about Caligula: that he would place himself between the statues of Castor and Pollux and confer privately with Jupiter Capitolinus, imagining that he was intimate with and of equal dignity to these divinities. As for the poets who have lived since the fall of the Roman Empire, it would be ridiculous to suppose that they possessed this highest enthusiasm, since they did not believe in the existence of the sources from which it is uniquely and genuinely derived.

**P. 67. *The attentive power of the soul.*** This is that part or power of the rational soul which primarily perceives the operations of the senses. For the rational soul not only possesses potential intellect, the <term>dianoetic</term> power, will, and choice, but another power, which is called by the best of the Greek interpreters of Aristotle, as well as by Iamblichus, <term>tò prosektikòn</term>, *the attentive*. This power investigates and perceives whatever occurs within a person and says, "I understand," "I think," "I am of the opinion," "I am angry," or "I desire." In short, this attentive part of the rational soul passes through all the rational, irrational, vegetable, or physical powers. If, therefore, it is necessary for it to pass through all these powers, it will also proceed through the senses and say, "I see," or "I hear"; for it is the characteristic of that which perceives energies to speak in this way. Thus, if it is the attentive power that says these things, it is this power that perceives the energy of sensible objects; for it is necessary that the nature which perceives all things should be one, since man is also one. For if one part of the soul should perceive some things and another part perceive others, it would be just as Aristotle says: as if *you* should perceive this thing and *I* should perceive that. It is necessary, therefore, that the attentive power should be one indivisible thing.

**P. 74. *For the human soul is on all sides darkened by the body, which he who calls the river of Negligence, or the water of Oblivion, etc.*** —will not by such names sufficiently express its depravity. “The whole of generation, as well as the human body,” says Proclus in his commentary on the *Timaeus*, book 5, page 339, “may be called a river, because of its rapid, impetuous, and unstable flow. Thus also in the *Republic*, Plato calls the entire <term>genesiurgic</term> nature the river of <term>Lethe</term>; in which are contained, as Empedocles says, Oblivion and the meadow of <term>Ate</term>; the voracity of matter, and the light-hating world, as the Gods say; and the winding streams

P. 105. *But there are a certain few who, by employing a certain supernatural power of intellect, are removed from nature, etc.* The class to which these few belong is beautifully described as follows by Plotinus, in the beginning of his *Treatise on Intellect, Ideas, and Real Being*:

> “Since all men from their birth employ the senses before the intellect, and are necessarily first familiar with sensibles, some, proceeding no further, pass through life considering these as the beginning and end of things. They understand that whatever is painful among these is evil, and whatever is pleasant is good; thus thinking it sufficient to pursue the one and avoid the other. Those among them who claim a greater share of reason than others also esteem this to be wisdom, being affected in a manner similar to heavier birds who, collecting many things from the earth and being weighed down by the weight, are unable to fly high, even though they have received wings for this purpose from nature. But others are elevated to a small degree from subordinate things, as the more excellent part of the soul recalls them from pleasure to a more worthy pursuit. However, as they are unable to look upward and do not possess anything else which can afford them rest, they turn themselves—along with the name of virtue—to actions and the choice of inferior things from which they at first endeavored to raise themselves, though in vain. In the third class is the race of divine men who, through a more excellent power and with piercing eyes, acutely perceive celestial light. They raise themselves to the vision of this light, above the clouds and darkness, as it were, of this lower world, and staying there, they despise everything in these regions of sense. They are no otherwise delighted with the place which is truly and properly their own than he who, after many wanderings, is at length restored to his rightful country.”

See my translation of the entirety of this treatise.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the nature of reality, specifically discussing the descent of form into matter and the historical decline of traditional religious efficacy as noted by Iamblichus and the Emperor Julian.</meta>

into parts, and passing into bulk and separation, through a final progression into generation—namely, into matter; for matter is always truly the last sediment. Because of this, the Egyptians also call the dregs of the first life—which they symbolically name water—matter, being as it were a certain mire. And matter is, so to speak, the receptacle of generated and sensible natures, not existing as any definite form, but as the state or condition of existence; just as the indivisible, the immaterial, true being, and things of this kind, are the foundation of an intelligible nature. All forms, indeed, exist both in sensible and intelligible things, but in the former materially, and in the latter immaterially; that is, in the one indivisibly and truly, but in the other divisibly and like a shadow. Consequently, every form in sensible things is distributed according to material extension."

P. 120. *Through the innovation and illegality of the Greeks.* Iamblichus says that through this innovation and illegality, both names and prayers have currently lost their power. For during his time, and for some centuries prior to it, the genuine religion of the Greeks was rapidly declining because of their novelty and volatility, which he complains about here. Hence the Emperor Julian, in the fragments of his treatise against the Christians, preserved by Cyril, says, speaking of the Christians, 

> "If anyone wishes to consider the truth about you, he will find that your impiety consists of *Judaic audacity* and the *indolence and confusion of the heathens*. For deriving from both not that which is most beautiful, but the worst, you have woven a web of evils. — Hence, from the *innovation of the Hebrews*, you have seized blasphemy toward the venerable Gods; but from our religion, you have cast aside reverence for every nature more excellent than man, and the love of *ancestral traditions*." <meta>This page continues the commentary on Egyptian theology, specifically identifying the primary principles mentioned by Hermes with Platonic and Orphic hierarchies.</meta>

mentioned in this chapter, as celebrated by Hermes, the first corresponds to the *one itself* of Plato, and the second to *being itself*, or superessential being, the summit of the intelligible triad; these two principles are beautifully explained by Proclus in the second and third books of his treatise on the *Theology of Plato*.

P. 122. *He arranges the God Eneph prior to, and as the leader of, the celestial Gods.*—But prior to this he arranges the indivisible one, which he says is the *first paradigm*, and which he names *Eicton*. It appears to me that the former of these two divinities is the same as Saturn, who is the summit of the intellectual order of Gods; and that the latter is the *animal itself* of Plato, or the Phanes of Orpheus, who exists at the extremity of the intelligible triad. For the God Eneph is said by Iamblichus to be an intellect intellectually perceiving itself, and directing its thoughts to itself; and these are the characteristics of Saturn. And the God Eicton is said to be the first paradigm, and this is also asserted of Phanes.

P. 123. *For the books which are circulated under the name of Hermes, contain Hermaic opinions, though they frequently employ the language of the philosophers: for they were translated from the Egyptian tongue by men who were not unskilled in philosophy.* Only a few of these books still survive, but what Iamblichus says here sufficiently proves their authenticity, and that they contain the genuine doctrines of Hermes. They have doubtless, however, been occasionally interpolated by some of the early Christians, though not to the extent that modern critics—and that mitred sophist <note>The author refers to William Warburton, the Bishop of Gloucester.</note> Warburton—suppose.

P. 123. *And such as have written concerning the decans.* The twelve parts mentioned in the preceding chapter, into which the Egyptians divide the heavens, are the twelve signs of the zodiac. But the thirty-six parts are the twelve houses of the planets, divided into three other portions, which they call decans. Ptolemy, however, in his *Quadripartite*, refutes this doctrine of the Egyptians. Concerning these decans, see Scaliger on Manilius, Kircher in the second part of *Oedipus*, and Salmasius in *On Climacteric Years*. Gale also provides the following extract from Hermes relative to the decans, which had not been published before, and which he derived from a manuscript copy of Stobæus in the possession of Vossius.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Hermetic books and the role of the decans in the celestial hierarchy, transitioning into a commentary on divine flexibility as mentioned by Iamblichus and Homer.</meta>

Φαμὲν ὦ τέκνον, περιεκτικὸν τῶν ἁπάντων εἶναι τὸ σῶμα. ἐννόησον οὖν αὐτὸ ὥσπερ κυκλοειδὲς σχῆμα —— ὑπὸ δὲ τὸν κύκλον τοῦ σώματος τούτου τετάχθαι τοὺς λϛ δεκάνους, μέσους τοῦ παντὸς κύκλου τοῦ ζωδιακοῦ. —— νοήσωμεν ὡσπερεὶ φύλακας αὐτοὺς προϊστάσθαι τῶν ἐν κόσμῳ ἁπάντων, πάντα συνέχοντας —— καὶ τηροῦντας τὴν τῶν πάντων εὐταξίαν. —— ἔτι δὲ νόησον ὦ Τὰτ, ὅτι ἀπαθεῖς εἰσιν ὧν οἱ ἄλλοι ἀστέρες πάσχουσιν. οὔτε γὰρ ἐπεχόμενοι τὸν δρόμον στηρίζουσιν, οὔτε κωλυόμενοι ἀναποδίζουσιν, ἀλλ᾿ οὐδὲ μὴν ἀπὸ τοῦ φωτὸς τοῦ ἡλίου σκέπονται, ἅπερ πάσχουσιν οἱ ἄλλοι ἀστέρες. ἐλεύθεροι δὲ ὄντες ὑπεράνω πάντων, ὥσπερ φύλακες καὶ ἐπίσκοποι ἀκριβεῖς τοῦ παντὸς, περιέχονται τῷ νυχθημέρῳ τὸ πᾶν. —— ἔχουσι πρὸς ἡμᾶς τὴν μεγίστην δύναμιν. i. e. “We say, O son, that the body [of the universe] encompasses all things. Think, therefore, of it as being of a circular form. —— But under the circle of this body, the thirty-six decans are arranged as the mediators of the entire circle of the zodiac. —— These, likewise, must be understood to preside as guardians over everything in the world, connecting and containing all things —— and preserving the established order of all things. —— Furthermore, understand, O Tat, that these decans are unaffected by the things that the other stars experience. For they are not detained so as to stop their course, nor are they hindered so as to move backward; nor are they, like the other stars, hidden as if by a veil by the light of the sun. But being free above all things, they encompass the universe as its guardians and precise inspectors throughout the <term>Nycthemeron</term> [the space of a night and a day]. —— They also possess, in relation to us, the greatest power.”

P. 125. *So that what you add from Homer, “that the Gods are flexible,” it is not holy to assert.* The words of Homer are στρεπτοὶ δὲ τε καὶ θεοὶ αὐτοὶ, and are to be found in *Iliad* IX, line 493. But when Iamblichus says it is not holy to assert the Gods are flexible, he means that it is not holy according to the literal meaning of the words; divine flexibility indicates nothing more than this: that those who, through depravity, were previously unfit to receive the illuminations of the Gods, and as a consequence were subject to the power of avenging demons; when they later obtain pardon for their guilt through prayers and sacrifices, and through methods of this kind apply a remedy to their vices, they again become partakers of the goodness of the Gods. So that divine flexibility is a resumption of the participation in divine light and goodness by those who, through their own unsuitability, were previously deprived of it.

<summary>This page describes the thirty-six decans as the celestial guardians of the zodiac who maintain cosmic order and explains that the "flexibility" of the Gods refers to humanity's ability to return to divine favor through virtue and ritual.</summary>
<keywords>decans, zodiac, Tat, Nycthemeron, Hermes, Homer, Iliad, Iamblichus, divine flexibility, demons, atonement, cosmic order</keywords>

--- Page 392 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the nature of divine governance and the soul's liberation, moving from a discussion of celestial guardians to the role of dæmons in the human body and the soul’s eventual return to its intellectual source.</meta>

P. 130. *Dæmons preside over the parts of our body.* Proclus, in the fragments of his *Ten Doubts concerning Providence* preserved by Fabricius in the eighth volume of his *Bibliotheca Græca*, observes, “That the Gods, with an exempt transcendency, extend their providence to all things, but that dæmons, dividing their superessential subsistence, receive the guardianship of different herds of animals, distributing the providence of the Gods, as Plato says, as far as the most ultimate division. Hence some of them preside over men, others over lions or other animals, and others over plants; and still more specifically, some are the inspectoral guardians of the eye, others of the heart, and others of the liver.” He adds, “all things, however, are full of Gods, some of whom exert their providential energies immediately, but others through dæmons as intermediaries: not because the Gods are incapable of being present to all things, but because ultimate things are themselves unable to participate in primary natures.” Hence it must be said that there is one principal dæmon, who is the guardian and governor of everything that is in us, and many dæmons subordinate to him, who preside over our parts.

P. 134. *Hence it is necessary to consider how he may be liberated from these bonds.* “The one salvation of the soul herself,” says Proclus in *Tim.* book v. p. 330, “which is extended by the <term>Demiurgus</term>, and which liberates her from the circle of generation, from abundant wanderings, and an ineffective life, is her return to the intellectual form, and a flight from everything which naturally adheres to us from generation. For it is necessary that the soul, which is hurled like seed into the realms of generation, should lay aside the stubble and bark, as it were, which she obtained from being scattered into these fluctuating realms; and that purifying herself from everything surrounding her, she should become an intellectual flower and fruit, delighting in an intellectual life, instead of <term>doxastic</term> <note>related to opinion rather than certain knowledge</note> nutriment, and pursuing the uniform and simple energy of the period of sameness, instead of the abundantly wandering motion of the period which is characterized by difference. For she contains each of these circles, and twofold powers. And of her horses one is good, but the other the contrary <note>as is said in the Phædrus</note>. And one of these leads her to generation, but the other from generation to true being. The one also leads her round the <term>genesiurgic</term> <note>related to the process of physical birth or generation</note> circle, but the other round the intellectual circle. For the period of the same and the similar elevates to intellect, and an intelligible nature, and to the

<summary>This text discusses the role of dæmons as specific guardians of the human body and explains how the soul can achieve salvation by detaching from the material world and returning to an intellectual state.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, dæmons, providence, soul's salvation, Demiurgus, generation, intellect, Timaeus, Phædrus, doxastic, genesiurgic</keywords>

--- Page 393 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's ascent, concluding the explanation of how the soul returns to its divine and intellectual state through purification and the shedding of material "vehicles."</meta>

...first and most excellent habit. But this habit is the state by which the soul, being winged, governs the whole world, becoming like the Gods themselves. And this is the universal form of life in the soul, just as the other is the partial form, which occurs when she falls into the final body and becomes something belonging to an individual instead of belonging to the universe. The middle of these is also the partial-universal state, when she lives in conjunction with her middle vehicle as a citizen of <term>generation</term>. 

Therefore, by dismissing her first habit—which exists according to an alliance with the whole of generation—and laying aside the irrational nature that connects her with generation, as well as governing her irrational part by reason and extending opinion to intellect, she will be led back in a circle to a happy life from her wanderings through the regions of sense. Those who are initiated by Orpheus in the mysteries of Bacchus and Proserpine pray that they may obtain this life, together with the allotments of the <note>celestial</note> sphere and a cessation of evil. 

But if our soul necessarily lives well when living according to the <term>circle of sameness</term>, this must be even more true for divine souls. However, it is possible for our soul to live according to the circle of sameness when it is purified, as Plato says. Therefore, <term>Cathartic virtue</term> alone must be called the salvation of souls; since it cuts off and forcefully obliterates material natures and the passions that cling to us from generation; it separates the soul and leads it back to intellect, and causes it to leave behind on earth the vehicles with which it is clothed. For in descending, souls receive different vehicles—aerial, aquatic, and terrestrial—from the elements, and thus finally enter into this physical mass. For how could they proceed into this body from immaterial spirits without an intermediary?

### THE END.

---

->C. Whittingham, College House, Chiswick.<-

<summary>The text concludes by describing the soul's return to a divine, universal state through "Cathartic virtue," which purifies the soul and allows it to shed the elemental vehicles acquired during its descent into the physical body. It references Orphic mysteries and Platonic philosophy to illustrate the soul's journey from individual generation back to intellectual sameness.</summary>
<keywords>soul, purification, Cathartic virtue, generation, intellect, Orpheus, Bacchus, Proserpine, celestial sphere, circle of sameness, Plato, vehicles, subtle body, Thomas Taylor, Neoplatonism</keywords>
